PREFACE 

EVERYBODY knows there are already too many books. 

One believing this, as I do, should not give us another one on too slight provocation. Even then he owes us an explanation. This is mine: I cannot remember when I have not been deeply interested in speaking. 

The sense of its value has grown upon me, and is stronger today than ever before. I am convinced that the art of speaking taught in our colleges by competent instructors would yield as large returns in terms of life values as any subject in the curriculum. 

I have hoped that I might be able to achieve two results, first, to say over again the old things so that they might have new meanings for modern students, and second, to make an original contribution to the subject in the way of formulating for the first time the foundation upon which all speech must rest, the source from which all speech must spring, the point of view from which all speech teaching and all speech learning must proceed.

As to the first of these results, any other writer on the subject is just as likely to prefer his manner of telling it to mine, and he may be right. As to the second, the conception of speech as the combined effect of the five responses, intellectual, emotional, verbal, vocal, and physical, I am convinced is destined to rescue "elocution" from the fire of criticism that threatened to destroy it.

The book is intended to serve as a suggestive reference for individual speakers and as a textbook for col-

Preface lege classes. By a little discretion on the part of the teacher in the way of judicious omission and selection it may just as well serve as the basis of instruction for high-school classes, and as a guide to readers, not yet speakers but ambitious to become speakers, who are unable to avail themselves of the benefits of a teacher's personal instruction.

The book makes no claim toward being complete or exhaustive; it does claim that the thoughts it contains are as straight as the author is capable of thinking them and are expressed in the best language at his command. 

It is believed that the book either expresses or suggests most of the important ideas of speaking. Many things, of course, remain to be said. The author was obliged to choose. Instead of making a larger book or printing it in smaller type, he preferred to omit many topics. that might profitably have been discussed. The teacher is at liberty to supply from other sources what is omitted from the book and is needed by his particular class.

If the teacher prefers a different order for the subject matter, I see no reason why he may not have it. In sending the book forth for teachers and students, the one thing I regard as of more importance than any other or than all others together is that speaking shall be studied and taught as the cumulative effect of the five responses. If studied and taught in this way, speech will be spontaneous and natural; if in any other way, it is bound to be imitative, stilted, affected, and unnatural.

If I may be allowed a final word to the student, it shall be an admonition not to regard speaking ability as an instantaneous result that comes to him, all at once, at the end of his course, but to think of it rather as a progressive achievement beginning with the first recitation and continuing, without a break, throughout

Preface all his efforts. College exercises and reports, in every subject, are the best possible practice in speaking, if the teacher gives the student an opportunity to stand and express his thoughts in a few connected sentences. 

The wise student will avail himself of every such opportunity.

THE SELECTIONS 

The selections for oral reading constituting Chapter II have been very carefully chosen. For their literary excellence alone they are a very creditable anthology. 

They also lend themselves admirably to voice development. Many of them are so old that they have become the common property of us all; but they are none the less literary gems. The many others are the copyrighted property of their respective publishers. It is through their courtesy that we are permitted to use them here. For this privilege the publishers of this book are deeply grateful, as will be also the students who use it. 

If the author is right in his belief that familiarity with these masterpieces will impel the reader to seek a larger acquaintance with their respective authors, then all parties will be abundantly repaid. A more specific statement of our indebtedness is:

THE ART OF SPEAKING CHAPTER I INTRODUCTION 

The heights of great men reached and kept 

Were not attained by sudden flight;

But they, while their companions slept, Were toiling upward in the night.

I cannot conceive anything more excellent than to be able, by language, to captivate the affections, to charm the understanding, and to impel or restrain the will of whole assemblies, at pleasure. Among every free people, especially in peaceful, settled governments, this simple art has always eminently flourished, and always exercised the greatest sway. For what can be more surprising than that, amidst an infinite multitude, one man should appear who shall be almost the only one capable of doing what nature has put in every man's power? Or, can anything impart such exquisite pleasure to the ear and to the intellect as a speech in which the wisdom and dignity of the sentiments are heightened by the utmost force and beauty of expression? Is there anything so commanding, so grand, as that the eloquence of one man should direct the inclinations of the people, the consciences of judges, and the majesty of senates?

CICERO. 

As one goes about the ordinary affairs of life, speech, because it is so common, is likely to impress one more as a commonplace than as an art. There is nothing strange in it till one begins to think about it; then it is marvelous. At first it is so simple that it needs no attention; then after we have thought about it, it seems too wonderful for any attention we are able to give. 

Then it dawns upon us that speech may become an art, even one of the fine arts. So it becomes necessary for us as students of the art of speaking to understand the meaning of art in general. This we shall try to do in the next chapter. The purpose of this introduction is to present some preliminary views or aspects of speak- 

II

ing that will enlighten us and encourage us throughout the entire course.

THE UNDERLYING CONCEPTION 

The author of this book claims, it is hoped with due modesty, but also with a good deal of assurance, to have set forth for the first time the fundamental conception on which must rest everything worth saying that can be said on the subject before us. By writers on speech we have always been admonished to speak naturally. Then these writers have proceeded to tell us how to speak naturally. No one ever speaks naturally when he speaks as some one has told him to speak. 

No one can tell any one else how to be natural. By working at it hard enough and long enough he can make some progress in arriving at a manner of speaking that will be natural for him, but it would not be natural for any one else. What we have lacked so far and what our present study proposes to supply is this underlying conception of speaking, on which or from which each one of us may work out a manner of speech natural for him but affected and unnatural for everybody else; worth everything to him but altogether worthless for everybody else.

This underlying conception of speech, on which all correct teaching and learning must be based, regards speaking as the cumulative effect of five responses made by the speaker. These five responses are not difficult to understand. Nor is it difficult to see that there are just five of them, no less and no more. Neither is it difficult to see that all speech teaching and learning that proceeds from this conception is good, and all that proceeds any other way is bad. But it is far from easy for one to make his own speech conform to these responses, and is probably even more difficult to teach others how to do it. In both these respects there are

Introduction 

13 countless thousands who are my superiors; my only claim to credit is for formulating the fundamental conception that all of us must follow or fail.

Briefly stated, these five responses that every speaker must make and that every speech must be the result of are: 

1.  is to the facts or materials on which the speech is based. In its nature this intellectual response is thought. The result it arrives at is meaning. By thinking on facts the speaker arrives at meaning, which is or should be most of his message. 

2. The Emotional Response is to the thought or meaning. The meaning is not quite all of the message. 

Sometimes meaning is all or nearly all of it. At other times there is a slight, almost negligible, shade or tinge of feeling to go along with the meaning. At still other times the feeling is most of the message. It outweighs the meaning, although it arises from the meaning. If a speaker is so constituted or is so ill trained that his emotional nature will not respond to the meaning with the suitable feeling, he can make at best only part of a speech, for he has only part of a message to express. 

Just as one may learn to see meaning in facts, so one may learn to have in mind the feeling that belongs with meaning. Both are susceptible of development. This development comes from proper training. 

3.  is made up of the words one uses. They are the beginning of the speech. The thought and the feeling are not parts of the speech, but they cannot be omitted from our conception, for they make up the message to be expressed. There is not anything that can be expressed except meaning and emotion. Like the others, one's verbal response can be improved. There is a little beauty in the mere sounds of words, and beauty also in the way they are

connected, but the chief excellence of one's verbal response lies in the accuracy, the clearness, and the adequacy with which his words express his meaning and his emotion. There are very few educational activities that pay larger dividends than the study of words. 

4. The Vocal Response is contributed by the speaker's voice. It is made up of the speaker's tones. If they add to the pleasure an audience gets from following a speaker's thought, participating in his feeling and hearing his words, then the speaker's vocal response is an asset; otherwise it is a liability. The vocal response is the adaptation of a speaker's tones to his meaning, his emotion, and his words. Almost any worthless voice can be made into a good one by diligent and intelligent practice. Few things are more beautiful than a beautiful voice, or uglier than an ugly voice. Let us begin today to improve our voices. 

5. The Physical Response is what the speaker does with his body in the way of posture and movement to assist his words and his voice in their effort to express his meaning and his emotion. It is all included under posture and movement either of the entire body or of its parts. Good posture and appropriate movements are an incalculable help to a speaker; bad posture and wrong movements are a great hindrance. Here again improvement is possible. All it costs is close attention and plenty of hard work. It will pay. 

While we may not prefer to say that all speech deserves to be classed as a fine art, we must concede that some of it does. If right at the beginning we desire to know when speaking is a fine art, the answer is, speaking is a fine art when the five responses of which it consists are the best they can be made. When we have followed through the discussion as to the meaning of art, it will then be clear to us that speaking, as a

Introduction 

15 

form of literature, belongs under the finest of the fine arts. At any rate, the student is requested, and urged, throughout the entire book or course, to consider speaking, all speaking, as the cumulative effect of these five responses, intellectual, emotional, verbal, vocal, and physical. If a speaker proceeds in a manner some one had told him to follow, his procedure is from without in, and is necessarily artificial, unnatural, affected. 

If on the other hand he allows his speech to be his own way of making these five responses, his procedure is from within out, and is bound to be natural, easy, and pleasing. As a result of what a speaker learns by reading and observing others, his way of making these responses will improve, but it must always be his way and not a borrowed one. One improves his manner of making these responses by thinking about it. That is the only purpose of books and teachers, to enable us to improve our manner of making these responses. 

OBSTACLES IN THE WAY 

When we listen to a great speaker and see his audience completely under his control, particularly if we happen to feel at the same time how far we are from him, our first thought is likely to be, "Orators are born." 

Yes, in a way they are. All men are. But not in the way we are likely to think. It is undoubtedly true that some men have an infinitely richer inheritance than the rest of us. Their native capacity for intelligence and emotion and will is far above ours. They have thought and feeling and resolution impossible for us; but this they have, in most cases, only after a period of study, training, development that we are not willing to undertake, or if we undertake it we try it half-heartedly for a day and drop it. We are not born with speech; it is the result of education. We all understand that we come into the world with a capacity for speech that has

been denied to the other animals; but if this capacity were allowed to remain permanently just as it is at birth, we too would be dumb animals. Speech making is an attainment, and any attainment means work. 

While it would be unwise to dispute this difference in native capacity, one's attainments, achievements, depend even more upon training and effort than upon one's native capacity. By this I do not mean that one by training may become able to do what he has no capacity for doing; but I do mean to say that in at least ninetynine cases out of a hundred the protest of lack of native ability is a lazy alibi for unwillingness to put forth the effort. I feel safe in making it stronger and saying that not more than one person in a billion ever brings his achievement in any line up to what he had the native capacity for.

PHYSICAL HANDICAPS 

Along with this we must recognize that some of us must labor under handicaps that others do not have. 

Some of us are too tall, others too short. Some of us are too large, others not large enough. Some of us are ugly, others fine-looking. Any of these conditions and many more do make a difference. Yes, for the first minute; but almost any one willing to make the effort can learn to speak so that after the first minute they are altogether unnoticed.

One may have such defective vocal organs that speech is impossible. In many cases even of this kind modern surgery can easily remove the defect. In many more cases the failure to speak well is not at all due to defective vocal organs but to a wrong use of them, which any one with the aid of a good teacher, and most persons without a teacher, can easily correct. Of course if one's tongue is inflexible, if he has no vocal chords, or if his under jaw has been left out, his speak-

Introduction 

17 ing can never become an art; but there are comparatively few such defects as these. Nine tenths of the 'speech defects" are imaginary, they do not in reality exist. The real defect is in the manner of speaking, which any one who makes up his mind to do it can correct. Nine tenths of the one tenth of real speech defects doctors can remedy. The few who are so unfortunate as to find themselves included in the class of the one tenth of the one tenth may console themselves with the thought that after all there are more important things than speaking. It is always better to sit down and think without being able to speak than to get up and speak without being able to think, and there are some such speakers. They too need not worry for they do not know but that they are thinking. So far as one's feelings are concerned one is no happier when he thinks than when he only thinks he thinks.

MENTAL HANDICAPS 

In addition to these physiological defects real and imaginary there is a mental obstacle that is just as hard to overcome. It is a kind of stage fright. It is a foolish fear, - foolish because the speaker knows he is in no danger and that the presence of the fear will not help but hurt his speech. A touch of this feeling is not bad, but good. It is good because it indicates that one is taking in earnest the responsibility of making a speech. 

It is always a large matter, a great undertaking, a real responsibility to stand up before a few or many people in the capacity of their instructor. The speaker must use this feeling to spur him on to greater preparation before he comes to his audience and to greater effort when he stands before them; but he must not allow it to get the better of him. He must keep it under his control. Whenever he allows himself to come under its control, then he is gone. There is no telling what he

will do or what he will say, except we may be sure that whatever he does or says will be the wrong thing. One sure way to let this feeling of timidity outgrow itself and become a greater handicap is to admit to ourselves that we have it; and another way is to tell everybody else that we have it and cannot control it. It is better to say nothing to any one else, and say to oneself, yes, I am deeply concerned, making that speech will be an important matter for me, I shall spare no effort, but when the occasion comes, I shall be master of it and of myself.

SPEAKING WELL AN ACHIEVEMENT 

Making a great speech always looks easy to one who sits back and looks on, but it is not so easy to the man making the speech. Or if it has become easy to him, it has not always been so. Some of our greatest political and platform orators have frankly admitted to us that to succeed they were obliged to master obstacles that would have discouraged the rest of us. 

Capacity for speaking is a gift. Yes, but speaking well is an art. Most of the men history has handed down to us as born orators had to conquer handicaps we know not of and make their way over obstacles that to us would have appeared insuperable: Demosthenes holds the place of the world's greatest orator; yet he is said to have had a weak voice, to have lisped, and to have been awkward in his manner. He cleared up his indistinct enunciation by making himself speak distinctly even with pebbles in his mouth. He strengthened his lungs and improved his breathing by declaiming as he ran up hill. By making his voice carry through or over the noise of dashing waves he became able to speak to great throngs of people. This also gave him such mastery over himself that later the clamor of mobs had no terror for him. It is inspiring

Introduction 

19 for us to realize that one who, without this effort and training, could not have been even a tolerable speaker, with it became the master of his art and the model for all the orators that were to follow. 

Charles James Fox entered the English Parliament as a very awkward youth, speaking with needless repetitions, yet by intelligent practice became one of the foremost English orators. Henry Clay, born in the midst of poverty and hardship, had but the most meager opportunity for schooling. History has named him "The Mill Boy of the Slashes.' He was denied altogether the privilege of early training, which means so much. At the age of twenty-seven, he began the practice, which he continued for many years, of reading and speaking aloud on the contents of historical or scientific books. This daily practice in the woods, in the cornfields, or in the barn with the horses and the oxen for his hearers was the only school of oratory he ever attended. To it he attributes his marvelous eloquence.

Abraham Lincoln was not unlike Clay in this respect. 

He tells us he never allowed to pass unused an opportunity to make a speech, or to try to make one. Out in the woods, even before he had finished splitting his rail pile, on the slightest provocation he would mount a stump and make a speech to his fellow workman. This was his preparation for the Lincoln-Douglas Debates, his "House-Divided against Itself" speech, his Second 

Inaugural Address, the Cooper Union Speech, and the

Gettysburg Address. 

Daniel Webster, with all his native ability for speech, had handicaps to overcome. In his elementary school he could not speak like the other boys. He would commit his piece to memory, but when the day for speaking it came, he could not raise himself out of his seat: 

If at the earnest entreaty of his teacher he at-

tempted it, he always felt that he had failed, and as he says, "went home and wept bitter tears of mortification."

WRITING OR SPEECH 

Which is mightier, the pen or the sword, is easily answered if we take for the sword the orator's tongue, and entrust the pen to any but the most facile fingers. 

Every time, at least the temporary victory will go to the speaker. Good speakers have always been much in demand. Plutarch tells us what large sums were paid at Athens to teachers of rhetoric; and Emerson adds that if the pupils got what they paid for, the lessons were cheap. Lord Chesterfield, who though not the wisest of men was certainly not without a generous share of practical wisdom, constantly repeated his admonition to his son to master the art of speaking. 

One of England's greatest poets, Ben Jonson, has given us a classic description of this art: "There happened in my time one noble speaker who was full of gravity in his speaking. No man ever spoke more neatly, more expressively, more weightily, or suffered less emptiness, less idleness, in what he uttered. No member of his speech but consisted of its own graces. 

His hearers could not cough or look aside from him without loss. He commanded where he spoke, and had his judges angry or pleased at his devotion. The fear of every man that heard him was that he should make an end." Jonson is doubtless referring to Lord Bolingbroke, the idol of both Swift and Pope. Dugald

Stewart says of the paragraph quoted, "No finer description of the perfection of this art is to be found in any author ancient or modern." 

THE VALUE OF SPEECH 

Cicero, the greatest of Roman orators, says, "Great is the labor that qualifies speaking, great the art itself,

Introduction 

21 great its dignity, and most great, too, the influence connected with it.' Pericles says, "A man who forms a judgment on any point without being able to express it clearly to the people might as well have never thought on the subject." This, of course, is a very ancient estimate of the value of speaking, and times do change. 

Yet there are good reasons for believing that speech is now constantly increasing its claim for a larger share of attention. And modern authority is quite as strong in praising it as were the ancients. 

George Murray, Canadian educator and literarian, says, "Mankind seems to agree almost unanimously that no accomplishment gains consideration for its possessor so speedily as public speaking; and there is none for which there is so persistent a demand.” 

INTEREST GROWING 

More and more people are becoming interested in speech. There are now few if any high schools and colleges that do not teach it; and in all of them along with the courses in public speaking even more emphasis is placed upon the less formal speech of conversation, of literary and other clubs, of student gatherings, of directors' meetings and of similar small assemblages. 

Everybody seems eager to learn. There never has been a time when a warmer welcome awaited the man who has something to say along with the ability to say it in a pleasing, forceful manner. Even while I am writing this paragraph I am interrupted to hear read from Booth Tarkington the statement that in his opinion people everywhere from this time on are going to depend less and less on books and more and more on speech. 

Of course he does not know, but this is certainly a very significant prophecy to come from one of America's foremost writers of books.

A NEGLECTED ART 

Notwithstanding the array of authority in favor of the merits of speech, it is almost completely neglected by at least ninety-nine persons out of every hundred, while it deserves to be stressed as an important part of the education of at least nine hundred and ninety-nine persons out of every thousand. I am omitting one in every thousand on the assumption that possibly this small number either from being tongue-tied or of too low mentality might not be able to profit by the training. But I have been too conservative; there is probably not one in a thousand unable to profit by speech training. I have just finished reading an excellent speech article by Emily Clegg Balls in which she admits that there may be a few cases of tongue-tied children but says that "in all my handling of more than 5000 school children I have never seen a tongue-tied child." So, nearly all the defects of speech, and they are legion, are due to neglect. 

Now, why the neglect? I shall let Dr. George 

Murray answer. He gives two reasons: First, most people regard speech as a gift or instinct peculiar to the few and unattainable by the many. They believe that the speaker is born, not made; while it would be nearer the truth to say that the speaker is made, not born. 

Mr. Murray admits, as we all must, that there are a few who never can become good speakers, that in this respect some are better fitted than others to become speakers; but he correctly puts the emphasis where it belongs, on intelligent practice. He gives as the second cause of the neglect, that even those who admit that the results in speaking are due chiefly to labor are unwilling to put forth the effort required.

Introduction 

23 

WHEN TO BEGIN 

Many suppose that they can never become able to speak because they did not begin speaking early in life. 

Henry Ward Beecher recommends as early a start as possible, and he is evidently right; but he does not mean to say that it is ever too late to begin. Speaking is not different from other achievements in this respect, — the earlier one begins it and the harder he works at it the greater will be his accomplishment; but a late beginning under wise direction and with dogged persistence will accomplish more than an early spurt that is over almost as soon as it begins. In the same way, one of only ordinary ability with intelligent study and persistent practice will in the end outstrip his more brilliant competitor who depends upon brilliancy to carry him through. As we observe different speakers, noting that some seem to be ever ready and others never ready, we can easily fall upon the wrong explanation of the difference. Without denying a difference in native capacity for speaking, we may be reasonably certain that the never-ready man has postponed his preparation to a more convenient season. This is true not only in one's mastery of language but also in his marshalling of his thought. For one ambitious to be a speaker it is a good practice to think through clearly and definitely every topic that is suggested, finding the arguments for and those against every proposition, and writing them out in the very best language one can command.

Nothing but weeds just grows. Every kind of harvest means hard work in abundance. The soil of natural genius, if let alone, is more likely to produce weeds than fruit.

READINESS 

Readiness in thought and speech is universally conceded to be a merit. Other things being equal, the

This readiness of speech enhances its value. In this rapidly moving world this is truer today than ever before. value that everybody attaches to readiness can, however, easily lead us into error, the error of speaking, or talking, before we have anything to say. It is always better, even at the loss of a reputation for readiness, to have speech wait upon thought. This is not easy. 

Indeed it is a task too big for most of us. I would not be thought irreverent, but I have often wondered why it would not have been better if our creator had made us so we could not talk without thinking. Since for some reason unknown to us he did not see fit to do it in that way, we ourselves must do the best we can to make the verbal response wait upon the intellectual, to hold the mouth back till the mind catches up. It will be difficult, but the results will abundantly justify the effort. 

In many cases a mouth moratorium of a year would not be bad. It would be good if the time were spent in thinking. We do not fool any one but ourselves by talking without thinking. Noisy volubility, unrestrained garrulity, empty loquacity may be talk, but it can hardly be called speech. It is a mere biological response akin to the goo, gloo of the infant or the oug, oug of the savage, from which in time speech may or may not evolve. Most speaking is improved by painstaking effort to reduce the quantity and enhance the quality.

MOOD 

It is very important to a speaker that his audience be in the right mood, the mood in which he wants them for his particular purpose. But it is vastly more important that the speaker himself be in the right mood. If he is in the proper mood the chances are that the audience soon will be; and then if he is in the wrong mood, it will be impossible for him to get his message to them even if

Introduction 

25 their mood is perfect. The speaker's first task is to see that he is in the right mood to speak. He must be confident of himself and of his subject. He must be free from worry, annoyance, anger, or any other ugly feeling. Then he must proceed to put his audience in a receptive, responsive, coöperative, sympathetic mood. 

This he can do by letting them see that they have a part, a large part, in the speech; that the success of it depends as much upon them as upon himself. They too, on most occasions, must be in a happy, joyful mood. He must see that they are in the right mood or state of mind before he starts. The whole purpose of his introductory remarks or stories is to put his audience in such a mood or state of mind. But remember: the mood is for the message. Don't keep the stories going so long that no time will be left for the message. 

EARNESTNESS 

A listless, lifeless speaker will be listened to by us, if we are reasonably sure that he is in possession of facts that we are obliged to have, but we never hear him gladly. All the great orators, whether of ancient or of modern times, have been men and women of great earnestness. They have likewise been men and women of great energy, but energy and earnestness are not quite the same. The word energy is more likely to suggest much movement, which may or may not go along with earnestness. Earnestness is more of the nature of feeling. It is a kind of inner fire or enthusiasm for the subject being presented. It is universally admirable. It was Saint Francis who said, "People will always come out to see a fire." So they will. Earnestness will make up for a multitude of missing qualities. 

If a speaker is deeply in earnest, we hear him even when we know his thought is wrong. If this inner earnest-

ness has coupled with it the right kind and amount of physical movement, it is all the more impressive. It is always better, however, to have too little movement than too much. Earnestness can be very impressive with but little movement, but much movement without real earnestness is intolerable. About the same relation exists between earnestness and the loudness of one's voice. If properly combined, the effect is always best. Yet earnestness can go along with very low tones and still be effective, while bawling and yelling without real earnestness only makes us wonder what it is all about, if it does even that much. 

As a rule, young men have more earnestness than old men. At least this is as it should be. Many times, however, a young man makes a mistake by assuming the quiet manner of an old man that he admires. This is precisely as it should not be. By means of a rich experience acquired in a long and eventful life a man comes into possession of characteristics that may hold the attention of an audience even in the absence of much earnestness. This absence, however, must always be regarded as a defect and not a merit. It is always commendable in youth to want to learn from age, but they must never carry this to the extreme of imitating the shortcomings of age. Every young man should kindle this Saint Francis fire, and every older man should keep it burning as long as he can. In speech there is but little excellence without great earnestness.

DETERMINATION TO SUCCEED 

The speaker must always do his best, but he cannot always expect to succeed. Any one who speaks often must occasionally fail. He must not allow himself to be too easily discouraged. The farmer does not always reap a bounteous harvest. Occasionally his crop will

Introduction 

27 fail, but when spring comes he plows again. So must the speaker. He must neither be too much elated by praise nor too much cast down by criticism. One must make up his own mind as to whether he can do the thing or not. If he thinks that he can, feels that he can, wills that he can, there are few obstacles that he will not override. When Richard Brinsley Sheridan, who had been eminently successful as a dramatist, entered the English Parliament and made a miserable failure in his maiden speech, Woodfall, a good judge of public speaking, said to him: "I am sorry to say that I do not think this is your line; you had much better have stuck to your former pursuits." After a short pause Sheridan said: "It is in me, and, by God, it shall come out."

It did come out. In his parliamentary career of twelve years he won a name as an orator. Particularly his speeches against Warren Hastings have given him enduring fame. He had many faults in speech that he was never able to overcome; but by prodigious toil he became one of the greatest orators of a distinguished group of great orators. When he ended his six-hour speech against Hastings, Parliament burst into an involuntary tumult of applause. This, we are told, was the first time that any speech in that august body had ever been received with cheers. Burke, the leader of the prosecution, who spoke not six hours but six days, said of Sheridan's speech that it was "the most extraordinary effort he had ever witnessed." Fox said of it, that "all that he had ever heard, all that he had ever read, when compared with it, dwindled into nothing, and vanished like vapor before the sun." Pitt, one of

Sheridan's critics, said of this speech that "it surpassed all the eloquence of ancient and modern times, and possessed everything that genius or art could furnish to agitate and control the human mind." It is unfortunate that this great speech and the author's first

attempt could not have been preserved so that we might read them in parallel columns and know more of the toil that bridged the gulf between them, but both of them are lost.

Benjamin Disraeli's experience was not altogether different. His maiden effort in Parliament was cut short by a burst of inextinguishable laughter. He was obliged to stop, and closed with these words: "I am not surprised at the reception which I have experienced. 

I will sit down now, but the time will come when you will hear me." We all know well how true was his prophecy.

But no matter where we go we find the same old story over again. Almost without exception, the great orators, both ancient and modern, became great by working their way up through obstacles seemingly insuperable. No excellence without great labor is truer nowhere than in speech.

The author makes the following recommendations to be kept in mind by students throughout all the recita tion or discussion Exercises of this book: 

(1) Do not, of course, unnecessarily multiply your words, but think enough on each topic or question to be able to make a connected speech on it of three or more sentences. (2) In making a recitation or report of this length, though not of course when your answer is to be a single word, stand. (3) When you stand, stand the best you can. Right at the very beginning, and throughout the entire course, assume and maintain the best possible posture when you stand or sit. (4) Whether your recitation is long or short, speak the best you can in every respect. When you learn anything about speaking, begin immediately to put it in practice.

Introduction 

29 (5) Do not assume the debating attitude and feel under obligation to refute all the statements made in the book; some of them may be correct. Do not go to the opposite extreme of feeling bound to agree with every statement of the author. Have an open mind. 

Have independence of judgment. Feel free to take exception to any statement that to you seems wrong. 

State your own views. Give your reasons for them. 

If the author could hear them, he might agree with you.

Remember: a large part of any education is the ability to make independent judgments, to make one's own decisions.

EXERCISES 

1. Why is it that speech sometimes seems to be only a commonplace, and at other times a fine art? What is the purpose of an introduction? 

2. State briefly the underlying conception of speech that the author claims as his contribution to the subject. In the light of this new conception of speaking, tell when it is natural and when it is the opposite. 

3. State briefly, but clearly, the first of the five responses that make up this new conception of speaking. The second. The third. The fourth. The fifth. 

4. In what sense are orators born? In what sense are they made? Distinguish capacity for speech and ability to make speeches. 

5. Name and discuss each of the two kinds of speech handicaps. 

6. In this matter of speech, distinguish gift and attainment. 

7. What points of interest can you mention with reference to the world's greatest orator? With reference to other eminent speakers? 

8. Discuss (a) writing and speech, (b) Jonson's description of a great orator, (c) the value of speech, (d) its neglect, (e) the time to begin speech training, (f) readiness, (g) mood, (h) earnestness, (i) determination.

CHAPTER II THE MEANING OF ART 

IN understanding any subject it is first necessary to form at least a rough boundary of the field in which it is included. To arrive at a notion of the art of speaking we must come to an understanding of the meaning of Art in general. This we shall do best not by making a formal definition but by grasping a few fundamental distinctions. This need not be difficult. It is probably true that a large majority of students beginning this study are having their first acquaintance with art in any form. This is well enough. No liberal education is complete or even tolerable without this elementary grasp of the meaning of Art. It would not be out of place in connection with other studies, but it is certainly in place in connection with our particular subject. Indeed it is indispensable here. Let us note the following distinctions:

ART IS EXPRESSION 

The first, simplest, most elemental notion of art is that it is expression. Art is always expression. 

Whether expression is always art we may determine later. Man expresses himself because he must. It is his nature. Of all human instincts that of expression is the deepest, most fundamental. We are perfectly safe in saying that expression is the law of life. 

USEFUL AND FINE ARTS 

The classification into useful and fine arts is based upon the end in view. The useful arts aim at the 30

accomplishment of certain work, while the fine arts seek only the satisfaction of the emotions. One aims at work; the other, at beauty. Or, we may say, one is work; and the other, play. We never ask a musician to work a piece; we always request her to play it. It would be well enough to speak of the works of Immanuel Kant, but it is always best to speak of the plays of Shakespeare. A useful art aims at an end beyond itself; a fine art is an end in itself. 

THE TWO COMBINED 

A useful and a fine art are often combined. Then the aim is twofold, first, to produce a useful thing, and second, to make it as beautiful as possible. Here belongs Architecture, in which the object is not merely to build a house that will shelter us, but one that is also pleasing to look at. It must be both useful and beautiful. This same combination is present also in all home decoration and in every phase of the decorative arts. 

That is, it is always some useful thing that is decorated. 

We might note in passing that it is always the useful phase that dominates. To this the emotional end must be subordinate. Any attempt to embellish a useful thing is commendable only so far as it does not detract from its usefulness. When we go to this extreme, we are justly accused of bad taste. The two aims are combined more often than we at first think, and our aesthetic judgments take both into consideration. For illustration, a roof of tile or tin or wood may be beautified by paint, for the paint not only does not lessen its utility but increases it; but such a roof could not be made more beautiful by perforating it with holes arranged in symmetrical figures. Whatever pleasure we might be able to get from the symmetry would be more than offset by the thought that the holes render the roof less useful in serving its original purpose, to

The fundamental turn away the rain and the snow. purpose of a window is to admit light, not to serve as a rack for hanging curtains and draperies. These of course may be used both to beautify the window and to soften the light; but when they are used so as to exclude the light, we feel disappointed. The utility that we labored to create has been destroyed. The feeling is not that of beauty, but just the opposite. The purpose of a column is strength to support a part of a building. We may beautify it if we can and as much as we will, provided only that we do not interfere with its utility; but whenever we undertake to add beautiful figures to it by cutting them into it, we are not pleased but pained by the thought of diminished strength. 

The fundamental purpose of a hymn is a feeling of reverence, of worship. Any frills or runs or waltz touches that may be wrought into it are admissible so long as they add to the religious feeling. Yes, they are permissible, if in themselves they are pleasing, even so long as they do not detract from this feeling. But if they mean the loss of the feeling, or even a lessening of it, they are not art. So far as I know, whenever the two ends are combined, it is utility that controls, and beauty is subordinate.

THE TWO ENDS COMBINED IN SPEECH 

It is not impossible to view our own subject in the relation just explained. The fundamental purpose of all speech is utility, the expression of thought. To make speech an art we must not only express truth, we must express truth beautifully. Unless beauty is present there is no art. Art without beauty is impossible. 

The embellishment of speech is not only allowable, it is necessary to make it an art. This one warning, however, is in place: Whenever one permits either the kind or the amount of embellishment that renders the mean-

ing to be expressed a little less clear, the feeling is one of pain, and so there is no art. Covering up meaning with adjectives merely to exhibit the adjectives is worse than painting a lily or a stone; it is like whittling a column. 

SCIENCE AND ART 

The word science always has reference to knowing, and the word art to doing. In the consideration of portions of writing or speech it is not always easy to know whether to class them as science or as art. Sometimes a very slight change in the wording will change art to science or science to art. If the end and aim of a book or a paragraph is merely to set forth relations, then that book or paragraph is science; but if its purpose is to tell us how some work may be accomplished, then it is of the nature of art. "Lime has a tendency to take the sourness out of soil," is science; "if you want to sweeten sour soil, lime it," is art. be Then the distinction between science and art may seen in another view. The difference is not one of subject matter. Science and art may both treat the same subject. The basis of the distinction is the nature of the mental state. The character of the writing or speaking depends upon the nature of the mental state to be expressed. It is interesting, and it may be helpful, to note four ways in which thoughts may arise in consciousness and the corresponding difference in their expressions. First, a thought may arise in the mind as a cold, utterly indifferent relation. Then its expression is science. It may appear chiefly as relation but more or less tinctured with feeling. Then its expression is the so-called popular or magazine science. 

In the third place, a feeling may arise so hazily connected with any thought and therefore so incompatible with any form, that any attempt at expression is silly sentimentality or mere mush. In a fourth form, a

thought may arise in connection with a feeling that dominates but does not obscure it. The expression, in suitable form, of this feeling together with its thoughtsetting, is art.

FOUR FORMS OF WRITING OR SPEAKING 

It would be easy to find in literature illustrations of these four forms of expression of many different thoughts or conceptions. We shall take space here for only one such conception, Death and Hope. (1) The physician's plain announcement that when the heart ceases to beat and the lungs to breathe the condition is death, is science. And when the psychologist writes that the human mind is so constituted that even in the face of the most adverse conditions it kindles a hope, this also is science. (2) For an example of popular or magazine science, almost any magazine article on our subject would answer. Also, any good sermon on Death and Hope would illustrate it. (3) Emotional sermons and patriotic addresses furnish abundant illustration of mere sentimentality. Or, about as good an illustration would be the last sentence in an old subscription book called The Royal Path of Life, which I am taking time to set down here as an excellent example of how we should not often write or speak. It happens to treat the same subject, Death and Hope. Here is the sentence: "When this hand of mine shall be pulseless and cold, and motionless as the grave wherein it must lie; when the damp, dewy vapors shall replace this sensible warm motion, and Death shall spread my couch and weave my shroud; when the winding sheet shall be my sole vesture, and the close-sealed sepulcher my only home, and I shall have no familiar companion, and no rejoicing friend but the worm; oh, thou cold hand of Death, unlock for me the portals of eternal life, that whilst my

body rests in its bed of earth, my soul may recline in the bosom of God." While all the emotions in this paragraph are permissible and even commendable, the author has linked them with such flimsy and hazy thought and has so paraded them that his peroration is a highly satisfactory example of mental mush. As a fairly safe standard we may say that the art value of any paragraph, book, or speech is inversely proportional to the square of the number of its exclamations. This paragraph is all exclamation. Art is always impersonal and universal, while this paragraph is personal and particular. It makes us think, and without much regret, that it is the author who has passed away; while art would rather make us think of death as equally applicable to all. Also, the author of this sentence puts his passions on parade, while the artist would always hold them in reserve. Now, for a real art expression of these same conceptions, Death and Hope, much of both poetry and prose is open to us. I shall remind you of but two classic illustrations, with both of which we are all familiar: When Robert Ingersol stands over the open grave of his dead brother he says, - "Life is a narrow veil between the cold and barren peaks of two eternities. We strive in vain to look beyond the heights. We cry aloud, and the only answer is the echo of our wailing cry. From the voiceless lips of the unreplying dead there comes no word; but in the night of death, hope sees a star, and listening love can hear the rustle of a wing." When one hears or reads these wonderful words, one knows he is face to face with art. 

Alfred Tennyson has given us another masterpiece of art dealing with the same theme: 

Sunset and evening star, And one clear call for me, 

And may there be no moaning of the bar 

When I put out to sea;

But such a tide as moving seems asleep, Too full for sound and foam,

When that which drew from out the boundless deep 

Turns again home. 

Twilight and evening bell, And after that the dark, — 

And may there be no sadness of farewell 

When I embark; 

And though from out our bourne of Time and Place 

The flood may bear me far, I hope to see my Pilot face to face 

When I have crossed the bar.

NATURE AND ART Art must be distinguished also from nature. Art always looks to nature for its patterns, and succeeds or fails according as it reproduces nature well or ill. Art must faithfully reproduce the phases of nature it selects, and is accountable for the fidelity with which it does it. 

It is accountable also for the conditions under which, and the purpose for which, it reproduces nature. A great responsibility rests upon art. It cannot justify itself merely by its faithful reproduction of some phase of nature. Not all phases of nature are equally worthy of being reproduced. There are star-lit nights and golden sunsets; but there are also marshes, bogs, and swamps. Art is accountable for the nature it selects. 

And then art must answer for its purpose. This purpose is always to leave life better than it finds it. 

The old slogan, "Art for Art's sake," has been superseded. Art is not a finality. Only life is that. All art is for life's sake. Any expression that is without such a purpose, that does not seek in some way to lift life to a higher plane, whatever else it may be, is not art. Any expression that picks out random phases of nature and

reproduces them, no matter how faithfully, may be photography, but unless it has a purpose it is not art. 

Art must have a purpose, and this purpose is always life. 

Nature and art are alike in that both are beautiful. 

Some of nature is beautiful, all of art is beautiful. The two differ in their origin. We find nature; we make art. All art is a product of the human hand. It is an old saying that "God made the country and man made the town. We might as well make it general and say, "God made nature, and man art.”

Nature and art are often combined. The sky is nature, and the earth and the sea. The rugged mountain side untouched by the hand of man is nature. The winding roadway we make up the mountain, the trees we plant to shade it, and the cottage we build by it, are art. A beautiful girl dressed for a reception is probably the most marvelous combination of nature and art to be found in earth or sea or sky. The girl herself as God made her is nature. The gown in which she is to make her conquests is art. Her own golden hair as it hangs in hundreds of entrancing ringlets is nature; the creations in which she does it up are art. The sparkle in her eye is nature; the way in which she sparkles it is art. The color in her cheek is nature; the skill with which it is put on is art.

In a sense, art must always be true to nature. Otherwise it could not be understood, and could have no meaning for life. Art is never under obligation to copy nature in all its details or particulars, but in the large it must square up with reality. For illustration, a novel may represent a character different from any person that ever lived, but the character must be such a one as might have lived. He may do things that have never been done, but the things he does must not be impossible. This makes it possible for art to enlarge upon nature, and bring to us lessons that nature nowhere

contains. It is perfectly correct to say that fiction may be truer than history, meaning that its generalizations may contain for us infinitely more truth, and sometimes more helpful truth, than the little reality it is possible for us to experience.

RELIGION AND ART 

Throughout all time there has existed a very close relation between art and religion. The reason for this connection is apparent when we look a little into the meaning of each. Art is always and everywhere the expression of man's longing for beauty; while religion, as some one has told us, is on its inner side the need man feels for a friend, and on its outward side always and everywhere the expression of this need. The two are not the same, but are closely akin. The impulse that gives birth to art and the other impulse that is religion are both feelings. The two are mutually dependent. 

As the Greeks were fond of saying, the beautiful and the good are inseparable. A reverent mood gives beauty a stronger appeal, and an æsthetic state of mind makes one more receptive to religion. On the one hand, friendship is the most beautiful thing in the world; and on the other, religion has never been satisfied with any expression of itself that is not beautiful. Throughout all the ages, art has been the handmaid of religion, while religion has persistently striven to give itself not merely expression but beautiful expression. Just as a really beautiful spirit cannot be irreligious, so a really religious spirit cannot be ugly. 

Bailey, I think, is extreme when he says that "because man is religious he becomes an artist." It is closer to the facts to say that man becomes an artist because he is intelligent, for it is the sum total of his intelligence that clamors for expression. Every thought,

feeling, or impulse that arises in consciousness comes attended by a more or less conscious urge to give itself expression. This urge or longing is the mother of art, for without it all life would be subjective, art would never be born. Any beautiful thought and feeling will, if given adequate expression, become art. Of course the creator of a cathedral is an artist. So also is Helen as she weaves into her couch covers and her tapestries the heroic feats and the warlike deeds of the Achæans. So is Vulcan as he makes his hammer give expression in brass to the same or similar deeds on the shield of Achilles. Art, unfortunately, is not always confined to the good. The devil himself has always been represented as a consummate artist. As Mephistopheles seeks the destruction of Faust, he is approached only by Iago working for the undoing of Othello. It is true, however, and we cannot overemphasize it, that our religious thoughts and feelings, because they mean most to our lives, are the most insistent in their demands for expression. This accounts for the fact that the most of all art and the highest of all art is religious. And even when the main conception to be expressed is not religious, there is nearly always by suggestion or implication a sacred thread so run through its setting as to make the religious meaning the most of the message. If for just a moment, in one grand sweep of our vision, we might have held up before us all the art of all the ages, if then by one magic stroke we might eliminate all of it that is distinctly and predominantly religious, if by another stroke we could do away with all that was largely religious, and if then by another wave of our wand we might cause to disappear from what remained all that had in it any suggestion or hint of religion, would be interesting and instructive to see the little that remained in contrast with the much that had been taken away. 

it

ELEMENTS OF ART 

Always and everywhere art has four elements. First, it is feeling. This is the chief object of its existence. 

Herein is its striking resemblance to religion. A picture, poem, or piece of music that has not sprung from feeling and does not lead to feeling is not art. 

Second, it has meaning. Emotion can arise only in connection with thought. All art must have a message in the nature of meaning. Third, it has form. Indispensable as it is, the form must always remain subordinate to the feeling and the thought. In fact, its whole excuse for being is that it helps to make possible the expression of feeling and thought, emotion and meaning. Form varies, of course, with the different arts. 

The particular form chosen must always be the one that will give the most adequate expression of the emotion with its thought-setting. Fourth, it has technic. By technic is meant the use the artist makes of the materials at hand to compel the form to express the emotion and the meaning. It will be readily seen that the relative importance of these four elements varies with the different arts. For illustration, music is strong in emotion and gives us less meaning than any other art, while in literature the meaning is always dominant, the emotion being less prominent in some writing than in others. The reader might bear in mind that our own subject of speech is always expected to be in this respect much more like literature than like music. It scarcely needs to be pointed out that the art of speaking contains all four of these elements present in all art, feeling, meaning, form, technic.

COBBLER, ARTIST, GENIUS 

The element of technic enables us to see the relation of art to the field below it and to the realm above it.

For illustration, if one is haphazard in his procedure, works without a plan, has no method, he has no technic, he is not an artist but a cobbler or a bungler. Again, if one is not conscious of any technic, but builds better than he knows; that is, if the technic is really present without his being able to recognize it, he accomplishes great ends without being able to tell or even to know how or why he does it, he is not only an artist but a genius. It is entirely possible that a great workman should have his artist moments and his genius moods; that a masterpiece should be in part a creation of art and in part a contribution of genius. Critics have pointed out that in the same play, Shakespeare acts in all three of these capacities, cobbler, artist, genius. 

Speaking only for myself, Shakespeare's genius is always apparent, occasionally I get a glimpse of the technic that stamps him an artist, but it is more difficult for me to come upon him in the capacity of a cobbler. 

The statement just made, that technic is a mark of art, must not be construed to mean that it is absent from a work of genius. It is there just as it is in all art, but the genius is not conscious of it. The two may arrive at the same result. The artist does know, and the genius does not know, how he came by it. Here one must guard himself against the inference that if he doesn't know what he is doing, he is a genius. Maybe he is; but maybe he is something else. Neither the genius nor the cobbler knows what he is about. Edgar 

Allan Poe, James Russell Lowell, and Robert Browning were great artists; but John Bunyan, who left us a message that grade children and mature men may ponder with profit; Plato, who gave us the immortal 

Dialogs from which the world's philosophers for thirty centuries have gleaned their life-lessons; and Homer, the magic spell of whose tales holds us today as it held the slaves, the scholars, and the artists of ancient

Greece, geniuses. these men were more than artists, they were 

PRINCIPLES OF ART 

For all of the little that I know, there may be many principles of art. Here I am naming only two. (1) Unity. The first is unity. A work of art must have but one dominant emotion and one leading thought. Of course it may have many thoughts and many emotions, but all the others must be subordinate to the leading one. A house with two towers just alike and of the same size is a house divided against itself. 

It cannot stand as art. A ring with two stones just alike and of the same size is not pleasing until the two stones are made into two rings, or until the two are both subordinated to a larger one placed between them. A student has declared that the Bible, from cover to cover, is on but a single subject. The same is true of 

Homer's Iliad, of Hamlet, and of Othello. A great picture must tell but one story. A great sermon must never be two sermons. A great speech must set forth but one fundamental thought. The same is true of every great piece of music, it must have but one theme and not two. There may be diversity, but it must be only the diversity that can be in unity. For illustration let us recall Chopin's Funeral March. The name expresses the theme, a funeral march. First, there is no other step in which there is so much solemnity as there is in the march. The music not only lets us see and feel the march, but makes us hear the mournful rumble of the cold clay on the coffin. Following this is a strain of sprightly music, full of sunshine, gayety, sweetness, and mirth. How does this come to be in order in a Funeral March? Only Psychology can give us the answer. Here I think it is: The mind is so constituted that it cannot hold in view continuously and for

a very long time even the thought of a great sorrow. 

Instead it must be followed by its opposite. That is, for example, in the very instant when the mother is mourning for a child that is gone, there bursts into the mind the memory of the happiest moment she has ever spent with that child, — the rollicking fun, the playful laugh, the innocent prattle, the bounding life. It is the extreme opposite of sorrow. If the music should end here, it would be two pieces, and not one; it would not be art. But it does not end here. The musical artist knows not only the concourse of sweet sounds, but he knows also the working of the human mind. And so, just as the sorrowing mother in the presence of death will feel first the gloom of night, then the gayety and the beauty of the morning, and finally again the grief only deepened by the merry memory that but for a moment intervened, so Chopin in his Funeral March lets us hear first the rumble of the clods on the coffin, then innocent prattle and gay laughter, and finally leaves us again in the gloom of the grave. He does give us variety, but in a unity that is present wherever art is present.

Unity involves simplicity. All great art is necessarily simple. All unnecessary elaboration detracts. Affectation kills. Simplicity pleases. Here again we may cite the Bible, the Iliad, the Odyssey, Bunyan's Pilgrim's Progress, Lincoln's Gettysburg Address, and the

Angelus. Let us think for a moment of the Angelus. 

Many years ago, too many, while I was a student in Boston, some art association put on what was called The Ver-est-cha-gin Exhibition. In the advertisement, Ver-est-cha-gin was declared to be the world's greatest painter of war scenes and no one doubted it. Everybody was talking about the exhibit and everybody was going, so I went. At that time I knew even less about art than I do now, but I was eager to learn. I did not

know so very well what to look for in a picture, or how to see it when I was told, but I felt confident that a Boston audience would lead me aright in matters of art. 

The exhibit was on the four walls of a very large room. 

I entered it just behind a group of about twenty persons who looked and talked as if they knew what they were about. In just a moment we were passing before the most elaborate pictures I have ever seen. 

Great heaps and piles of cannon, muskets, swords, bayonets, wagons, dead horses, dead and dying men, and all the blood and carnage of war were portrayed in color on the canvas. 

They were also the largest pictures I have ever seen. 

The smallest single picture covered as much space as the entire side of an ordinary living room, and the larger ones would cover at least four such sides. I followed the group of strangers and we saw these large and elaborate pictures as we walked. When I had entered the room I noticed just to the right of the entrance a company of twenty or thirty persons, all looking in the same direction, standing very still and so close together that there was no room for anyone else, - all absolutely quiet, no one daring to whisper. When I came round to the starting place, they were still there. When I returned the second time and the third time they were there. I knew they could not be seeing as large a picture as I had been looking at, for the space was small, but I did want to know what could be keeping that little group from seeing the main exhibit, the great war pictures. Then my curiosity got the better of me.

I must see what holds these people as if by magic. For the moment I allowed myself to forget where I was, and elbowed myself in far enough to be able to see the picture. It was not a copy, but the original, — not large, not elaborate, probably the simplest picture I have ever seen, — not more than eighteen inches wide by thirty inches long. There were only two characters

in it, a young man and his wife, peasants out in the field digging potatoes or some such vegetable. The angelus bell has just struck. Instantly they cease from their toil, and with bowed heads they stand giving thanks to God for the few blessings that are theirs. 

This was the picture. All the Ver-est-cha-gin Exhibition portraying the horrors of war could not compete with the simplicity of the Angelus. If I were asked to name the theme of the Angelus, that is, to give its meaning that will account for its powerful appeal, I should say it is The Supremacy of the Spiritual over the 

Material. 

(2) Suggestion. The second principle of art is suggestion. Art must be suggestive. It must suggest more than it tells. It must not tell anything that it can suggest. Suggestion is more impressive and more pleasing than direct expression. Art proceeds by indirection. It hints or suggests the truth it wishes to convey by expressing another truth implying it. The underlying principle of art is said by a Latin writer to be idem in alia, the same thing in other things. Skillful questions that suggest the answer are more effective than bald statements. If Cicero had simply declared to Cataline that he should no longer abuse the patience of the Senate, his declaration would have been without force and without effect. The other Senators would have felt that Cicero himself had settled the question, leaving nothing for them to do. But Cicero was an artist. He asked the question, "How long, O Cataline, will you continue to abuse our patience?" This question not only suggests the answer, but makes every other senator feel that the responsibility is with him as much as it is with Cicero. Here is the psychology of suggestion: Approach or progress toward an end is more pleasing than having the end dropped at our feet. 

Suggestion is always progressive. Some time ago I

saw an advertising picture that I am sure would not be classed as high art, but it did contain in a high degree this principle of suggestion. Here is the picture: A young man hastily leaving a front door, his coat tails spread upon the wings of the wind, consternation in his countenance, his toes four inches above the floor of the porch, the moon exactly in the zenith, the smiling face of a mischievous maiden pressed against a parlor window pane, and the toe of an old man's bare foot just receding back through the half-open doorway. Nothing definite or complete has been said, but who is so stupid that he wants to be told any more? Or, what should be done with an artist that would insult our intelligence by making the picture any plainer? It is infinitely better art to stop with these few suggestive hints and allow each of us to complete the picture out of the fullness of his own experience. An unintelligent artist would continue this or any other picture till he would ruin it by its very completeness. Some, worse yet, not satisfied with one complete expression, will weaken it by undue repetition. Art permits some repetition. There may be a tune within a tune, a story within a story, a play within a play, a picture within a picture; but the tune, story, play, or picture within must always be a reflection or suggestion of the tune, story, play, or picture without. Every piece of art must stop when it is done. On this point, Hopkinson Smith says it requires two men to paint a picture, one to work the brush, and another to kill the artist when he has finished the picture and doesn't know it. It is probably wicked to say it, but I have occasionally thought that some sermons could have been improved by the services of a second man. All art must come to an end, and must not be too long about it. Long speeches are not the most effective ones. I do not recall a single occasion in my life as a public speaker when either I or my audience

was sorry that I did not talk longer; but I can look back with regret upon scores of occasions when both of us should have been happier if I had not talked so long. 

I believe that speakers generally would agree that in both private conversation and public speech, for every single word they now wish they had said and did not, there are at least a hundred words they did say and now wish they had not. It is always better to spend time in getting a few words ready to say than to say a host of words not ready to be said. My destiny, and yours, has hung or will hang on short speeches and shorter answers. Ninety-nine hundredths of all the two-hour lectures ever written could be improved by cutting out of each at least sixty minutes of it. Nearly all sermons that are longer than thirty minutes are too long. Was it not the great English evangelist, Spurgeon, who said that very few souls are saved after the first twenty minutes? If I were to give a rule for preparing a sermon, and it would apply to almost any other kind of address, - it would be: Spend at least six hours in writing a two-hour sermon, and then another hour in cutting it to thirty minutes without omitting anything. 

The same principle applies to writing. The reading public now prefer short stories and short plays to long ones. Very few people just now are hunting for long poems, unless it is the misguided poets themselves. 

Recently one of these long-winded poets asked a longsuffering editor, "What do you think I should get for a poem of a hundred and twenty stanzas of eight lines each?" Back came the answer, "I should say about fourteen years.' All good art, like all other good things, must come to an end. It is only the mediocre that can run on forever.

Note. There is a very prevalent misuse of the word art to signify only painting, or painting and sculpture. Many persons speak of music and art, or literature and art, as if music and literature were not

art.

But they are art, as much as painting is. 

Common as this usage is among undiscriminating speakers, there is nothing to warrant such a restriction of the term art. 

DEFINITION OF ART 

The foregoing should enable us to arrive at a definition of art, or at least to understand one that has been arrived at. Some Frenchman has defined art as, "Thought run through feeling and fixed in form." This is excellent. I would suggest but a slight modification.

Since I regard feeling, rather than thought, as fundamental or dominant in all art, I would make it read, Art is feeling run through thought and fixed in form. Or if in the light of the foregoing discussion I might be allowed to try my own hand at a definition, I should say, Art is the adequate expression in suitable form of a dominant emotion together with its thought-setting.
