CHAPTER III IMPORTANCE OF SPEECH 

We are all agreed, I take it, that speaking is an art, one of the fine arts. It is one of the literary arts. Some might not concede that speech is above writing, but it is certainly in more frequent demand. The climax of all the achievements of the universe down to the present, and we may well believe for all time, is the human mind. 

Its creations are therefore our chief concern. Now speech like writing is an art that communicates the creations or possessions of a mind to other minds. The more we reflect upon this the more are we convinced of the tremendous importance of speech. 

To be able to speak and to understand the speech of others is the greatest of all man's possessions. There is nothing else comparable to it. There is nothing else we would miss so much if deprived of it. Speech does more for us than anything else. Cultivated speech opens more doors to young men and women than any other accomplishment. Art in any form is great. 

Any one with even a tolerable proficiency in any art may justly feel proud, but the man or woman who speaks well is probably admired by more people than the practicer of any other art. Certain it is that any of us, if we might have only one of all the arts, would unhesitatingly choose speech. It is wonderful to be able to sing or play sweet music, to reveal the statue that lies buried in a block of marble, to conceive in all its proportions the image of a temple, or to spread landscapes in color on the canvas, but greater than any or all 50

of these is the art of speech, which not only enables us to express our thoughts to any one who will listen but puts within our reach all the thought of all the ages. 

Every person of normal intelligence feels the need of being a better speaker. Fortunately it is the one art that is within the reach of all of us; and yet, until very recently, it has received but little attention, and is yet receiving far from its share. 

THE FORMS OF SPEECH 

Speech has so many forms that each of us is obliged to use some of them; and many of us a large number of them. Public speaking, which is to have the largest share of our attention in these lessons, is by no means the most important form. A few people make public addresses, engage in public debates and dramatic recitals, while all of us are almost constantly concerned with social conversation, giving or receiving directions, making announcements, buying and selling commodities, teaching our classes, prescribing our medicines, and so on to an almost endless list of the uses of the ordinary or common form of speech. Partly because speech is so universally used, but partly also because it is beautiful in itself when properly used, the art of speech confers upon one the most admirable charm one can come into the possession of.

IMPRESSION AND EXPRESSION 

Speech is man's largest avenue of self-expression. 

While every living thing welcomes impressions, expression is quite as clearly a law of life. One's life is made fuller and richer by the impressions he receives, but one is made stronger by his efforts at expression. The richness and fullness without the strength would be only a handicap and a danger. Down deep in his being every one feels an urge toward self-expression, but most

of us fail to find time, or to take time, to master any art by which one's self can be expressed. Most of the strength any of us has is due to his efforts to organize his impressions and to get them expressed. 

Not only does every one long to express himself, all the rest of us long for him to do it. That is what we want with him. That is what he is for. That is his chief value to us. It is said that man develops speech because he is a social being. That means that we care for one another. In what ways do we care for one another? There may be several, but there are two very large ways. All the others are minor matters. These two large ways are: We want others to express themselves to us, and we want to express ourselves to others. 

Speech is by far the largest means of accomplishing these two ends. It does seem that more of us should care about reducing it to an art. 

Here a mistaken notion is prevalent, that only the great ones make a contribution by expressing themselves. So far as we know, every normal self is worth being expressed. It may be that the life of the greatest of us would be enlarged and enriched by the full expression of the least of us. Of course, there is a difference. 

Some are geniuses, and others are just plain people; but certain it is that mediocrity artfully expressed is a larger contribution than genius clumsily concealed. All education should encourage expression as well as impression, for these two are all there is of education, and the greater of these two, in the judgment of the writer, is expression. The largest, the best, art of doing it, for most of us the only art of doing it, is the art of speaking. 

IMPROVEMENT OF SPEECH 

It is implied above that there is recent improvement in speech. This I think is true; and while part of the

credit is due to the schools, I am inclined to think that the larger share of it must be attributed to the coming of the radio. When an announcer steps before the receiver, he is conscious that thousands, or it may be millions, will hear his message. This naturally makes him pay attention not only to what he says but to the manner in which he says it. He cares. 

He cares. He strives to make the best possible use of his words, his sentences, his emphasis, his pauses, his tone, his pronunciation, his inflection, in short his oral effectiveness. All this excellence is noticed by the thousands and millions who hear. They admire it. They, too, come to care for better speech. Little by little, some of it consciously and some of it unconsciously, the masses of people are making improvement in speech. This will require even more care on the part of the broadcasters, which in turn will improve the rest of us, and we in turn will demand more of the art of speaking in the announcers. At least let us hope that it will work in this way. Let us make it work in this way. 

STEPS OR STAGES IN THE MASTERY OF AN ART 

Everybody knows that conscious activity is less graceful, more awkward, than spontaneous activity. 

Spontaneity is an essential of all art. Many persons, however, have the misconception that this spontaneity comes at the beginning, while as a matter of fact it comes, to all but geniuses, only at the end. It can be attained by most of us only by going over or through certain steps or stages, which it is the purpose of this lesson to set forth.

In all kinds of practice resulting in such a degree of perfection as entitles the activity to be called an art, one must go through practically the same steps or stages. 

There are three of these. As we are at this time concerned chiefly with the art of speech, it will be helpful

to note these steps that must be taken. It will be instructive also to note that these same steps are involved in acquiring the art of standing, sitting, pronouncing words, singing, speaking, or even in so simple an act as walking across the floor or in so complicated a performance as portraying Hamlet or Macbeth by means of acting. They are present in learning to breathe properly, to give or receive an introduction, to eat at a dining table, to drive a car, or to play a musical instrument: (1) Unconscious Error. This is the stage one starts from, the stage one finds oneself in at the beginning. 

It is conceivable that some one might object, by insisting that some persons, geniuses, for example, might do the act perfectly from the beginning. I would answer by saying that if there really are such people, they are not concerned with mastering the art, for they have it already. And we need not be much concerned with them, for most of us are not in their class. I have had more than one student of composition who declined to take seriously the rules of Rhetoric because, they contended, Homer and Shakespeare did not learn to write by rules. Even if they did not, these students were unconsciously taking for granted a minor premise, which may not be valid, that they belong in the same class with Homer and Shakespeare. Let us keep in mind that we are thinking only of those persons who master an art, and the mastery of an art involves going from the wrong way of doing a thing to the right way. 

At the end we shall be doing the thing properly, but we began in error. Not only that, at the very beginning we were unconscious of the fact that we were making a mistake. The first stage may, then, very properly be called the stage of unconscious error. Here it may be objected again, in fact we often hear the objection, that if one will be sure that he sees and hears only the people

who do the thing in question perfectly, he like the geniuses will never be in error. He will unconsciously do the thing right from the start. This is highly probable, but it assumes a condition that is even more rare than geniuses. That is, that one may ever be so fortunate as to associate constantly only with persons that are never in error. Our first stage is, therefore, that in which the learner is doing the thing in the wrong way and does not know that he is making an error. (2) Conscious Correctness. There are only two ways to proceed with art in the first stage: We may abandon the student to his error, and give up altogether our problem of mastering an art. This, of course, is not what we want to do. It is not what he wants us to do. 

The other procedure is to lead him to see the right way. 

To do this, we are obliged to make him aware of his error and conscious of the right way. We must say to him: "Do not walk this way; walk the other way. 

Do not open your mouth in this way; open it the other way.

Do not breathe all the time with the upper part of the chest; make a conscious effort to breathe by using the diaphragm. On a slippery pavement if you must put your foot on the brake, press it down gradually, and be sure that at the same time you do not put your other foot on the clutch. Do not give that sound of the vowel; give this one. Do not use your knife; use a fork or a spoon. Do not mouth your words; speak the speech as I pronounce it to you.'

TWO OBJECTIONS 

Two objections are made to this procedure: Some invoke that old and valuable principle, - Learn to do by doing. This principle is invaluable if it is understood, but misunderstood it will hurt instead of helping. If we mean that if we are in error in performing an act, we can correct the error by more practice of the same kind,

we are mistaken. That would only strengthen and deepen the wrong tendency that is already too strong and too deep. The only way the principle can be used to aid us is by understanding it to mean, - Learn to do by intelligent doing. What do we mean by intelligent doing? We mean doing that proceeds according to a principle, doing that keeps before us the difference between the wrong way and the right way. Then we are agreed.

The other objection is, that no matter what one does, if he does it consciously he does it awkwardly. This is true. Some say, “I would rather have a boy or a girl, a man or a woman, go ahead and make his errors gracefully in all the abandon of ignorance, than to do the thing awkwardly by being conscious of his effort to avoid the error." I am inclined to agree with this.

If we had only these two stages, I believe that in a great majority of cases the people performing in the first would be happier than those performing in the second.

And certainly those of us who watch the two performances would be less miserable with the first than with the second. Then why have this second stage? Because it is necessary, yes, it is indispensable, to connect us with the third. (3) Unconscious Correctness. This stage is art. It is our goal. It is the great achievement. But why not leave out the second altogether and go directly from the first to the third. There is only one objection to that, - it cannot be done. If one is born in this third stage and his surroundings and conditions are such as to make it impossible for him to fall into error, then he is born an artist. He never masters an art. If there is such a person we are not now concerned with him. For every one like him we have at least a billion who begin with the wrong way that at first they are unconscious of being wrong. Our problem is to help these billion move

on to an unconscious right way. And the only possible route is through the conscious right way. When we make it a habit, it will be unconscious. It will cease to be awkward. It will be art. 

APPLICATION TO SPEECH 

The application of all this to speech is too plain even to need setting down. For the one who has no errors in speech or writing all this is a waste of time and space. 

For the correction of errors we are unconscious of, it is the only formula I know. I could tell other ways, but they would not work. This way will work. Try it on your pupils and on yourselves. Every one of us is doing something, it may be many things, pertaining to speech, in the wrong way without suspecting that we are in error. Let us welcome the friend or the book that will show us our error and point out the right way. 

Then let us practice this right way consciously till it becomes a habit. Then it will be unconscious as well as correct. Then we shall have mastered at least a small part of the art of speaking. 

COMMON ERRORS 

It is a common error to think that if we write correctly, it matters little if at all how we speak. It would still be an error, but nearer the truth, to reverse it and say, "If one speaks well, it does not matter how he writes.' One would make a mistake to choose either statement, for both speech and writing are tremendously important, but it is true that the way one speaks is more important than the way he writes. We do not need to go far for the reason, — almost every one speaks oftener than he writes. We should strive earnestly, therefore, to add to our speech every beauty and to eliminate from it every blemish.

Another error, quite as common, is to assume that

99 just so one is careful in his public utterance he need not be much concerned with his manner of speaking in ordinary conversation. Here again we should come much closer to the truth if we should reverse the statement and say, "If we speak well in ordinary conversation, we need not be nearly so much concerned about public addresses.' Here again the reasons are obvious: 

We may not all be public speakers, but every one of us takes part in conversation every day and almost every hour. It is true too that others are inclined to be lenient in overlooking errors in one's public speeches and rather critical of our conversations. Again both are important. If we are to favor either above the other, it should be conversation. No one ever becomes a good conversationalist as a result of his being a good public speaker, but the opposite is generally true. In fact, the best possible way to train for being a good public speaker is to strive to be good (1) in oral reading and (2) in ordinary conversation. Not only in our public efforts but in all our ordinary conversation, let us guard our speech. One by one let us get rid of our errors.

Let us see that every word, every phrase, every sentence says what we intend it to say and sounds as we intend it to sound.

If one wishes an outline for improving his speech, this one would not be bad: (1) See that you grasp clearly the meaning you wish to express, then see that your language is the best possible expression of that meaning. In this, one will be helped by two things. First, the meaning he wishes to convey he must turn over and over in his mind many times before attempting to express it. This is thinking. 

Then he must be certain of the meaning of all the words he uses. Consult your dictionary. Be sure that the words you select convey the meaning you wish to express.

(2) Speak your words and sentences in the best possible way. Look after your enunciation, articulation, pronunciation. (3) When you read or speak, stand in the best possible posture, breathe properly, and speak what you have to say as if it were a matter of importance, and so that it may be heard and understood by all in the room. 

With permission of his publishers, The Macmillan Company, I am closing this chapter with the two paragraphs with which Cross ends his book, Fundamentals in 

English: 

"One who has ability to speak English with fluency and accuracy and simplicity is very fortunate, for he can express his thoughts in speech that will be understood not only in Great Britain and the United States, but almost anywhere in the world. Any one who is interested in being exactly understood, who wishes to avoid being marked by the oddity and crudity of his language, who has an interest in preserving English as a world speech, must learn to speak the words of the language distinctly without blurring his enunciation, to pronounce his words as educated men and women pronounce them, and to combine them in sentences according to the laws and customs followed by English speakers who respect their language.

The English language is a rich inheritance. It is like a rare musical instrument, handed down from generation to generation. It should not be marred by careless usage. If you owned a harp upon which the Saxon bards had once played the accompaniment to Beowulf, you would not willingly desecrate it by playing on it out of tune. Neither should you desecrate the language which has been handed down to you— the instrument of expression which has served Chaucer and Shakespeare and Milton and all the great folk who have used the good English tongue. You should not employ it to

express empty-minded nothings in slovenly sentences with ill-chosen, badly enunciated, and badly pronounced words. You have inherited the English language. It is a simple and beautiful speech, capable of expressing every human thought, the simplest or the most profound. Use it as an instrument of value, and think it a privilege to keep it fine for yourself and to teach others its usefulness and its beauty."

CHAPTER IV THE INTELLECTUAL RESPONSE Speech as Five Responses. The effect of a speech de pends partly upon the posture and the movement of the body and of its parts, particularly the face and hands; it depends partly upon the kind and quality of voice; partly upon the nature of the words and sentences used; and very largely upon the quality of the emotional and thought content expressed. So we may say the total effectiveness of speech is the outcome of a series of five responses. These five responses will abundantly repay us for any amount of study expended upon them. These responses are: I. Intellectual, 2. Emotional, 3. Verbal, 4. Vocal, 5. Physical. try throughout our study to understand what they mean. All expression is of a mental content. The only mental content in the expression of which the speaker is much interested is thought and emotion. Just now we are concerned with thought. No thought is ever delivered to one bodily. It cannot be transferred. It cannot be imparted. It may be had only by thinking. 

This process of thinking that puts one in possession of thought or thoughts is what is meant by intellectual response. 

Let us

Fact and Thought. Any response is always a response to something. An intellectual response is always a response to facts. No thought is possible entirely apart from facts. No speech is worth while unless it expresses thought. Even when its primary purpose is to express emotion, it can do this only through the expression of 61

thought. Every speech must therefore either directly or indirectly deal with facts. If speech cannot in some way be traced to facts, it is up in the air. No one is able to know why it is made. There is no excuse for its existence. Before he starts, it is good for the speaker to note the factual basis of what he is about to say; and occasionally after he starts he should check up on what he is saying to see that it is not too far removed from facts. Facts are first; but they are not final. What most concerns the speaker and the audience is the meaning of facts. Since meaning is thought, it cannot be imparted; it comes only through thinking. In the strictest meaning of the words, thought cannot be expressed. In the strictest meaning of the words nothing can be expressed. That is, the speaker cannot press his thought out of his mind into ours. Then what can he do? He can talk the best he can about the thought that is in his mind but that does not get out, so that we may have thought in our minds. It has not come into our minds, it is constructed in our minds by ourselves. Then of course the speaker is said to express his thought and we are said to get his meaning; but it is worth while to keep in mind that all the meaning one has he makes. After the speaker's explanation we often say, "We get your meaning," but we don't; he keeps his meaning and we make ours. His contribution is to induce us to make our meaning. This he does by talking about his meaning. This is what we mean by the expression of thought. 

Two mistakes are here easily possible. First, the speaker may proceed with so little reference to facts that we do not see what he is driving at and we do not care. 

The affinity for reality is strong in all of us. 

When we are entirely out of reach of facts, we are lost. 

We don't know where the speaker is going and we don't know where he is taking us.

Then we have no interest in the

journey. We decline to embark. Let me say again, every speech should be founded in fact. 

A second mistake quite as common as the first, and no less fatal, is to stop with facts. Indispensable as they are they are not enough to justify much speech. It is difficult to see how a fact is worth more than a fiction until we are permitted to see its meaning. Indeed a fiction that has meaning is invaluable while a fact that has not is worthless. Meaning, thought, understanding is the only pretext for speaking and the only incentive for listening. Certainly we want facts, but only that we may see their meaning. A speaker may have his head full of facts, but if he does not see their meaning, he might as well have them in a barrel, a bucket, or a basket; and if he does not permit us to see their meaning, we wonder what it is all about. Let the speaker first furnish us with the facts, and then let him show us their meaning. This matter of finding meaning is always an intellectual response to facts. An audience, any audience, will sit almost breathless while meaning is being revealed. I think I have never known an audience, large or small, to stir while understanding was being born. Understanding is nothing more than seeing meaning.

A few illustrations of the intellectual response will make it clear. It has already been said that this response is always to a fact. A fact may be anything that is done or that happens. It may be a place, a location, a memory, what some one has said, a bit of history, an act of imagination, a reference in literature, a predicament or a situation the speaker finds himself in. 

And the response itself is the act of giving meaning to this fact, understanding it, thinking it through. It is the organization of the material at one's disposal. 

(a) The White Murder Case: Joseph White was an aged sea captain, eighty-two, of Salem, Mass. He

was a thoroughly good man and very wealthy.

On the morning of April 7, 1830, he was found dead in bed, with thirteen deep dagger wounds and a fractured skull. 

No trace of the murderer could be found. There was no evidence of robbery, his money and goods being all in place. Daniel Webster was employed to assist the prosecution. A statement from a prisoner in jail at 

New Bedford, seventy miles away, led to the arrest of one Crowningsfield as the murderer. Later a man in 

Belfast, Maine, charged Frank Knapp, a distant relative of White by marriage, with having instigated the murder that he might share the dead man's wealth. 

Knapp had been seen on the Salem streets the night of the murder. Crowningsfield, when he was confronted by the man accusing Knapp, committed suicide in his cell. These were the facts from which Webster constructed his theory: Crowningsfield, had committed the deed. Knapp had conspired with him to do it. His motive was money. Webster's presentation was so plausible, his argument so irresistible, and his plea so eloquent and so powerful that the jurors seemed almost to have witnessed the execution. The speech that sent Knapp to the gallows is said to have been one of the greatest ever delivered to a jury in the annals of the bar. Henry 

Cabot Lodge says of it that "it has never been surpassed in dramatic force by any speaker, whether in debate or before a jury." 

(b) A Doctor's Case: Dr. William Stevenson Baer of 

Baltimore, clinical professor of orthopedic surgery at Johns Hopkins University, was surgeon general of the orthopedic section of the American Expeditionary Forces, medical service. He noted these facts: Many a shell-wounded soldier lay unattended in the fields until his sores were alive with maggots. Every one of such unfortunates should have died from blood poisoning. 

But to his surprise and that of everybody, many of these

men made speedy recovery. After pondering over the coincidence for a decade, this was Dr. Baer's intellectual response to the facts observed: Of course, mediæval doctors had applied maggots to festering sores, but this practice had long been discarded as ignorant quackery. But could there not have been after all some reason in it? Might it not be possible that in these war cases the maggots had relieved the patients both by taking into their own bodies some of the poison and by emitting a healing secretion? He thought it over and over. He experimented. He thought through his theory. As the outcome he has treated more than 300 cases of wounds with his maggots. Every one of the children and four out of five of the adults were cured. 

At the time of this writing, I am reliably informed, the Department of Agriculture of the United States government is breeding colonies of maggots to be used by Dr. 

Baer in the healing of stubborn sores. Facts alone are insignificant, but their meaning is marvelous. 

(c) Beecher's Case: It is reported of Henry Ward 

Beecher that once when he was addressing an angry audience, some members of it became so desperate that they threw brickbats at the speaker. One of these fell at his feet. This was a fact, a hard fact, to which he must then and there make some kind of intellectual response, and on the nature of that response hung destiny. What was he to do? That would be determined by what he thought. A less courageous man would have run from the platform, but Beecher was not a coward. He picked up the brick and held it in his hand. A reckless man might have hurled it back at the audience, as they thought he was about to do. That would have been the end of his speech and probably the end of his life. He was too great a man to do that. 

He knew that audience deserved a sound thrashing, and he had made up his mind to give it to them, but not by

physical force. As he held the brick in his hand he said, "Here it is, look at it; this is the only answer my opponents are able to make to my arguments.' 

Very soon the cat-calls, the hoots, and the hisses were transformed into cheers and applause.

For a host of illustrations of the intellectual response one does not have to look far or long: When Charles 

Darwin gathered the facts about animals in relation to their environment, his intellectual response was the principles or laws known as the struggle for existence and the survival of the fittest. Continued reflection upon these in relation to other facts resulted in his hypothesis of evolution.

Thomas Jefferson and his co-workers reflected over the nature of man, his innate demand for freedom as a necessary condition of his life and work, and the intellectual response was The Declaration of Independence. 

Alexander Hamilton, James Madison, and their associates ran through their minds the facts pertaining to government, the necessity of government in its relation to man's love of liberty, and their response was the great scheme of American Democracy as embodied in the Constitution of the United States. 

When the mighty intellect of Isaac Newton was let loose upon the meager facts of the physics, mathematics, and astronomy of his time, its response was the universal law of gravitation and such improved methods of calculation as enabled him to guarantee the movements and the positions of heavenly bodies a million times more accurately than we today can predict the arrival of trains. Laplace meditated over the same and similar facts until his great mind responded with the Nebular Hypothesis, which accounts for the origin of our solar system.

Reflection upon the facts pertaining to the nature of man, the struggle in his life of the two opposing prin-

ciples of good and evil, enabled Dante to respond with the Divine Comedy, and Goethe with Faust. In fact, every principle or law of government, education, morals, science, philosophy, is some mind's intellectual response to facts. This same reflection that gives birth to truth is also the mother of error. Some are not as sure of their facts as they should be and do not grasp all their meaning, and so mistakes are made. Of one thing we may be sure, and that is, the man who best discriminates between fact and fiction and sees most fully, most correctly, and most clearly the meaning of facts is the man everybody is looking for when a speech needs to be made.

PURPOSE AND ATTITUDE 

If one's purpose does not justify a speech, no speech should be made. In the first place, if I may be allowed so to express it, the purpose of a speech should be not subjective but objective. That is, no matter whether he is making a long speech or a short one, a sermon or a sentence, an oration or an answer to a question, to be effective he must aim not at something in himself but at something outside himself. He must be all the time thinking and feeling not that he has a message to give, that it is a great message, that he is giving it in an artistic way, that people will admire him for the way in which he is doing it, and that when he has done it he will himself feel this way or that, and he himself will be such and so. This is all subjective and wrong. Instead he must be all the time thinking and feeling that the persons to whom he speaks need to have a change wrought in them, that they do not see, and his message will help them to see; they do not understand, and it will help them to understand; they do not care, and his problem is to make them care; they are against his cause, and his task is to make them for it; or they are

indifferent to the cause, and his purpose is to have them do something about it. This is all objective and right. 

The subjective attitude makes one self-conscious, artificial, awkward, embarrassed, and unimpressive; the objective attitude makes him natural, independent, forceful, graceful, and at ease. With all the training in the world one is bound to fail if he goes before an audience of one or a thousand feeling that his object and his aim is a result in his own mind; and with no training he is sure to be listened to if he is intent upon the purpose to produce a change in the minds he addresses. Be sure to distinguish clearly the subjective and the objective attitude, and to remember that in all teaching, reading, and speaking, the subjective attitude is wrong and the objective attitude is right. 

The first recorded speech ever made was, Let there be light. This should be the purpose of all speeches. 

Unless one has at least a suspicion that he is in possession of light, he should not speak. His purpose in speaking is to let that light, not himself, shine. If he has the light and can reveal it, he will speak as authority and be heard gladly.

THE ORGANIZATION OF THOUGHT 

It goes without saying that no one can speak well on all subjects, or even on many subjects. The fact that a man is in demand to speak on a certain subject or series of subjects over which he may rightfully claim a degree of mastery is no evidence that we should care to hear him on other subjects that have engaged but little if any of his attention. No one has a right to speak upon a subject unless he has thoughts on that subject. That is important, but even that does not justify one in speaking. It is immensely valuable to have a prolific mind, which will turn out thought in abundance. This contributes largely to the interest

of one's message. Every speech should be full of thoughts. Every sentence should express one of them.

But yet another quality of mind is needed. For a man to be listened to, his mind must be not only prolific but logical. Just thoughts are not enough. A speaker must be able to think thoughts and to think them in order. It is not uncommon to hear speakers whose minds are fertile in turning out a surprising number of thoughts but who nevertheless fail to satisfy us. We admire the wealth of thoughts, we listen with interest at least for a while, but at the end we are not satisfied, we are disappointed. What is the matter? It is this:

We have had many thoughts presented to us, for which we are grateful, but at the end we have little if any better understanding of the subject than at the beginning. Now understanding is what we all want. The lack of understanding is the cause of every question that arises in the mind. Understanding should be the purpose of every answer and of every speech. Here we may remember that by high authority we have been told that understanding is the principal thing and have been admonished with all our getting to get understanding. 

It is a vital mistake to think that any one having thoughts on a subject is entitled to speak on it. In addition to thoughts, understanding is necessary. The purpose of all study is understanding. Any one who speaks is expected to express the result of his study. 

The speaker himself is supposed to have understanding, and his purpose in speaking is to produce understanding in others. Individual thoughts come with a degree of spontaneity; understanding comes only as a result of study. The speaker must keep constantly in mind that his aim is to produce understanding. Just before one opens his mouth to speak he should hear the command, Let there be light, and should remember that that light is understanding.

Only the one who himself has an understanding of a subject can speak so that his listeners will have it. 

We should not underestimate the value of merely having thoughts. It is certainly well worth while. 

Most of us most of the time are unable to have very many individual thoughts. And then without the individual thoughts there is nothing to organize, and so understanding is impossible. We must remember, however, that understanding is the speaker's ultimate aim as well as the listener's most earnest wish. 

Now what do individual thoughts lack? What must be added to them to make understanding? The answer is organization. What is organization? It is order.

To organize thoughts is to order them, that is, to put them in order. In this respect, minds are not all exactly alike. Some seem to care much for individual thoughts, but hardly at all for organization. Others want only the minimum of organization. Still others demand complete organization. Everything must be in its place. This third class is the one the speaker should strive to satisfy in his presentation of a subject. 

If he pleases them he will please all, for listeners that are not orderly in their own thinking admire it in others. The organization of any subject-matter consists in putting in order the thoughts pertaining to it.

This order is by no means an arbitrary one. It is fixed by the sequence in which the normal mind puts its inquiries. In this respect all normal minds are so nearly alike that organization for one is organization for all. If this were not true, it would be futile to speak at all. Our problem is merely to find out the order in which a normal mind makes its inquiries, the order in which it wants its questions answered. If we can but hit upon that order, our presentation will result in light, understanding; if we miss it, the result is mental darkness, confusion.

ORDERING OUR THOUGHTS 

Now let us try to see what we mean by the organization of thought on any subject. Of course, we mean putting our thoughts in order. Well, what order?

Let us keep in mind that a speaker is all the time doing two things, causing questions to arise in the minds of his listeners and supplying or suggesting the answers to these questions. Causing the wrong question to arise, or even the right question at the wrong time, produces an effect similar to striking the wrong key on the piano, or the right key at the wrong time. For the benefit of students I am submitting eight questions in the order in which a normal mind makes its inquiries when studying a subject and in which a listener wishes the answers when a subject is being explained to him. 

This of course cannot be taken as a hard and fast and finished formula, but rather as a tentative scheme that the reader is at liberty to work over into a plan that will be helpful to him. If any one thinks the questions and answers belong in a different order, that does not prove that there is no such thing as the ordering of our thoughts. It proves only that I have mistaken this order, or he has, or we both have. Anyway the outline or scheme I propose for the organization of our thought on any subject we may be investigating or presenting to an audience, be that subject large or small, heavy or light, is comprehended in the answers to the eight questions that follow.

A FORMULA FOR THINKING 

It is well known that thought must precede speech, yet there are many persons ambitious to become good speakers who overlook completely the fact that they must first become good thinkers. While it is not possible to lay down rules that will forthwith transform

thoughtless persons into good thinkers, it is a matter of common knowledge that those who can think can improve their thinking. How this may be done is the object of our inquiry in this lesson. 

It has already been made clear that thought that is to lead to understanding must be organized. Now let us try to see a little more definitely what is meant by the organization of thought. We are not concerned with thought in the abstract, if there is such a thing; we are now caring only about thought on some specific subject, for when one speaks he is supposed to speak on a subject. 

Now, as it seems to me, one's thought on any particular subject may be said to be organized when he has, in order, the answers to eight questions on that subject. 

And when, as a speaker, he is able to give these answers in order, his presentation will lead to an understanding of the subject. If any reader prefers other questions, he is at liberty to substitute them for mine. Or if he thinks they should come in a different order, he will change that also. After considerable attention to the matter, I submit the following as the most suitable questions to ask about a subject if one wishes to arrive at organized thought on that subject: 

I. What is the subject? Sometimes one will be helped greatly in answering this question by asking, What is it not? Anyway, we must first be able to grasp it, take hold of it, see it as a whole. It is good first to sit down in a quiet place and think about it. Turn it over and over many times in the mind. This should always be done with pencil and notebook in hand, and every glimpse of light should be set down. Do not wait. It may not come again. One must define his subject, that is, he must think its boundaries around it, so that he may see clearly where it begins, where it ends, what it includes. Whether or not one begins his speech by expressing his definition is a matter of indi-

vidual preference. Some very effectively lay down the definition and work from it; others just as effectively begin with other facts or principles that lead up to the definition. Let each speaker use the procedure that to him seems best for him on each particular subject and on each particular occasion; but from the very beginning the speaker must have the definition of his subject clearly in his own mind. That is, he must know what he is talking about; otherwise he will find it much more difficult to make his audience know what he is talking about. If his audience do not know what the speaker is talking about, instead of trying to follow him, they will spend their energy in wondering why he is talking at all. They will not be kept in the dark very long; so, very early in one's speech one should let his hearers know what he is talking about. One gets this conception of his subject by thinking about it and continuing to think about it until it begins to look not altogether unfamiliar. Many times a subject that at first is as black as midnight becomes infinitely significant after a period of real study. Of course, if one cannot, by thinking, get any kind of a conception of his subject, then one must read about it; but it is better always to read after a period of thinking. Then, too, one must think after a period of reading. Reading, in itself, is never valuable, never final; it is only for the purpose of stimulating thought. Some one may ask, "Do we not sometimes read for information?" Yes, but information is worth no more than misinformation except that it is a better aid to our thinking. Students of speech cannot be too often reminded that a head full of facts not thought about would be more helpful to one if he had them in a bag thrown over his shoulder. In the process of getting hold of a subject, finding out what it is, thought must be both the beginning and the end. 

So far we have come upon no art to enable one to

present clearly what is hazy in his own mind. Clearness comes only from thinking, and then more thinking. 

II. What does it belong under? What is it a part of? 

What brought it about? What caused it? Of what is it a sequence? Of what larger subject is it a part? 

Out of what condition or conditions did it arise? 

What are its antecedents? How has it come to be? 

What is its history? Here I prefer to list several questions, because in some investigations one question and in other investigations another question will best bring out the relation wanted; and yet in other investigations one might find himself in need of the answers to all these questions. This second step is the reference of the subject in hand to something above or beyond it. 

The grasp of this relation is necessary to the understanding of any subject one talks or thinks about, and in my thinking it seems to be next in order after grasping the meaning of the subject. 

III. What other subjects belong under the same head?

That is, what subjects or parts are coördinate with the one under consideration? What other subjects are in the same class with it? Just as our second question led us to look up, this one leads us to look out. 

IV. What is the relation of each of these coördinate subjects to the subject in hand? This question helps us to grasp additional relations by first looking out from and then back to the subject in hand. It must be kept in mind that we understand a subject only when we see it in all its relations. The only way to grasp a new relation is to take a different view of our subject. 

These eight questions are intended to enable us to take all possible views of a subject and therefore to see it in all its relations.

V. What is the specific relation (or relations) of the subject in hand to the broader subject under which it belongs? This fifth question continues the inquiry of

the second. The real purpose of the second is the fifth. 

VI. What belongs under it? That is, what subjects or facts belong under the subject in hand? Just as the second question directed us to look up and the third to look out, this sixth question lets us look down; and just as the purpose of the third question is the fourth, so the purpose of this sixth question is the seventh.

VII. How are these divisions related to one another; and particularly how is each related to the subject in hand?

VIII. What is the bearing of the whole discussion upon the conclusion to be established? The purpose of the speech will determine what relations are to leave less, and what more, emphasis. The speaker is fighting a battle. His contention is the conclusion he wants to uphold. The relations in which it may be viewed are his forces. He, the general, will marshal them so that they may strike the hardest blow.

Now, valuable as I think this scheme or formula may be to a speaker who has not yet mastered the art of thinking, it would be better for him to throw it all away at the very beginning than to think that it must be followed slavishly in every case, without deviation.

Subjects may come before us in the explanation of which one or more of these eight steps seem superfluous. 

In such cases it would be foolish to strain ourselves and weary our audiences by an effort to follow the formula. 

And if any student, after a careful examination of it - I am sure it is worth that — decides that he can think better without it, I am here and now releasing him from all obligation to use it. But for the many who realize the importance of order and have not yet learned how to organize their thought, I commend very strongly this outline or something like it.

An application - Suppose, for illustration, that the subject under consideration is Literature. 

(1) Tell what Literature is. What it is in a broad or general sense, and what it is in its more restricted meaning. In which of these senses is it one of the fine arts? If this is the way in which one wishes to discuss it, then 

(2) Fine arts is the answer to No. 2. 

(3) The answer to No. 3 is given by naming the other fine arts architecture, sculpture, painting, music. (4) Under number four one has an opportunity to show how Literature is related to, that is, how it influences and is influenced by, each of the other fine arts. 

Or if one wishes also to consider Literature as one of the branches of education, then one would have an opportunity to show how it influences and is influenced by each of the other branches of study, as well as by industry, commerce, invention, religion, and life. One can show how Literature has helped to mold or give setting to individual lives, and how in turn the particular mold or setting of individual lives has made their expression in literary form inevitable. Here illustrations would be in place. They are to be found in abundance and are exceedingly interesting and instructive. (5) Under (5) one would want to show that the elements indispensable in Art - thought, emotion, form, technic, - are all present in Literature. Some thinkers, owing to their particular attitude or inclination, would regard Literature as the greatest of the arts. They would then be permitted to give their reasons for this view. (6) The answer to number six might be poetry and prose, which would be further divided into drama, novel, short story, letter, oration, etc. (7) Number seven would be answered by comparing these different forms of Literature and showing their relative values.

(8) The answer to number eight will depend upon the purpose of the discussion. If it is to show the value of Literature for Life, then all the points bearing on this conclusion will be marshalled toward this end. Or they could be so set forth as to show the value of Life for 

Literature. 

The same series of questions, or very similar ones, might be answered in the discussion of Hamlet, The Tariff, The Constitution of the United States, The Demo cratic Party, The Gulf Stream, Evolution, The Kel logg-Briand Treaty, The Conjunctive Adverb, The 

Fourth Dimension of Space, or The Snakes of Iceland. 

It must be kept in mind that the formula is proposed only as a help for those who need it in organizing their thought on a subject. Experienced thinkers are not expected to make any change in any scheme of organization that has proved satisfactory for them. And even beginners will certainly not subject any topic to undue strain to make it conform to this formula. The author does believe, however, that the order of investigation suggested by these eight questions will be found helpful to most persons in the organization of their thought on most subjects. Give it a trial. It cannot hurt; it may help. It will not be difficult to drop it when something better appears.

EXERCISES 

1. Upon what does the effectiveness of any speech depend? 

2. Name the five responses that constitute every speech. 

Explain and illustrate each. 

3. Discuss facts in their relation to speech. State and illustrate the two mistakes that are possible with reference to facts. Which of these mistakes are you more likely to make? 

What directions would you give for avoiding both these mistakes? 

4. Give your own illustrations of the intellectual response: (a) from history; (b) from government; (c) from law; (d) from

science; (e) from economics; (f) from agriculture;

(g) from education. 

5. What would you say about a speech in relation to its purpose? 

6. Name and distinguish clearly the two mental attitudes possible for a speaker. Discuss their comparative value. 

7. What is the purpose of a speech with reference to light? 

8. What two things must one be in possession of to be entitled to speak on a subject? Distinguish thoughts and their organization. What is meant by the organization of our thoughts? For most persons, which is more difficult, having individual thoughts or getting them organized? Explain what is meant by the organization of thought. Discuss it in its relation to understanding. 

9. What is the relation of speaking to thinking? 

10. When one is striving to understand a subject, what is the first question one wishes to have answered? Discuss this question and its answer. What is the relation or relations of reading, thinking, and speaking? 

II. Discuss each of the seven other questions. What is your own opinion as to the order in which the speaker should answer them? 

12. Discuss the applications given in the text. What is your opinion of the formula? 

13. Just to give it a trial, make your own applications of it to subjects in (a) Politics, (b) Education, (c) the 1931 Depression, 

(d) Literature, (e) Religion.

CHAPTER V THE EMOTIONAL RESPONSE 

THE intellectual response, indispensable as it is, is not speech. It is only a part of the preparation for speech. 

The same is true of the emotional response. It is another part, a very necessary part, of the preparation for speech. The two are very different in their nature, -one is thought and the other is feeling. They differ also in another respect. Thought is an indispensable background of all speech, while feeling is an element in only some of it. Most mathematical, scientific, and historical explanations are clearer without feeling or at most with only a minimum of it. But wherever feeling is a part of the mental content to be expressed, the emotional response is necessary. Speech in the expression of pure thought is a useful art; in the expression of thought mingled with feeling, speech is a fine art. 

Now everybody knows that the speaker who expresses not only the thought but also the emotion that goes along with it is much more effective than the other speaker who expresses only the thought. The difference between the two is accounted for by the presence in the one case and the absence in the other of the emotional response.

This emotional response seems to be natural for little children, but for us older persons it requires training and practice, the older we are the more training and the more practice. No matter how expressive of emotion children may naturally be, we all go through a period of life in which our conventionalized civilization 79

requires us to inhibit or suppress many feelings. This is of course as it should be; but it unmakes or undoes a mental attitude that later must be remade or done over again before we can hope to be effective speakers. 

Most of the training we have to go through with to awaken this emotional response is to counteract the training we once had to go through with to inhibit this same emotional response. It looks as if this waste of energy should in some way be prevented; but here and now it is in place only to insist that nearly all speaking that is forceful involves full, free, spontaneous emotional response.

It must be kept in mind that emotion never occurs entirely apart from thought. Still another connection must be noted. Emotion is closely related also to corresponding physiological changes and physical movements. The word emotion literally means action, a moving out. In a state of emotion one's mind is stirred up. Not only so, this moving out or stirred up state is in the body also. Anger, love, hate, joy, sorrow go along with corresponding changes in the countenance. They show in the eye. In anger one's breathing is more rapid, his muscles become tense, his fists are clenched, and his face flushed. In fear one is pale, his limbs tremble, sometimes the muscles are paralyzed, and it is recorded that people and animals have been so frightened that the vital processes permanently ceased. So the old expression, "scared to death," though in most cases a gross exaggeration, may sometimes be literally true. In embarrassment the cheeks are flushed, the speech is inarticulate, and the physical movements lose their coördination. In emotions of grandeur and sublimity the whole organism feels elevated. And in nearly all emotion, along with these physiological changes there goes an inclination to perform some physical movement.

The Emotional Response 

81 In cases of emotion, therefore, we note the presence of three closely related things, thought, physiological and physical changes, and the emotion itself. Not all psychologists are yet sure as to the order in which these three belong. The old psychology was certain that they belong in this order: (1) the thought, (2) the emotion, (3) the physiological and physical changes. More recently we have been taught that they belong in this order: (1) the thought, (2) the physiological and physical changes, (3) the emotion. The writer is strongly inclined to think that (2) and (3) may each be either the cause or the effect of the other, that one not only runs because he is scared but that he is still more scared because he runs; that not only does the passing of fear enable one to whistle but the whistling helps the fear to pass. Anyway, while we are awaiting a definite answer from the psychologists, we students of speech may make the fullest and freest use possible of both thought and physiological and physical movements to aid in emotional response. For illustration, if the old theory should turn out to have some truth in it, that one sometimes runs because he is afraid, then one must control his emotion, for he dare not run; and if the newer theory should turn out to be true a part of the time, that one is afraid because he runs, then his problem is how to get his legs under better control, for he must not be afraid. Or again, if one whistles because the fear is passing, then he must direct his attention to the control of the emotion; but if the fear passes because he whistles, then no man's education is complete till he learns to whistle. The point, if it could only get itself expressed, is that the speaker must make use of every means to induce the emotional responses. 

If clearer and more definite thought leads directly to the emotion, one must strive to think more clearly and more definitely. If on the other hand the emotion is

the immediate sequence of bodily changes and movements, then he must learn how to induce these changes and to make these movements, and how also to inhibit them.

It is easy to illustrate the difference between speech that expresses pure thought and emotional speech. "A straight line is the shortest distance between two points," "Pittsburgh is more than 400 miles east of 

Indianapolis," and "The earth is larger than the moon," are statements of fact to which there is only a minimum, if any, emotional response; yet mathematical relations, great distances, and great masses can be so thought as to fill the mind with emotion. 

From the facts of history Longfellow pondered long the thought of the long and severe winter in the northland, of the extreme suffering of the Indians of Hiawatha's tribe, of the impossibility of finding any kind of game for food, of the fatal fever that accompanied the cold. All these conditions and their consequences he reconstructed in his imagination. Then when he came to express his conception together with its emotional response, he gave us the Famine in "Hiawatha." 

A resourceful memory and a sympathetic imagination are powerful instruments in inducing emotional response. Also, the temperament of the speaker has much to do with it. When Lincoln stood on the field of Gettysburg, Nov. 19, 1863, remembered the years of bloodshed and suffering, looked down upon the long rows of soldiers' graves, and pictured in imagination the deeds that had been done upon that field, he gave us the Gettysburg Address, acknowledged to be one of the three greatest American orations. Although the briefest great oration ever delivered, it is so full of emotional thought that even yet it brings tears to the eyes of any sympathetic reader.

The Emotional Response 

83 Henry W. Grady remembered the beginning of the 

Civil War when he was a child of ten, and his crushed life when a little later the dead body of his father was brought home from the battlefield. He remembered the happy home in which his early childhood had been spent and its destruction in the war that followed. 

In his imagination he saw the same fate of thousands of other homes and pictured what he hoped and dreamed his southland might again become. The outcome of all of it, The New South, is another great American oration, full of feeling, that did more to dispel the bitterness of war than anything else ever said or written, excepting only The Gettysburg Address. 

Ingersol's Vision of War and Vision of Peace is another marvelous oration based upon events of the same great war. It also is so filled with feeling that it melts the stoutest hearts and it too sprang from the marvelous memory and the vivid imagination of a great personality.

A speaker without memory and imagination may tell off a few dead facts, but he will not touch any one else with feeling, because he himself is not touched. And then all the memory and all the imagination in the world apart from a great personality, if such a thing were possible, would never touch an audience. All three of these men were masterful personalities, and each spoke from the wealth of his own experience. 

Each as he spoke was full of emotion, and so he filled with emotion those who heard and even ourselves as we read. One who hopes not only to instruct his audience but also to move them must lose no opportunity to strengthen his personality, enrich his experiences, develop his memory and imagination, and make his emotions responsive. Instruction is never final. 

The ultimate end of all speaking is to move people, to have them do something they would not otherwise

have done or refrain from doing what otherwise they would have done. Only speech that springs from feeling and leads to feeling can accomplish this result. So far as possible, let every one ambitious to be an effective speaker master his emotional response. 

Just one caution: Any attempt to express a feeling that is not genuine is not only futile but disgusting. 

It does cause the audience to feel, but not as the speaker would have them feel. Audiences large or small hate shams. They love sincerity. They soon tire of exaggeration. They abominate affectation. They are moved by feeling, but they are moved in the right direction only by the artistic expression of feeling that is not feigned. One who has made his emotions responsive to the touch of this thought has taken a long step in the direction of mastering the art of speaking. 

EXERCISES 

1. What is meant by emotional response? 

2. To what do our emotions respond? 

3. What kinds of thinking are free or almost free from emotion? 

What other kinds are mingled with feeling? Cite illustrations. 

4. To what extent is the presence or absence of feeling in thought due to difference in personality? Illustrate. To what extent is emotion in our thinking under our control? In this respect how do we differ from children? 

5. What is the literal meaning of the word emotion? How does this literal meaning manifest itself in the expression of emotional states of mind? To what extent may emotion modify one's organic or physical movements? 

6. Of the three things, thought, physiological or physical processes, and emotion, what is the normal order? What difference does this make? 

7. What do memory and imagination have to do with the emotional response? Illustrate. What training would you recommend to develop emotional response? Is it possible to go too far in this direction? Discuss inhibition of feeling. What educational subjects would you recommend to develop emotional response?

CHAPTER VI THE VERBAL RESPONSE 

We have already noted that both the intellectual and the emotional response belong under the preparation for speaking. The verbal response, the language one uses, is a very important part of the speech itself. 

Not all of it, for the speech includes also the voice in which the language is spoken and the bodily positions and movements that accompany the delivery, that are a part of the delivery.

By the verbal response we are to understand the words, phrases, sentences of a speech together with the method or manner of speaking them. 

Under this head the first requirement is that the language of the speaker shall be grammatically correct. 

True enough, even experienced speakers make occasional slips. Toward these the rest of us should be very lenient, at least not too critical, for the next slips may be ours. English grammar is a rather difficult subject, but intensely interesting to one who has been properly introduced to it. Even a tolerable mastery of it requires study and painstaking effort, but any one at all interested in the subject will be abundantly repaid for his time and labor. A mastery of the subject, however, is not necessary to enable one to speak and write with a fair degree of correctness. 

To do this it is necessary to master only a few principles, not more than eighteen or twenty, together with their applications. One going about this in earnest can do it in a very short time, but it must be done. 

One of course will lose no opportunity to improve his own speech by reading well-written books and listening 85

to the speeches of men and women supposed to be models, but in addition to this he must master the principles also and note their application. No one can speak correctly and with assurance that he is speaking correctly merely by imitation. If a question ever arises, it cannot be answered merely by saying that Mr. So and So says it in that way. It is answered only when the form in question is lined up with the principle involved. In saying this I am of course not unmindful of the fact that the principles themselves are only the expression of the best usage. The principles are set down by grammarians as the masters of usage illustrate them. This record, grammar, is for the benefit of those who are neither masters of usage nor grammarians. For the benefit of students who need to be reminded and at the risk of giving pain to those who do not, I am setting down in the next paragraph a few of the grossest of errors. Any one who wishes may pass over without reading the ones most displeasing to him. The reader will not be much benefited by merely correcting each error, but will find it very helpful to state clearly and correctly the principle involved in each case and show just how each incorrect form violates this principle and how the correct form squares with it:

I. He set on a bench and scolded the dog that was laying in the sun. 

2. Some one of the men know Mary and I. 

3. He don't intend we boys to know it, but between you and I she taken* it. 

4. In fact she taken* it several times before I had took it at all. 

5. We written* him often before he had ever wrote us once.

These forms are uncommon, but I personally know of communities in three different states where they are often heard.

6. I think I should know for he set between Tom and I when he seen my paper. 

7. You men do not expect much of we women. 

8. Us older people are all sure of it. 

9. She is the girl whom I thought was in your seat. 

10. This boy thinks he speaks English correctly but it is clear to all of we older people that he don't. 

II. I had did it before he seen me. 

12. He had saw me before I done it. 

13. The law in the different states protect the people. 

14. The roads in this particular locality is considerable improved. 

15. He don't know what she said, and she don't know what he said. 

16. When I seen John coming, I said, It is me. 

17. He is a good batter, but he runs too slow. 

18. It is no use to discuss it, we have already did the work. 

19. You better prepare more, for you are not near so good a speaker as her. 

20. He is up to Aunt Mary's; he don't know when he will be back. 

21. He went like he come. 

22. Can I eat this piece of pie? 

23. I will hand you the book later. 

24. Discuss: Shall she come in? Will she come in? 

25. The size of our congregations vary. 

26. Yesterday she run home. 

27. Those kind always annoy me. 

28. She is the girl who I believed told we students about Henry and I. 

29. He whom I thought was in the garden was in the barn. 

30. You hadn't ought to believe all you hear. 

31. To safely cross the river in that place requires good rowing.

32. I will either engage you by the day or by the piece. 

33. As I have ran a long distance, I will lay down for an hour. 

34. I wish every person in the room to leave their reports. 

35. If anybody don't like my answers they may give their own. 

36. John and him wanted Lucy and I to go. 

37. He thought it was me. 

38. He thought them to be Mary and I. 

39. She and her brother was mistaken for John and I. 

40. The new teacher was thought to be I.

RULES OR PRINCIPLES 

Here for the convenience of those who have lost or mislaid their grammars I am setting down in simple form the rules or principles that will correct all the errors listed above. Let the students apply the proper principle to the correction of each error. Please keep in mind that the chief object of this exercise is not to correct these few errors, but to establish the habit of avoiding errors. 

1. The subject of a finite verb is in the nominative case. 

2. The subject of an infinitive, when not in any other construction, is in the objective case. 

3. The subject of any participle that is used substantively is in the possessive case. (We object to his remaining.) 

4. The object of a transitive verb is in the objective case. 

5. The object of a preposition is in the objective case. 

6. A copulative verb, like am, is, are, be, was, were, been, to be, takes the same case after it as before it. 

7. A singular subject requires a singular verb, and a plural subject a plural verb. This applies also to contracted verb forms. 

8. The past indicative form of a verb cannot be used with an auxiliary verb before it; and the perfect participial form of a verb cannot be used without an auxiliary verb before it. 

9. Careful speakers do not separate an infinitive from its sign to by inserting an adverb except when necessary. 

10. Adjectives must have the adjective form, and adverbs the adverbial form.

II. Such verbs as lie and sit, in any of their forms, are intransitive and must not be given objects; while such verbs as lay and set, in any of their forms, are transitive.

12. A pronoun must agree with its antecedent in person, number, and gender.

This brief list of speech errors is intended to be only suggestive. Students working either individually or as a class may enlarge it, discussing each error as directed above. The principles or rules listed will cover 95 per cent of all grammatical errors. 

An excellent rule for every student to follow is, Be very exacting with yourself and very lenient with others. 

The second requirement is that every word used should express the meaning intended. Every speaker, in addition to observing carefully and thoughtfully the way others use their words, lives and works close to his dictionary. It is a rare occurrence for any one to spend five minutes with his dictionary without being instructed. A good dictionary is a very interesting book to read, an invaluable book to consult, an everpresent help in time of trouble. In addition to his dictionary, every speaker will have a book of synonyms and antonyms. Roget's Thesaurus is excellent. It will be found invaluable in making an intensive study of the words and phrases of at least a half dozen of the student's favorite speakers and writers.

Now instead of more specific directions, it will be better to leave the matter of language helps to the individual preference of different students, with this one word that no student can afford to deny himself any reference he regards as helpful. Books cost money but they are worth more than they cost. As a rule we appreciate them more when we make our own selections. In spite of this I must insist that every discriminating speaker shall provide himself first with a good dictionary, second with Hartrampf's Vocabularies, and third with collections of great orations and masterpieces of literature. The best collection of American orations is found in Shaw's History of American Oratory

published by The Bobbs-Merrill Company. For all readers and students of speech, I have never found, in any single volume, a collection of material equal to that in William J. Farma's Prose, Poetry, and Drama for Oral Interpretation, published by Harper and 

Brothers. 

The third requirement is that all the words, phrases, and sentences a speaker uses shall have in themselves and in the different combinations the maximum of clearness, force, and beauty. This is an easy assignment, but a very difficult task. The first thing here is that the speaker shall decide upon the thoughts he wishes to express. Then he must turn them over and over in his own mind to make sure that he himself understands them, that they are clear and correct. 

Then he must turn them over several additional times to see that they are in order. Many times a speaker will discover that his thought series will be more forceful if the third thought in it and the seventh are transposed, if the thought he now has first were put last, or if the thirteenth were omitted altogether. Then when he is satisfied with his thought, he must look to its expression. Here again it is easy to think some are born so that artistic expression is natural and therefore easy; but when we come to understand it we see that most of the difference in people in this respect is measurable in work. Most of us make one attempt at expressing a thought or series of thoughts and that is the end of it. The real artist starts as we start and in many cases his first attempt is no better than ours. 

Here is the difference, · - we are satisfied with mediocre results in expression, or if we are not satisfied, we think the improvement possible would not justify further effort, while the artist does not allow himself to rest until his expression is the best he can make it. Nearly all the great artists in expression who have taken time

to enlighten us as to their methods have confessed that their excellence was the result of tireless effort. As a rule, the difference between the commonplace and art is work. The work is not always done at the very time the art is created; but if it is not done then, we may be sure it had been done previously. 

An illustration or two will make it clear that the same conception may be given either a commonplace or an artistic expression. The two may be equal so far as their correctness is concerned, but they are worldwide apart in beauty. I have myself noticed that birds are more likely to choose their mates in the spring, and that the sentiments of young people are noticeably stronger in the spring, but my expression of this truth is not likely to live. It certainly contains no trace of art. The scientist has expressed the same truth: "In the spring the chief activating gland of the kinetic system, the thyroid, shows distinct enlargement."

This impresses us as being more scientific than my common words, but it is not art. Mr. Tennyson knew what I know about it and possibly what the scientist knows. Then in addition to that he ran it through his beauty mold and it came out art: "In the spring a young man's fancy lightly turns to thoughts of love." 

Again, any of us can think of night, death, and sleep; but only the poet ever thinks of death being the brother, and sleep the child, of night. Not many of us ever thought of night as walking over the wave or as being hidden away in a cave. Only a poet would ever think of the hair of night, or of her kisses, as blinding the eyes of day. It is the witchery of the poet's imagery that transforms the prosaic commonplace into literature that illustrates the complete verbal response. In this connection let us enjoy again Percy Bysshe Shelley's apostrophe

TO NIGHT 

Swiftly walk o'er the western wave,

Spirit of Night! 

Out of the misty eastern cave,

Where all the long and lone daylight 

Thou wovest dreams of joy and fear, Which make thee terrible and dear, Swift be thy flight! 

Wrap thy form in a mantle gray, Star-inwrought! 

Blind with thine hair the eyes of Day; 

Kiss her until she be wearied out; 

Then wander o'er city, and sea, and land,

Touching all with thine opiate wand 

Come, long-sought!

When I arose and saw the dawn, I sighed for thee;

When light rode high, and the dew was gone, And the moon lay heavy on flower and tree, And the weary Day turned to his rest, Lingering like an unloved guest, I sighed for thee. 

Thy brother Death came, and cried, Wouldst thou me? 

Thy sweet child, Sleep, the filmy-eyed, Murmured like a noontide bee, Shall I nestle near thy side? 

Wouldst thou me? - and I replied, No, not thee! 

Death will come when thou art dead, Soon, too soon; 

Sleep will come when thou art fled; 

Of neither would I ask the boon 

I ask of thee, beloved Night, Swift be thine approaching flight, Come soon, soon!

Another illustration or two. Whether one's language is commonplace or literature is partly a matter of thought and also partly a matter of imagination. 

There must be thought on a subject that gets close to our experience. Then in the expression of this thought, imagery must be so woven into it that pictures appear. 

The result is such a combination of thought and feeling that its expression is literature, art. This is complete verbal response. The difference between a commonplace expression and a piece of literature is determined partly by the one who works on it and partly also by the amount of work he does. For illustration, any one could say, I myself could say: "Death is a mystery we cannot make out. When we are dead, we might just as well be turned over to nature. There is nothing the living can do for the dead. If they will, they may remember us; if not, they may forget us. When we are dead we shall not see and hear the things we now see and hear. Indeed we do not know whether we shall be able to remember or not when we are dead. We may, and we may not." This expresses clearly enough one's thought on the subject, but it is not literature. 

What does it lack of being literature? It lacks the pictures, the imagery, of an artist. It will be easier to tell more specifically what it lacks when we have read 

SONG By Christina G. Rossetti 

When I am dead, my dearest, Sing no sad songs for me; 

Plant thou no roses at my head, Nor shady cypress-tree: 

Be the green grass above me 

With showers and dewdrops wet; 

And if thou wilt, remember, And if thou wilt, forget.

I shall not see the shadows, I shall not feel the rain; 

I shall not hear the nightingale 

Sing on, as if in pain : 

And dreaming through the twilight 

That doth not rise or set, Haply I may remember 

And haply I may forget.

THE SPEED OF SPEECH 

We are living in an age of speed. Everybody and everything is in a hurry. Everything to be done must be done quickly. Within certain limits this general rush applies to speech. Audiences want all that the speaker knows, but they want it in a hurry. They do not want to wait. They do not want to waste any time, although they may not do anything with the portion of it that they save. How rapidly must a speaker talk? This will depend partly upon the kind of thinking. If it is technical, difficult, and deep, deliberation is necessary. If it is light, interesting, popular, and easy to follow, it need not be expressed with the deliberation of a mathematical demonstration. 

Most of the very rapid speakers I have heard have material that is not difficult to grasp. In such cases the audiences welcome it as fast as it can come. They enjoy being kept busy. Here a greater fault than rapid speaking would be giving easy material so slowly that the audience would have time to go to sleep between thoughts.

Then the size of the auditorium has a good deal to do with it. If it is very, very large, more time is required between words. Roscoe Conkling before an audience of fifteen thousand, before the time of megaphones, spoke so slowly that every word was heard distinctly even in the most remote corners of the room. Every

one was pleased. This was the only way to make everybody hear; but his great deliberation would have been objectionable before an audience of a hundred or even fifteen hundred. Also a speaker's manner of speaking has much to do with it. If his enunciation is good, he may speak much more rapidly. If we are obliged to guess at most of his words, he must allow us time for the guessing. Clean-cut, clear enunciation is an invaluable asset to any speaker. In most cases one may speak as rapidly as he can enunciate. 

While we all want things to be moving and to keep moving, it is possible of course to speak too rapidly. 

The value of a speech is more likely to be in inverse than in direct proportion to the number of words it contains. Some persons think more rapidly than others. A rule safe for most of us is that one may speak as rapidly as he thinks, provided only that he thinks clearly and gives us the best expression of that thought. A speaker is never at liberty to try to put before an audience conceptions not clearly thought through or to dump upon it a host of words not used with discrimination. It is easy for a speaker who has the speed craze to fool himself into thinking that he speaks rapidly because he thinks rapidly. This may be true, but occasionally the opposite is true. It is not easy for one to change the speed of his speech after it has become well established.
