A speaker friend of mine whose mind does work with exceeding rapidity was told repeatedly by his wife that he spoke too rapidly. He admitted it, and promised to slow down; but she was unable to see that her instruction had any effect. If any change were noticeable, he seemed to be speeding up. One evening after she had listened to one of his lightning-like addresses, she came to him with a clipping from the funny column of the Literary Digest. 

She pretended not to understand it and asked him to

explain it to her. It read: "Scientists have discovered that when the tongue is making forty thousand revolutions a minute the brain is in neutral." He did not tell me whether he succeeded in making its meaning clear to her, but he assured me that he understood it and that it would cure him. However, when I heard him opinion that he needed at 

One should always speak speak again, I was of the least one more treatment. with animation. Indolent drawling is never in order.

Nor is speech to be thought of as a contest for the largest possible number of words in the shortest possible time. A speaker's purpose is to express his thoughts and feelings so that if possible his audience will think and feel as he does.

EXERCISES 

1. What is meant by the verbal response? To what is it a response? Who makes it? 

2. What is the first requirement of the verbal response?

Discuss it. Give illustrations. Discuss the forty sentences listed. Which of them, if any, are without error? Correct all the errors in the others, stating clearly what principles of usage are violated. What additional errors have you observed in speakers? Correct them, stating clearly the principle each violates. What injury do you think grammatical errors do to a speech? 

3. State the second requirement of the verbal response? 

What will aid us most in complying with this requirement? Illustrate. 

4. What is the third requirement? What will aid one in meeting this requirement? 

5. Make your own choice (not from the text) of three prose selections distinguished by the force, clearness, and beauty of their words. 

6. Make four such selections of poetry. Point out the quality or qualities in which you think each of these selections excels. 

7. What is the value for speech of the study of such selections?

8. What direction would you give for the speed of speech? 

9. How could speaking err by being too rapid? By being too slow? 

10. What cure would you recommend for speaking too slowly? 

For speaking too rapidly?

II. What rule would you give for governing the speed of speech?

CHAPTER VII THE VERBAL RESPONSE: BUILDING A VOCABULARY 

99 SECOND only to his ideas and thoughts are the words at a speaker's command. Without symbols to express them, ideas and thoughts are worthless to all except the one who possesses them, and it is yet an open question in psychology whether without symbols to express them it is possible for one to have ideas and thoughts. The building of a vocabulary is therefore of inestimable importance to every speaker. I write "building advisedly, for a vocabulary comes about as a building does, not all at once but a little at a time and with all portions in proper order. Just as I am writing this chapter on words there comes to my attention, through the New York Times, a section of the annual report of 

Dr. Henry S. Pritchett, president of the Carnegie

Foundation for the Advancement of Teaching, which touches the same subject. Along with much other thought-provoking material, the report says that in his four years in college the student forgets almost as much as he learns, and that particularly with reference to words the college senior is but little if any wiser than the freshman. The report says, "The college senior recognizes only sixty-one out of one hundred words in familiar use by educated persons." It continues, “The effect of college on the word supply of the ordinary student appears to be almost negligible and in some cases positively injurious." More specifically the report describes one college senior as ignorant of the meaning of inert, lenient, baffle, and immerse; and he thought culpable meant tender, declivity meant climate; 98

: Building a Vocabulary 99 

demure meant abject; benighted meant weary; and spurious meant foamy. I should like to think this representation to be a gross exaggeration; but knowing something of the care with which such reports are prepared and of the men who prepare them, I am inclined to accept it as valid. My inclination in this direction is strengthened when I read further that the report was based upon the examination of 10,000 college students. 

VOCABULARY AND EDUCATION 

This should make us think. Something is wrong. 

Ideas and words, thoughts and sentences, are inseparably connected. If the purpose of going to college is not to have ideas and thoughts awakened in our minds, what is it? If this is done for the students, their vocabularies will show it. If they do not show it, it will not be long till some other report will raise the question. 

Why the college? Certainly the blame cannot all be placed upon the student. Of course, he may have wrong ideas, wrong standards of value; but it is our business to see that he gets right ones. 

If there is anything wrong in our methods, and right methods would not bring these results, - let us find out what it is and correct it. There is no such thing, there can be no such thing, as an educated person without a vocabulary. 

The teaching of courses in college and high school, and the taking of these courses by the student, is futile if it does not result in ideas and thoughts. Of the presence of ideas and thoughts there is only one test, — words and sentences. Of the existence of ideas and thoughts there is only one evidence, words and sentences. 

Let us do our best to build our vocabularies. 

METHODS OF IMPROVING VOCABULARY 

There is no particular time better than any other for beginning this vocabulary building, except, of course,

that the present is always better than the future. 

Every one of us should begin today and keep at it. If one will add to his vocabulary but a few new words every week, it will not be long till improvement is noticeable. The process should proceed orderly; that is, every word or phrase newly mastered should be thought of in connection with the subject in the development of which this word or phrase will be needed. 

Now, how may the enlargement or enrichment of one's vocabulary come about? In more than one way. 

Here I am designating only three of these ways, and without attempting to determine which of the three is best. Indeed, this cannot be done. Some speakers have profited most by the first, some by the second, others by the third. And if any particular student comes upon a fourth, fifth, or sixth way that is best for him, then for this student that way is best. (1) I mention first the use of the dictionary. If a speaker were permitted to have but one book, it would certainly be an unabridged dictionary. If at first one cannot have an unabridged dictionary, he will find that the student edition of Webster's, of the Standard, or the advanced edition of Winston's is excellent and inexpensive. Other than the dictionary there is no book on which more painstaking scholarship has been expended. Certainly no book treats more subjects or gives information more indispensable to a speaker. It tells us the meaning of every word, how that word is divided into syllables, the sound to be given to every vowel and every consonant of every syllable, which syllable or syllables are to be accented, how the word as a whole is to be pronounced, and the derivation of the word or of its parts. It also refers us to words of similar meanings, synonyms, and to words of contrary meanings, antonyms. One who has acquired the dictionary habit is well on his way toward a good vocabulary.

: Building a Vocabulary 101 

(2) The second, -or first if the particular student prefers, -to be recommended is reading, much reading, reading of worthwhile books and periodicals, discriminating reading, reading with pencil and notebook in hand. To select another's reading is a task too large for most of us. It seems safe, however, to lay down this general principle: When one picks up a book, a paper, or other periodical whose thought or language is below his own at its best, one could spend his time more profitably than in reading it. The only object in reading is to improve our thinking or the expression of our thought. We are under no obligation to spend valuable time in reading trash. It is seldom difficult for any of us to get hold of reading that is above us in both content and form, thought and expression. That is what we want. There is no time for any other.

Fortunately for us these two excellences seem to be together, good thought and suitable expression. It is the exception to find them separated. When the thought is good, the language is likely to be also; and it is inconceivable that language of real excellence could be the expression of meritless thought. Then to improve our vocabularies let us be discriminating readers, let us seek well-thought-out messages artistically expressed. 

When we come upon such a message so expressed, a cursory reading is not sufficient. We must not be in too much of a hurry. We must read, like Robert Louis 

Stevenson, with pencil and notebook in hand, putting down for future study the new words and phrases we wish to incorporate into our vocabularies. While a little alliteration is pleasing and certain sounds are more musical than others, the real basis of choosing words must always be the adequacy and effectiveness with which they express their meanings. Much of one's reading may be merely reading, in which one will give himself over to the book or periodical, expose himself to

I 

 it, for the purpose of absorbing a vocabulary as a kind of by-product; but much of one's reading also should be more than reading, it should be so discriminating and so intense that it will be study. In this connection it will be a study of words. It is easily possible to spend years and years in the reading of many books with but little improvement of one's vocabulary. On the other hand, it is possible by hard work to make a noticeable improvement in one's vocabulary in a comparatively short time.

In this matter of improving our vocabularies two things must be kept in mind: one is the enlargement of our vocabulary and the other is its refinement, acquiring more words and finding more suitable words. 

Both are necessary. Mere volubility without a sense of propriety is not very valuable. On the other hand, great nicety in the use of a few words will be but slight consolation when one finds himself in need of a wealth of words that he does not have.

BOOKS AND PERIODICALS 

Above I have recommended the reading, and even the study, of both books and papers and have tried to dissuade the student from spending his time on trash. 

There are those who will take this as a flagrant contradiction. We are often admonished to throw away the trashy newspapers and get hold of a good book, which if properly understood is good advice. But it is easily misunderstood. Throw away our trashy books and get hold of a good newspaper would be just as good advice. Let us say it in either form we please, provided only that we understand it. Let us be careful to avoid the implication that only trash is printed in papers and that all paragraphs of all books are gems of literature. 

This is not quite true. I am almost too strong a believer in books. I believe in them so strongly that

: Building a Vocabulary 103 

just now I am trying to write one; yet I am unable to bring myself into complete accord with those who speak and write as if all books are good and all newspapers are bad. That is not true. What we need to remember is that there are books and books, there are newspapers and newspapers. Occasionally at least we come upon a brilliant paragraph in a country newspaper and a stupid page in a metropolitan book. The student who really wishes to improve his vocabulary can hardly do better than to read, sometimes to study, the editorials and special columns of our really great American newspapers. This is true also of our weekly and monthly magazines. One of these that comes to my desk regularly is a weekly journal of religion. I find that almost every page and every paragraph in every issue of this journal is a model of effective expression that I can ill afford not to study both for thought and for form. 

Let us use all good literature to enrich our vocabularies. 

EACH HAS MANY VOCABULARIES 

It is important to note that instead of having a vocabulary every one has vocabularies, several of them. 

For illustration, the reader of this paragraph has vocabularies on each of several subjects like art, education, agriculture, history, science, mathematics, etc., the richness or poverty of each depending upon his familiarity with the subject. Then in another sense one has several vocabularies. He has a reading vocabulary, a hearing vocabulary, a writing vocabulary, and a speaking vocabulary.

ACQUISITION BY ASSOCIATION 

One's use of words depends upon the psychological law of association: The more strongly one has associated an idea and its corresponding word, the more readily will

one of these suggest the other. A modification of this law needs to be noted. Although the word and its corresponding idea are always the two things that are associated, it does not necessarily follow that either of them will as readily suggest the other. This is the modification referred to: Suggestion is more likely in the direction in which the association has oftenest taken place than in the opposite direction. That is, if one's mind has more frequently proceeded from word to idea, then the word is more likely to suggest the idea than the idea is to suggest the word; or if one's mind has more frequently proceeded from the idea to the word, then the idea is more likely to suggest the word than the word is to suggest the idea. In one case, one can read or hear more readily than he can write or speak; in the other case he can write or speak more readily than he can read or hear. This makes it easy for us to understand how one may be a fluent reader when he speaks only slowly and laboriously, and how the opposite may sometimes be true. It will readily be observed that in enlarging his vocabulary one proceeds in both these directions, from idea to word and from word to idea. For illustration, when one is mastering a new subject or is enlarging his understanding of an old one, he masters the terminology that will express his new ideas; and when in reading or study he comes upon words that are not familiar, he then seeks an understanding of the ideas this new terminology expresses. Work in both directions is valuable. The only way to waste time is in mechanically memorizing words without the ideas they signify. Instead of this, one should in every case make an active effort to weld together the idea and the word so firmly that it is almost impossible to have either recur without the other. Any two things that have been thought together tend to recur together. Think the word with its idea.

: Building a Vocabulary 105 

As soon as possible after making the acquaintance of a new word or phrase, one should find or make an opportunity to use it. When one has used a new word three or four times in speech and as many times in writing, always in its proper signification, it is then a permanent part of his vocabulary. 

In building a vocabulary, only a small portion of one's effort should be in the direction of fine writing, but nearly all of it should be for exactness, clearness, and force. Word pictures that seem to spring spontaneously from one's conception of his subject are beautiful; but those that seem to have been spread on as additional decoration, with effort and in a spirit of affectation, are an abomination. Any adjective necessary to express a quality of an object one is describing is necessary to good writing or speaking; but a string of adjectives redolent of the thesaurus and hauled out to be tacked on, fools no one but the speaker. Why is it that avoiding affectation requires an effort on the part of most of us when affectation itself is so ugly to all of us? Let every thought and every emotion spring spontaneously from the subject under consideration, then let the words be the natural and adequate expression of these thoughts and emotions, and there will be no semblance of affectation because there will be no affectation. 

ETYMOLOGY 

(3) A third method of building a vocabulary is by the study of etymology, the origin and history of words. 

Language scholars tell us that our Modern English is at least the equal of any language ever spoken. It is helpful to every speaker to have at least an elementary understanding of the structure and origin of our language. Any extended discussion would not be in place here, but a paragraph or a page may be.

SOURCES OF ENGLISH 

For years, indeed for centuries and until quite recently, the opinion prevailed that our English was sprung in some way from Latin and Greek, and so through the centuries the study of these languages was the major part of every liberal education. No one questions the greatness of these languages or of their literatures. They are studied in our best colleges today and may continue to be studied for centuries to come. 

Our English is indebted to them for generous and valuable contributions, but not for the great bulk of our words. Our language is not Latin or Greek but Teutonic in its origin. Its growth, its development, has for the most part been indigenous rather than by additions from the outside. Most of our Modern English is 

Old English, with or without modifications. Our articles, a, an, the, are Anglo-Saxon. So are most of our commonly used nouns, pronouns, adjectives, verbs, prepositions, and conjunctions. So, our language, the 

English language, exists in its own right. 

ROOTS, PREFIXES, SUFFIXES 

But Modern English, particularly the English that scholars speak and write, is heavily indebted to both Latin and Greek. Any one wishing to enrich his vocabulary will profit greatly by the habit of analyzing words to find what their parts are, what they mean, and how these parts are put together to form words. These parts are roots, prefixes, and suffixes. A root is the heart of a word, the simple form of it that has, or once had, an independent meaning, as duc (lead), in production. A prefix is a little word or syllable placed before a root word to modify its meaning; as, ante in antedate. A suffix is a little word or syllable annexed to the root word to modify its meaning; as let in booklet.

: Building a Vocabulary 107 LATIN ROOTS 

Here I am following the usual custom of listing the verb roots in the infinitive form, all coming in alphabetic order. 

After each root I am writing one, two, or three words derived from it. It will be interesting and valuable for the student or reader to extend this list as far as possible, mastering the meaning of each derivative word, incorporating it in both his written and his spoken vocabulary. amare, to love: amiable, amity, amicably. anima, life: animate, inanimate, animation. aqua, water: aqueous, aqueduct. audire, to hear: audience, audible, auditorium. capere, to take: captive, capture, captivate. clamare, to call out: acclaim, acclamation. clarare, to clear up: clarify. cor, heart: cordial, accord, discord. credere, to believe: credulous, incredible, credulity. ducere, to lead: induce, induction, deduction. facere, to make: manufacture, factory, faction. ferre, to bear: ferry, inference, transfer. fidere, to trust: confide, confidence, confident. gradi, to walk: transgression, transgressor. habere, to have: inhabit, inhabitant, inhibit. jacere, to throw: project, inject, dejected, subject. liber, book: library. litera, a letter: literal, literary, illiteracy. malus, bad: malefactor, malady, malnutrition. mittere, to send: commit, commission, permission. movere, to move: movable, immovable. nomen, name: noun, pronoun, nominate. orare, to speak: oration, peroration, orator. pendere, to hang: dependent, impending. regere, to rule: regular, irregular. scribere, to write: inscription, description. venire, to come: convenient, inconvenient.

GREEK ROOTS 

This brief list may be considerably extended by any one interested. arch from archein, to rule, or to be first: anarchy, monarchy, archives. auto from autos, himself: autocrat, automobile, automatic. chron from chronos, time: chronicle, chronological, synchronous. graph from graphein, to write: autograph, biography, geography. log from logos, word: psychology, physiology, geology. metr from metron, measure: metronome, metrical, meter. philo from philos, loving: philosophy, philanthropy, Philadelphia.

ab, meaning away from, as in abrupt, abbreviate. Before v and m the b is dropped, leaving the a to express the same meaning, as in avert and amend. CHAPTER VIII THE VOCAL RESPONSE: BREATHING 

The breath is the support of the voice. No breath, no voice. Poor breathing, bad tones. The primary purpose of breathing, of course, is to sustain life; its secondary purpose is to sustain the tones that make up speech. Breathing for life is automatic, regular, and rhythmical; breathing for speech is irregular, partly automatic and partly voluntary.

The Mechanism. There is a very prevalent theory, with which I am not altogether out of sympathy, that speaking is done best when one disregards entirely the whole mechanism. Those who hold this theory contend that one will speak at his best when he is altogether unconscious of having lungs, tongue, mouth, teeth, vocal chords, and thinks only of his tones. They say that if one will form a correct conception of proper tones, center his attention upon them, listen to them, feel them and the processes of making them, forgetting completely the mechanism involved in producing them, they are least likely to be bad. It is possible that I am even more strongly in sympathy with this view than I should be. Many excellent speakers have followed it. 

It is possible that it might be best for all of us, if we were only able to think and feel, that is, to image, the tones both in the making and when they are done. 

However, the matter of breath control is so vital to speech, and improper breathing is so common, that it seems to me some knowledge of how correct breathing takes place can hardly fail to be helpful. 114

The lungs are saclike receptacles containing the air. 

They themselves are passive and not able to take any active part in breathing. Air is forced into them and out of them by muscles. It may not be best to say that air is forced into the lungs, for air goes without forcing into any vacant space it can reach; but it is literally forced or squeezed out of them by the act of the muscles in making a smaller cavity. This constitutes the process of breathing, which is nothing more than the rushing of air into the lungs and the forcing of air out of the lungs. In ordinary life breathing the process is altogether automatic. That is, the muscles contract and relax without any effort and even without any consciousness on our part. This breathing process, however, can at any moment become conscious and largely voluntary. The reader may easily test this out: Catch yourself sometime when your breathing has been going on automatically and unconsciously; now take charge of it yourself. Take in more breath, hold it longer, force more of it out. Let everything be at a standstill for a moment. Now take in a little breath, hold it for a few seconds, then continue to fill the lungs, hold for a moment, and then force it all out explosively. Now fill the lungs by expanding first the upper part of the chest, then the middle, and finally the lower part. Do this again by changing the order to lower, middle, upper. 

Then again by changing the order to middle, lower, upper; and again by changing it to middle, upper, lower. This will make it clear that the act of normal breathing, which is automatic and involuntary, may by an effort on our part become conscious and voluntary. 

If this were not true, it would be meaningless to talk about improving one's breathing. One can improve his breathing by taking charge of it. One can consciously and by effort practice a different form of breathing until it is so well established that it will then

become automatic and involuntary. That is precisely what we are getting ready to do.

ALL BREATHING IS BY MEANS OF MUSCLES 

How We Breathe. Let us keep in mind, then, that we breathe by contracting and relaxing muscles. It would not be safe for men or animals to have been intrusted with the responsibility of controlling these muscles for life breathing. In all normal breathing the muscles take care of it without direction. For purposes of speech, however, we can take over the control of this muscular activity. We can control the form of breathing. We must therefore know which form is best for speech. A speaker must so master these muscles that they may be completely under his control. He must so master the best form of breathing that it will become automatic. 

The process of breathing is itself very simple, the air goes into the lungs and out of them. Muscles by contracting enlarge the chest cavity, which permits the air to rush in. These muscles by relaxing and others by contracting diminish the chest cavity, which forces the air out. These two processes are respectively inspiration or inhalation and expiration or exhalation; and these two processes are what we call breathing, or respiration. 

The muscles chiefly used in breathing are (1) the diaphragm, (2) the abdominal muscles, and (3) the rib muscles. 

1. The Diaphragm is a heavy muscle forming a roof over the abdominal organs and a floor under the chest organs. When it is at rest, it is curved or arched upward, and when contracted it is flattened. When it contracts, it gives the lungs more room, so they may fill themselves with air. When it relaxes, it arches upward again, forcing the air out of the lungs.

2. The Abdominal Muscles, working in harmony with the diaphragm, push the wall of the abdomen forward as the diaphragm flattens, thus helping to enlarge the lung capacity for more air. Then as we exhale, the wall of the abdomen drops inward as the diaphragm rises. 

3. The Rib Muscles are outer and inner. The outer rib muscles by contracting lift the ribs out and up in inspiration. In expiration these outer rib muscles by relaxing let the ribs drop. At the same time the inner rib muscles draw the ribs down and in, helping to force the air out.

RULES FOR BREATHING 

A student whose voice very soon became husky and hoarse in speaking was asked by his teacher, "How about your breathing?" His reply was, "Oh, that is all right, I have been doing that all my life." Yes, but possibly he had not been doing it properly. There are four rules for breathing that will be found helpful to a speaker: (1) Do not fill the lungs to their utmost capacity just before beginning to speak. One needs plenty of breath for the utterances he must make, but if he has too much, the surplus is only in the way. If one feels that he is running a little short of breath, there will soon be an opportunity to take in a little more. Or if there is not such an opportunity, the speaker must make one. It is better to refuel a little oftener than to carry too much dead weight. (2) Do not allow the air in the lungs to become completely exhausted. Of course, it is never possible to expel all the air from the lungs, but one may come so near it that he can no longer speak words, he can only gasp for breath. One is not on the floor to gasp, he is there to mold his breath into tones and syllables that will be good to hear. One must not allow himself to run out of

breath; he must hold a little air in reserve. 

He may need it. A period of depression may be just around the corner. (3) A speaker or singer must acquire speed in inhalation. No matter how frugal a speaker or a singer may be, there will come times when the breath in reserve is not sufficient for the phrase just ahead, and not much of a pause is in order. Under such conditions one must be able to inhale at least a little air instantly. He must not make a noticeable pause, nor must he try to speak while he is inhaling. He has not time for a full breath, but he must have some. He must be able to get it quickly. (4) A speaker or singer must practice the strictest economy in using the breath that is in the lungs. At the instant he may be well supplied, but he cannot afford to waste any air he has gone to the trouble to get into his lungs. In just a moment a long phrase may require all the breath he holds in reserve and just at a time when he cannot conveniently take in more. As another reason for economy in exhalation, we may note that the use of more breath than is necessary injures the tones.

Don't waste your breath. Make it all up into tones, and make a little of it go as far as possible. 

FORMS OF BREATHING 

There are three fairly well-defined forms of breathing: 

Clavicular, Costal, Diaphragmatic.

I. Clavicular, or Upper-Chest Breathing. In this form or method of breathing one lifts and pushes forward the breast bone and elevates the shoulders. This, of course, is done by muscles and it does give a little additional expansion of the upper chest so that more air may be taken into the lungs. This is the poorest and most fatiguing of all forms of breathing. Inexperienced speakers and singers use it. Bad results are sure to

follow, - a cramped and irritated throat, poor tones, and a tired feeling. Both for health and for speaking it is to be altogether condemned, — with one possible exception. It is the quickest way to get a little air into the lungs when one finds that his supply is exhausted and he is obliged to have more breath at once. In this case it is better for the speaker or singer to breathe in this bad way than not to breathe at all. Such a condition will not often arise if one has adhered closely to rules 3 and 4. 

2. Costal, or Rib, Breathing. In this form, the intercostal muscles lift the lower ribs upward and outward, thus greatly increasing the chest capacity and giving the lungs room for a larger supply of air. The chest, as outlined by the ribs, being cone shaped, smaller at the top and larger at the bottom, makes this second form of breathing greatly superior to the first. Another point of superiority is in the fact that the lower ribs, being unattached to the breast bone, are much more movable than the upper ribs, which are attached. Costal or rib breathing is not altogether bad for any use of the voice, and it is excellent for health. 

3. Diaphragmatic, or Abdominal, Breathing. It is called diaphragmatic because the diaphragm is the muscle that has most to do with it; and abdominal, because in this kind of breathing the abdominal muscles coöperate with the diaphragm. As the diaphragm contracts it flattens or presses downward while the abdominal muscles, by contracting, extend the abdominal walls, all together greatly enlarging the space in which the lungs can expand. This kind of breathing, although it is used by the lower animals and human babies, in some way gets away from most of us as we grow older and has to be learned over again. Most persons are strangers to it. Tell them to take a deep breath and they will raise the shoulders and throw out

the breast bone. That is not a deep breath; it is the shallowest breath possible. Deep breathing is diaphragmatic breathing. It is the kind of breathing that means most for health, and it means everything for speaking and singing. The breath supports the tone, and the diaphragm supports the breath. When one is speaking, the gradually relaxing diaphragm constantly presses upward so as to force out the proper amount of breath for the tones to be uttered. If by a slight contraction at the wrong time the diaphragm is pulled downward, there is left no breath support and the voice breaks. Many persons are in the habit of speaking so that we miss the latter part of many of their sentences. 

That is because they contract the diaphragm and leave the breath without support.

A Breathing Test. 

is right, let us test it.

Now to be sure that our breathing 

Stand in good posture. See that the clothing is loose around the waist. Place the hands on sides, with thumbs extending backward and the fingers toward the front just a little below the lower ribs. Take a deep breath. If your fingers feel the abdomen going in, or not moving, while you are inhaling, you are not breathing properly; if they feel the abdomen pressing outward against them in inhaling and drawing in from them while you are exhaling, you are breathing properly. Keep this up until the correct breathing is completely under your control. Now take a deep breath, making sure that as you inhale you feel the diaphragm being drawn downward and the abdomen pushed outward and forward. Use this breath in speaking a few sentences, any that you have in mind, feeling the diaphragm as it relaxes constantly pressing upward to form the breath support. Be careful not to use more breath than you need. Listen to your tones. Be sure that they satisfy you. you hear them. While practicing these sentences, see 

Feel them as

that you have a big wide-open throat. See that your head, nose, and mouth cavities are all open. Listen for the resonance. Is it pleasing, musical? If you do not like the sound of these sentences, find others that you do like. Select sentences that express the mood you happen to be in. Practice deep breathing as you take your walks. Do it after you retire at night and before rising in the morning, while lying on your back. Lie down on your back on a hard floor and practice deep breathing for fifteen minutes every day. Keep your mouth shut. In all practice exercises breathe through the nose. In speaking or singing always breathe through the nose if the pause in order will permit it. 

Inhale through the mouth only when the time between phrases is too short for nose breathing.

CHAPTER IX THE VOCAL RESPONSE. ENUNCIATION, ARTICULATION, PRONUNCIATION VOICE MECHANISM 

We shall take space here for only a word as to the vocal organs. Those who care for more information will readily come upon it in the physiologies; those who prefer to forget all about it and think only of the tones are at liberty to omit even this brief word. 

At the upper end of the trachea, or windpipe, is the larynx, or voice-box. It contains the little bands called the vocal chords. When air from the lungs is forced between and over the vocal chords, they vibrate, thus producing sound. If the chords are relaxed, the tone is low; if they are contracted, the tone is higher in pitch. 

This is tone, but it is not speech. This tone is transformed into speech by the action of the mouth cavity, the tongue, and the teeth. The tone produced by the volume of air rushing through the larynx, between and over the vocal chords, is called the fundamental tone. 

In speaking or singing this fundamental tone is modified, either for better or for worse, first by the action of the throat, and second by overtones. An overtone is a tone resulting from vibrations reflected from the nasal and head cavities. When one's overtones reinforce his fundamental tone, the result is his speaking or singing tone. A wide-open throat is a valuable asset to the speaker, reader, or singer. A tone is never improved by restricting the throat. The same is true with the nasal and head cavities. The more open they are, the more 122

will their overtones improve the fundamental tone. 

Keep these cavities open. Feel the vibrations in them. 

Speak through the nose as much as possible. This always makes the tones more beautiful. 

ENUNCIATION, ARTICULATION, PRONUNCIATION 

By vocal response is meant every sound a speaker utters to give expression to his thoughts and feelings. 

In its totality it includes all the elementary sounds of the language and their various combinations to form the words, phrases, and sentences that convey his message. 

The words enunciation, articulation, pronunciation, are often used synonymously, and are seldom used with the nice discrimination necessary in studies of this kind. 

Unfortunately different writers on speech do not all use these words in the same senses. While they all do select meanings of these terms authorized by the dictionary, these meanings are not always the ones most helpful to students. Well aware that my own effort may be just as far from satisfying everybody, I submit the following as the senses in which these words should be used for the sake of clearness.

Enunciation is the rendering of the individual elementary sounds of the language. 

Articulation always has reference to the joining or connecting of the elements or parts of speech. These elements or parts may be sounds joined into syllables, syllables into words, words into phrases, or phrases into sentences. It always has reference to the kind of joint or connection made.

Pronunciation is the rendering of the sounds of individual words.

Keeping these definitions in mind will enable us to have clear ideas of what is referred to when we use these words or hear them used by others. We will under-

stand that enunciation and articulation are essential parts of pronunciation; that correct enunciation could exist along with faulty articulation; that articulation can be faultless even when the individual sounds connected are wrong; and that one with perfect enunciation may by defective articulation render his pronunciation faulty.

Every student should make an earnest effort to detect the errors in his manner of speaking and should welcome any criticism from teacher, classmate, or friend. When one finds out that an error exists, the next step is to define it or identify it; that is, to determine whether it is an error in enunciation, in articulation, or in pronunciation. Then it will be in order to settle upon the particular exercises that will remedy the defect. 

ENUNCIATION 

It is hardly possible to overestimate the value of enunciation. The primary purpose of speaking is that one may be understood. We sometimes say it is to be heard, but it is rather to be understood. Many times a speaker is heard but not understood. He might as well not speak at all. It only annoys us to hear what we cannot understand. The two are not the same. 

Hearing depends upon the force or loudness of a tone, while understanding the words of a speaker depends nearly altogether upon his enunciation. There must of course be some degree of loudness or force, but it is surprising how little is necessary if the enunciation is good. This is commonly misunderstood by both speaker and audience. Many times when we are striving but failing to catch the words of a speaker, we call out, "louder, louder," when that is not at all the thing we need. The speaker often makes the same mistake. When reminded by the presiding officer that he is not understood, he is likely to say, "I shall speak

louder."

In a large majority of such cases, what is really needed is not more loudness, but a cleaner, clearer utterance of sounds, better enunciation. If we were called upon to name the one speech defect that is most prevalent, it would certainly be lazy, hazy, mouthy, mussy, slouchy, slovenly enunciation. We are often reminded, and properly, that if our words are to carry to the distant parts of the auditorium our tones must be properly placed. True as this is, we need to be reminded also that tone placement depends largely upon enunciation. The effort to mold or shape a tone properly almost certainly insures its proper placement. 

It is very difficult, if not impossible, to utter a tone correctly without placing it properly.

The matter of defective enunciation, common enough in beginners, is entirely too much in evidence among experienced speakers. There is no excuse for this. It is intolerable. It is almost criminal. It is obtaining the time and attention of an audience under false pretense. It would certainly strike us as not only unusual but absurd in an architect or builder to declare that while he is much concerned about the building as a whole he cares little or not at all for the individual bricks or blocks that compose it. Or what should we think of the pianist or violinist who would say that his business is to play the whole composition without any concern for the individual notes he strikes? Yet that is precisely what we have in a man pretending to make a speech but careless of his enunciation. Each and every individual sound of each and every word should be exactly that sound and not a remote approximation of it. 

If one anxious to become a speaker wishes to know where to begin, he will not go wrong if he looks first to his enunciation. If an experienced speaker is ambitious to improve, in most cases he will do it best by more attention to his enunciation.

PHONETICS 

All speech may be conveniently thought of as sound and combinations of sound. The science of the sounds of speech together with their symbols is known as phonetics. Any student of speech will be abundantly repaid for the time and effort necessary to an understanding of these speech sounds individually and in their various combinations. The more completely one has mastered these sounds the better will he speak. By the mastery of these sounds is meant not only an understanding of what they are but also skill in uttering them. 

In our country we have two well-organized and authorized sets of symbols for designating our English speech sounds. One of these is the scientific alphabet recommended by the American Philological Society and used in the Standard Dictionary, and the other is the series of diacritical marks used in Webster's Dictionaries and nearly all textbooks. The scientific alphabet, in which one sound has but one symbol and one symbol but one sound, is ideal and in time will prevail. In this book, however, the writer has for practical reasons decided to use the familiar diacritical marks. Here only a brief list of elementary sounds, those most frequently met in the pronunciation of common words, is given for special study. In this connection the writer strongly recommends not only a reading but a careful study of the article, A Guide to Pronunciation, and the article, Spelling and Pronunciation, in the Introduction to Funk and Wagnall's New Standard Dictionary. 

TWO CLASSES OF SOUNDS 

The sounds constituting our English speech are of two general classes, vowels and consonants. They are also spoken of as respectively open and closed. It may be readily noted that in sounding a vowel the mouth is kept

open and that in uttering a consonant sound the mouth is altogether or partly closed. Remembering this in connection with the other fact, that while brute animals can sound the vowels, probably all of them, man alone can sound the consonants, forces upon us the suggestion, which may prove helpful in ways innumerable, that a lower degree of intelligence is required to open the mouth than to close it. This principle might possibly be so applied as to aid us in determining the length of our speeches.

VOWELS 

The vowel sounds of our language are indicated by the letters a, e, i, o, u, and occasionally w and y, together with their different markings. The most important of these vowel sounds are indicated below:

THE а SOUNDS 

ā, long a, as in late, fate, rate, hate, date. ǎ, short a, as in at, cat, sat, bat, that, hat. å, intermediate a, as in laugh, calf, staff, ask, clasp, class, after, master. ä, broad or Italian a, as in far, cart, calm, arm, alms, father. a, as in all, call, fall, talk, stalk. a, the short o sound of a, as in swan, was, what. â, the modified short a, as in care, share, compare, parent.

Note. In discriminating pronunciation the long a is modified or obscured in unaccented syllables by lessening its quantity, as in senate, preface, prelate. The same is true of the short a, as in final, mortal, loyal, accrue, account.

THE e SOUNDS 

ē, long e, as in eve, serene, seen. ě, short e, as in end, yet, bet, set, let, debt.

e, modified e, like the modified short a, this sound of e comes before r in unaccented syllables; as in conference, writer, reader, lover. e, the u sound of e, as in her, serve, prefer, deter, infer. 

Note. In unaccented syllables short e is to be obscured or lessened in quantity, as in element, decent, filament. 

THE SOUNDS 

i, long i, as in ice, tile, line, vial. 

I, short i, as in it, little, sit, quit, bit. i, the long e sound of i, as in machine, gasoline, quarantine.* i, the û sound of i, as in sir. 

Note. This sound was originally called the long sound of i. 

THE O SOUNDS 

ō, long o, as in no, so, low, bow, row.

Ŏ, short o, as in not, lot, dot, tot. o, the oo sound of o, as in to, do. o, as in wolf. o, as in son. o, as in work.

Note. The long o and the short o are often obscured in unaccented syllables.

THE U SOUNDS 

ū, long u, as in use, fuse, abuse, mute, cube, pure, true, duty, lute. ŭ, short u, as in up, tub, under, usher, utter. u, the long oo sound of u, as in rule, rude, rumor, blue, flute, June. u, the short oo sound of u, as in bull, full, pull, push, put. û, as in urn, burn, urge, hurl. This is only a slight modification of the short u sound. u, the short i sound of u, as in busy. u, the short e sound of u, as in bury. 

Note. The last two are exceptional.

DIPHTHONGS 

Sometimes two vowel sounds are so closely united as to form what almost seems to be a single sound. Such combinations are called diphthongs. By giving them careful attention one can always analyze a diphthong into its two elementary sounds. Thus the diphthong oi in moist is seen (or rather heard), upon close examination, to be made up of the two sounds, a as in all and short i as in it.

The diphthongs most commonly met with are oi as in hoist, ou as in out, au as in fraud.

If we could only induce everybody to master the scientific alphabet, it would be better. Its 45 characters represent the 45 elementary sounds. It is recommended by the American Philological Association and by the Modern Language Association. Every character has one sound, and every sound one character.

CONSONANTS 

The clearness and distinctness of speech depend even more upon consonants than upon vowels. As previously stated, the consonants are sounded either by restricting the mouth cavity or by completely closing the mouth. b, as in boy, blow, rob. c, hard, equivalent to k, as in call, cave, cold, collar. c, soft, equivalent to s, as in civil, cypress, force, vice. d, as in day, do, dry, bed. f, as in fife, fly, fame. g, hard, as in get, give, go, gun, egg. g, soft, as in edge, gem, ginger. h, as in hate, hard, hit. j, equivalent to soft g, as in jar, jest, jam, project, majesty. k, as in kite, kill.

1, as in lie, low, help, else. m, as in may, me, mile, minute n, as in none, nine, needle, nation. p, as in pay, pray, cup, help. q is always followed by u, the two letters having the sound of kw, as in queen, conquest. r, as in ring, room, very, merry. s has two voiceless sounds, as in sun and sure; and two voiced sounds as in has and vision. t, as in tie, tune, time, turn. v, as in vane, vivid, live, move. w, as in we, wet, twin, twelve. w is often combined with h, as in what, when, where, which. x has a voiceless sound, as in box, wax, execute; and a voiced sound, as in exist, exert, exalt. The voiceless sound is equivalent to ks, and the voiced sound to gz. y as a vowel has all the i sounds except that of i in pique. As a consonant it is sounded as in you, year, yonder, beyond, vineyard. z, as in zeal, zone, azure, seizure. etc. 

PRINCIPLES AND PRACTICE 

Learning how to speak is a very important matter, but whoever thinks it is the whole matter should be reminded of Portia's reflection, "If to do were as easy,' In addition to knowing how, one must become able to do it as he knows it should be done. This requires drill or practice. In addition to one's actual practice on a real speech before a real audience, one will find himself greatly benefited by careful drill upon isolated selections. These may consist of one or more paragraphs, of single sentences, of phrases, of single words, or of any sound or combination of sounds the particular student may feel the need of more proficiency in. One may practice these exercises alone, or more profitably

I think with a group of persons all interested in the same results. The criticism of the group will be very helpful to all its members.

For this purpose, selections containing more difficult vocal combinations than are often necessary in actual speeches may be profitably practiced. Music students would all like to play real pieces from the very start, but skillful teachers keep them at work for a time on their scales. Even the master musicians devote a generous portion of their time to these same scales. So here the students of speech, and even experienced speakers, will profit by the careful drill on these and other vocal exercises. It may sound foolish to some critic who mistakes mumbling for speaking. Never mind him.

It was not foolish to Henry Clay or Demosthenes. A student should have at hand a generous group of such practice exercises and should enlarge it as he comes upon others he thinks helpful. Then he may choose the ones he thinks best for himself, but almost any combination of vowel and consonant sounds will be helpful. 

In drill on the exercises, the individual sounds must have attention and these same sounds in their varied combinations. Vowels may be practiced alone and consonants alone, and then each vowel in connection with all the consonants and each consonant in connection with all the vowels. In all this practice, mouth flexibility, proper tone placement, open throat, and breath support must be looked after. Also, every individual excellence of speech must be sought, clearness, distinctness, resonance, melody, emphasis, inflection, pause, pitch, force, vocal flexibility, and particularly clean enunciation, nice articulation, and correct pronunciation. Here the different members of the group may be mutually helpful. 

In practicing enunciation and articulation exercises it is better, particularly at the beginning and when one is

working to overcome wrong habits, to exaggerate the clearness, if such a thing is possible. It will be found much easier later to eliminate this exaggeration than to overcome well-established habits of slovenliness. Even sometimes in actual speaking in very large auditoriums with poor acoustic qualities it is necessary to exaggerate the nicety of enunciation or speak without being understood. Certainly in ordinary conversation or in speaking in a small room to only a few persons one would never tolerate the exaggeration. There it would be justly criticized as affectation and pedantry. But let me repeat, in practice to overcome faults, it is better to overdo the correctness than to fall short of it. One is expected always to remain in possession of his sense of propriety. On the one hand he must avoid a slovenliness that is intolerable and on the other an affectation that is disgusting. Some one who has heard both says that a cultured American speaks with distinctness and a cultured Englishman with distinction. This may be true. It may be a just criticism upon us; but even so, most audiences prefer a distinctness they get to a distinction they miss. One of the most complimentary things a speaker ever hears on his manner of speaking is, "I sat in the most distant corner of the room, away back under the balcony, and I got every word you spoke." 

Remember the old saying, “For the lack of a nail a shoe was lost, for the lack of a shoe a horse was lost, for the lack of a horse a commander was lost, for the lack of a commander the battle was lost, and the loss of that battle meant a complete change in the course of the world's history." The nails that we must keep in place are our vowels and consonants. 

The quality of every sound uttered depends largely upon the size and shape of the mouth cavity. Also, it is readily observable that the position of the tongue has most to do in shaping the mouth cavity. To enable us

to understand the tongue position in relation to each vowel sound I know nothing better than 

ALEXANDER MELVILLE BELL'S VOCAL CHART 

Note. Try out each sound designated to test its corresponding tongue position.

EXERCISES FOR PRACTICE 

I. Note that in sounding the vowels, ā, ē, ī, ō, ū, the mouth cavity is required to make corresponding changes. 

Observe (feel) the shape of your mouth cavity as you utter each vowel in the way that it sounds best to you. 

Observe that the deeper vowels like oo in moon and o in pole are formed higher up and farther back in the mouth; the shallow vowels like a in pay and e in see are formed right up against the front teeth; and that a as in law and a as in far occupy an intermediate position in the mouth. Now pronounce plainly each of the following words, and then give by itself the vowel sound in each word; as, moon, ōō; pole, ō; law, a = ô; far, ä; lay, ā; fee, ē.

Note. In this and all the following exercises the mouth must be kept flexible, the throat open, and the diaphragm gently pressing upward. 

II. For the purpose of securing strength of the lips, utter the m sound in mum, might, mill. Then the n sound in night, nine, not, noting the different lip positions for m and n. 

III. Now, for the purpose of securing resonance, make, not necessarily loud but very strong, m, n, and ng sounds. Prolong these, permitting the sound to escape through the nose. That is, hum these sounds so that a slightly musical resonance is observable. Do this as you pronounce the syllables, mum, hum, lum, bum, dum, num, sum, cum, sung, hung, rung. Now, following a leader, hum a little melody or portion of it.

If a speaker is fortunate enough to have the audience sing just before he speaks, he will find that nothing will put his voice in better speaking condition than humming the song while the others are singing it. Also, singing itself is excellent for the speaking voice. It is better, however, for the speaker to sing what is commonly called the tune, air, or melody than any one of the special parts. 

IV. In this exercise, as well as in all others, be sure of the correctness of each sound, observe (feel) the corresponding mouth position, and note the muscular change made necessary in the mouth as you change from any sound to the next in order. These combinations from Bell's vowel chart make an invaluable exercise in mouth calisthenics: (1) ah, ale, eel, pool, pole, awl. (2) awl, pole, pool, eel, ale, ah. (3) awl, pull, pool, eel, ill, ale, ell, air. (4) ah, an, ell, ale, ill, eel, pool, pull, pole, awl, ah. (5) ah, ah, ah, o, o, o, e, e, e, o, o, o, ah, ah, ah. 

V. In this exercise, utter each sound indicated with such delicate precision that it cannot possibly be mistaken, enabling the hearer to be as certain of each consonant as he is of each vowel: ah - ba ba 

- ba; ah ahfa fa- fa; ah ah-ha-ha-ha; ah ah ah 

- la- la- la; ah na ah ra ah ta 

na na; ah ra ra; ah 

ta ga ga - ka · da da da. ga. 

ka - ka. ma ma ma. pa pa pa. sa sa sa. va 

va. va ta; ah 

VI. In each of the following exercises see that the tongue and lip movements are accurately made, that every consonant and every vowel is clearly distinguishable, and that all m, n, l, and ng sounds are permitted to resound in the resonance chambers nose and head cavities:

(1) pē, pā, pī, pō, poo; fē, fā, fi, fō, fōo. (2) bē, bā, bī, bō, bōo; dē, dā, dī, dō, dōō. (3) mē, mā, mī, mō, mōo; nē, nā, nī, nō, noo. (4) wē, wā, wi, wō, woo; vē, vā, vī, vō, voo. (5) whē, whā, whi, who, whoo; tē, tā, ti, tō, too. (6) lē, lā, lī, lō, loo; shē, shā, shi, shō, shōo. (7) kē, kā, kī, kō, kōo; gē, gā, gī, gō, gōō (hard g). (8) vē, vi, võ, voo. 

VII. Most of us, possibly all of us, occasionally come upon sound combinations that are difficult to utter. 

Practice, diligent practice, will help. Nonsense combinations of sounds cease to be nonsense when they prove to be helpful in enunciation and articulation. A few of such exercises are set down here from the common storehouse. They have helped many to make ready and right muscular coördinations of the vocal organs.

Practice upon them is injurious to any one who permits himself to stammer and stumble over them. Safety first, then speed. The student may follow his own best judgment and his teacher's advice with reference to them: (1) Some shun sunshine, but why should you shun sunshine? (2) Bring me some ice, not some mice. (3) He saw six slim, sleek, slender saplings. (4) She sells sea-shells; should he sell sea-shells? (5) Milestones mark the march of time. (6) Our forefathers fought fearlessly for freedom. (7) Rejoice with them, though then and there they and theirs will be greater than thee and thine. (8) Seated on shore, she sees ships with shining sails on the shimmering sea. (9) No man need know need in this new nation. (10) Round the rough and rugged rocks the ragged. rascals rudely ran. (11) How much wood would a woodchuck chuck if a woodchuck would chuck wood?

(12) He spoke reasonably, philosophically, disinterestedly, and yet particularly, of the unceremoniousness of their communicability, and peremptorily, authoritatively, unhesitatingly declared it to be wholly inexplicable. (13) Peter Piper picked a peck of pickled peppers. 

If Peter Piper picked a peck of pickled peppers, where is the peck of pickled peppers Peter Piper picked. (14) Theophilus Thistle, the thistle sifter, sifted a sieve of unsifted thistles. If Theophilus Thistle, the thistle sifter, sifted a sieve of unsifted thistles, where is the sieve of unsifted thistles Theophilus Thistle, the thistle sifter, sifted. (15) Sister Susie's sewing shirts for soldiers;

Such skill at sewing shirts our shy young 

Sister Susie shows, Some soldiers send epistles, Say they'd rather sleep in thistles 

Than the saucy, soft, short shirts for soldiers 

Sister Susie sews.

VIII. In pronouncing the following words, enunciate distinctly every individual sound, noting carefully what help is rendered by tongue, teeth, lips, palate, throat, and nasal cavities. Also, see that the syllables of each word are properly linked together: accredited, stupendous, thither, booth, scythe, asthetics, athletics, resuscitate, reminiscent, scism, scenic, statistics, statistician, causality, casualty, casuistry, leisure, eligible, lilting, mirroring, purport, perplexity, probably, particularly, probability, ebbing, rubbing, biblical, vivacity, vivacious, irreverent, irrelevant, unintelligible, irrevocable, fiftieth, effervescent, witch, which, wart, thwart, jug, judge, dirigible, prejudice, prejudicial, curious, querulous, asked, exquisite, material, immaterial, memorable, mimic, arctic, articulate, article, intelligent, intelligible, unintelligible, indissoluble.

Note. Let each word be pronounced by each member of the class while the other members are listening attentively in order that every error may be detected and corrected. Be sure that every student understands the meaning of each word and is able to use it correctly in a sentence.

VOICE QUALITY 

The word quality is a very general term. By the quality of a voice is meant any characteristic or aspect of its tones that makes the voice what it is. It is by the qualities of their voices that we distinguish one from another when we hear our friends speaking and are unable to see them. In the same manner we distinguish the different instruments in an orchestra. All speakers and all instruments have tones, but the tones have different qualities. 

As we do not have quite uniform agreement as to what are voice qualities, we shall be obliged here to do the best we can. Here I am setting down as voice qualities (1) Force, (2) Clearness, (3) Pitch, (4) Inflection, (5) Range, (6) Resonance, (7) Modulation.

I. Force is sometimes identified with loudness, but the two are not quite the same. Loudness is always an element in force, but not the only element. The word stress comes nearer to the idea of force. The loudness of a tone varies with the volume of breath used and the economy with which the breath is used. A certain volume of breath makes a louder, stronger tone when all the breath is made up into tone than when part of it is wasted. But in addition to loudness, force includes an element of intensity that often adds more than the loudness. For illustration, there are many times when a speaker will add force or stress to a particular portion of his speech by lowering his voice instead of raising it. 

Also, it is a common observation that many of the loudest speakers are the least forceful. The speaker will have to determine from the thought and the feeling

of his message when there should be more force and when less. Then also he must determine for himself whether he can increase the force by raising his voice or by lowering it. It means much to be a forceful speaker. 

If one is not a forceful speaker, he is not a speaker. 

2. Clearness has reference to the purity of the tone. 

Ordinarily the purer the tone, the less obstruction put in its way, the better. There are certain feelings, however, that require an obstructed tone like the aspirate or the guttural. 

3. Pitch as applied to tone has reference to its position in the scale, whether high or low. Some voices are too high, others too low. Within certain limits, but only within these limits, the pitch of one's speaking voice may be modified. As a general rule, it is better for one to take the pitch that is natural to him, and strive to develop his natural voice by improving the other qualities. No voice, of course, whether high or low, will always be used at the same pitch. In speaking the pitch varies with the mental tone. For illustration, in fright the tone is very high; in the expression of sublimity very low; and in ordinary mental states, such as those expressed in conversation, in between the two. The pitch variation in speaking is much less than in singing. 

4. Inflection is the variation of the voice up and down the scale. Speaking continually in one pitch would be intolerably monotonous. Flexibility is a valuable quality of the speaking voice. It can be cultivated. 

If the flexibility one possesses is used with discrimination, that is, if one makes high the tones that should be high, and low the tones that should be low, he is almost sure to be a pleasing speaker. 

5. Range means the amount of variation in pitch. one's voice is able to compass. One may be able to reach a pitch, either high or low, without being able to

speak naturally in that pitch. This means that there is a domain of voice not yet subdued or brought under cultivation for speaking. It needs to be cleared up. 

The more range a voice has, the greater are its possibilities, and the less monotonous it is likely to be. 

For the most part the range of one's voice is rather definitely fixed, but singers are often able to extend the range both above the upper limit and below the lower. 

6. Resonance is a kind of ringing or singing quality of voice that adds much to its agreeableness. It is due to the reverberation of the fundamental tone through the nasal and head cavities so as to produce an overtone. 

When the overtones are properly blended with the fundamental tone, the result is resonance. A resonant voice is always pleasing. Resonance can be cultivated. 

This reverberation of sound that leads to resonance is occasioned by the combination of certain vowels with certain consonants, particularly the sounds of m, n, ng, and I. To develop resonance, lose no opportunity to hum. Hum all the tunes you know. If you do not know any tunes, hum something else. Any words or syllables containing the letters m and n are excellent. 

After taking a deep breath, hum the syllables, minum, manum, monum, munum, prolonging the initial and final m sounds and the n sound. Do this six times in your normal pitch. Do it six times in a higher pitch, and six times in a lower pitch. In the same manner practice the syllables, ming, mang, mong, mung, prolonging the m and the ng sounds. Feel the resonance up in the nasal and head cavities. Do the same with ling, lang, long, lung. Open your mouth. Also, in these exercises, and in all others, see that you have a big, wide-open throat. An unrestricted, open throat means much for the quality of one's tones and for the ease and comfort with which he speaks, but it means infinitely more for the health of the throat itself. Cul-

tivate resonance. It makes your voice pleasing and musical. If at all possible, always hum a little, the more the better, just before you begin a speech. 

7. Modulation is the amount of variation a voice is capable of in any or all the six qualities just discussed. 

Modulation includes changing pitch, changing force, changing resonance, and so on with the other qualities. 

A well-modulated voice is a good voice. If one's voice is lacking in modulation, he can improve it. Practice to vary your voice in every quality. In your practice much of this may be mechanical in order to bring your voice under complete control; but in all your actual reading or speaking, let every variation of voice be the natural response to the thought and feeling to be expressed.

EXERCISES 

1. Define vocal response, enunciation, articulation, pronunciation, phonetics. 

2. Run over again, before the class, the vowel sounds, the consonant sounds, and the diphthongs. 

3. Discuss the two methods of indicating the sounds of a language. 

4. Discuss Bell's Vocal Chart, and give again, before the class, vowel and consonant combinations listed. 

5. What is your own real opinion as to the value of such nonsense combinations of words as are listed? Give your reasons. 

6. To what extent do you think our speaking may be improved by intelligent training or drill? 

7. Express your opinion of the value of an understanding of the voice mechanism. 

8. What do you mean by voice quality? 

9. Speak briefly on each of the voice qualities, Force, Clearness, Pitch, Inflection, Range, Resonance, Modulation.

CHAPTER X PHYSICAL RESPONSE 

WHEN gesture is mentioned the thing commonly thought of is some movements of the hands. It is that, but it is more. In addition it includes posture, and the position and movement of all other parts of the body as well as of the hands. Gesture in this broad sense must not be thought of as something added to the expression. 

It is a part, an essential part, of the expression itself. 

Man is not yet all spirit, he is part body. A speech, any kind of a speech, long or short, is not made altogether by the mind, not altogether by the mouth; it is made by the whole man, mind and body acting in unison. At least, if it is made in any other way, it is injured in the making. By the mind is meant the whole mind. Not the thought alone, but also the feelings and the will. The baser feelings, the higher or spiritual feelings, every feeling possible to consciousness plays or may play a part in the making of a speech. 

So it is with the body. Not the hands alone, but also the arms, the feet, the legs, the hips, the chest, the back, the shoulders, the neck, the face, the eyes, all are organs of expression. Any one of them greatly helps or greatly hinders in the making of a speech. 

The total contribution of all these bodily organs is what we are here designating as physical response. 

When by thinking one, has arrived at the meaning he wishes to convey, and in response to that meaning the corresponding emotions have arisen, and in response to both meaning and emotions the most suitable words 141

have been chosen, and these have been uttered in proper tones of voice, then in answer to all of this, to the thought, to the emotion, to the words, and to the tones of one's voice, -the body makes its response, which is its contribution to the speech. It is pretty generally believed that if it were not for the handicaps in the form of wrong habits resulting from previous wrong training, the whole body would instinctively assume the right postures and make the right movements. This view is strengthened by our observation of unspoiled children in their spontaneous speeches to one another. This is not the place to inquire into the correctness of this view. Whether it is altogether right or not we all know that long before one is twenty he has formed habits of posture and movement that need correction. Any one interested in the art of expression -and who should not be? — will be abundantly repaid for any time and labor spent in making the body better able to do its part, more responsive to mental states, more in harmony with words and tones of voice. 

POSTURE 

Even more important than the movements of his hands and arms and of his entire body is the posture one assumes when he speaks. Of course we cannot see ourselves as others see us, but we should try to do it, should come as near to doing it as is possible. Two very important helps are to be recommended. One is the criticism of teacher and classmates. This should always be welcome and be gratefully received whether it hurts or not. Each of us must assume that our friends who are enough interested in us to criticize our posture or movements are doing it for our good. Each must be on his guard against receiving a criticism in such a way as to discourage its repetition. Of course, teacher and classmates can be mistaken, but they arè

Physical Response 

143 

not likely to be and they certainly try not to be. The second help along this line is access to a large mirror. 

Many of the great orators and actors—most of those who have taken time to tell us about it — have spent much time in practicing before their mirrors. This is objectionable to some persons because it strikes them as frivolous vanity. So indeed it would be if one stood before his mirror to gloat over his own graces. Then it would be one of the ugliest forms of selfishness. But when the student does it to correct his own defects, to spare his friends the sight of ugliness, to get his message to them in a manner that would be most pleasing to them, then it takes its place among the higher social virtues. If the mirror will help you to improve your posture or movements and it certainly will — it is as commendable to use it as a book. 

POISE OR BALANCE 

A speaker's posture must be one that will give him poise or balance. If one stands as if his body would be easily unbalanced, it is more difficult to make his thought appear strong. One must be erect but not stiff. He must be at ease but not limp or slouchy. The head must be straight up in vertical line with the chest, not poked out in front at right angles with the chest. 

To secure the proper position of the head one teacher recommends that the back of the neck be kept pressing firmly against the collar. The chin must be neither so protruded as to express threat or defiance, nor so drawn in as to admit that one is whipped before he begins. 

The chest should be reasonably high, which will compel the shoulders to fall naturally into their proper position. 

If the neck is too long, one must shorten it; if it is too short, he must stretch it. The back must be reasonably straight, - -not so concave as to make the audience

think it has been sprung or bent, nor so convex as to suggest a hump. No matter how large or how small the abdomen is, it must not be allowed to protrude. The abdominal muscles must not be relaxed, but moderately contracted. The waist line in front must be drawn in. 

A thrust-out or protruded abdomen is lazy slovenliness. 

The weight of the head and trunk should not be on one hip but on both. The weight need not be equally distributed to the two hips, but both must be up and ready for any load that may be assigned to them. Even more slovenly than a protruding abdomen is a knocked-down hip. With most beginners the knees are inclined to shake, but this form of gesture is not effective. In standing one knee must be stiff, and most of the time the other should be also, although as in the case of the hips the weight need not be equally borne by the two knees. 

Each knee must hold itself in readiness to bend, at an instant's warning, as is necessary in stooping or walking; but in all other cases may give its entire attention to its chief business of holding the body upright until the speech has been finished. If a speaker has two feet, he should stand on both of them; if he has but one, he is entitled to the consolation that he can learn to use it more effectively than most of us do both of ours. It is the duty of both feet to assist in every way possible in the delivery of a speech. They can do this best by keeping themselves as much as possible out of the picture and supporting the body in such a way that it can most easily shift the weight from one foot to the other or get itself most easily and least obtrusively from where it is to where it wants to go. If one foot dangles in front or at the side, or reaches backward and rests itself upon its toes, it is not only failing to discharge its full duty in the case but is drawing to itself attention that rightfully belongs to the message and that its own intrinsic attractiveness does not warrant.

Physical Response 145 

HOW TO STAND 

While a speaker is supposed to stand on both feet instead of one, the weight is very seldom if ever equally distributed. Most of the weight is on one foot, the foot that at the time is under the center of gravity. The other foot, while not to be held up and not to be limp, bears only its own weight or at most the weight of the leg it belongs to. The only merit of any particular position of the feet above that of any other is that it best enables one to stand at ease and renders it easiest to shift from one foot to the other and to make such other movements as may be desirable. Now, what is this particular position? The two feet should not be so close together as to leave the impression that there is only one and thereby the impression that one's equilibrium is unstable, nor should they be sprawled so far apart as to make one appear ungraceful. All the time one should stand at ease. Neither the toes nor the heels should be in line. The feet are not to be parallel. 

Just how far apart the feet are to be is an individual matter, depending partly upon the length and weight of the body, and partly perhaps upon individual preference. One foot should be a little in advance of the other and not parallel with it but turned at an angle so that a straight line from toe to heel of the advanced foot would, if continued, run through the heel of the other foot. The distance between them ranges roughly from two to six inches at the closest point. In this position it is natural and easy to shift the weight from one foot to the other or to step either forward or backward. For illustration, if one's weight is nearly all on his left foot and the right heel is pointing toward his left heel, one may easily and gracefully change either forward or backward. If he wishes to change forward, he will lean forward and to the right, shifting the weight

from the left to the right foot and at the same time bringing his left foot forward so that its heel will point back toward the right heel; or if he wishes a backward change, the right foot, which is now free, may be brought backward a few inches so that the heel of the left foot will point toward the heel of the right, and the weight may either remain on the left foot or be transferred to the right. This gives us four positions of the feet: (1) Right foot forward with weight on the left. (2) Right foot forward with weight on the right. 

Left foot forward with weight on the right. (4) Left foot forward with weight on the left.

It will readily be seen that certain changes can be directly made, but others only indirectly. For illustration, one may change directly from (1) to (2), (2) to (1), (1) to (4), (4) to (1), (2) to (3), (3) to (2), (3) to (4), and (4) to (3). The other position changes can be made only indirectly. For illustration, if one wished to change from (1) to (3), it would be necessary for him to shift first from (1) to (2) and then from (2) to (3). In any of these positions it is better to let most of the weight rest upon the ball of the foot rather than upon the heel. When one stands with most of his weight resting upon his heels, he has less animation and finds any transition more difficult. This is what we mean when we say that the man who does things has to be up on his toes.

INSTRUCTION HELPS 

These are only very general directions, not hard and fast but flexible. They are for the speaker; he is not for them. If he is thoroughly convinced that he can do better without them, then they are not for him. A beginner, however, should not decide too hastily that his own way is better than that approved by the great speakers and the great teachers. Although the book

Physical Response 147 way might at first seem very unnatural to him and might require of him a deal of time and energy, yet in the end it might be better. This is true not only of the beginner, but even more true of an experienced and successful speaker who has had no instruction along this line. An old adage is worth remembering in this connection, "The good is the enemy of the best." 

The student can seldom afford to "let well enough alone." Yet if one ever becomes completely satisfied that rules are only a handicap to him, that his own way is more effective than the rule way, then he must follow his own way. All the rules set down in the books are only to make us more at ease, more graceful, less awkward. In a great majority of cases, in all except one in about ten thousand, they succeed. Occasionally they fail. If one turns out to be the one in ten thousand, if he has really given the rules a fair trial and they have failed, he is satisfied that he cannot make them work, they make him less at ease, less graceful, more awkward, then they are not for him, he is at liberty to throw them all to the winds. If he turns out to be only a very mediocre person with cranky notions, as he is most likely to do, the rules will lose nothing by his throwing them to the winds. If on the other hand he turns out to be a genius, which is by no means impossible, then he will go ahead and make a new way, the rules of which will be set down in books for the rest of us to study. Anyway, it is always better for a speaker to be well at ease in doing an awkward thing gracefully, than to be ill at ease in doing a graceful thing awkwardly.

RESERVED FORCE 

At all times, the speaker will stand and move and speak, not as if he is spending his last ounce of energy but rather as if he is holding tons of it in reserve. An

audience is never so much impressed by what a speaker says as by what it thinks he might say. It is impressed but little by what the speaker does, but tremendously by what it thinks he is able to do. It is reserved force, energy unexpended, that makes poise or balance. It is this reserved force, energy unexpended, that all of us are influenced by. We are all alike. The most cowardly of us would walk up courageously and spit his contempt right into the very mouth of a cannon that had just been shot off, but the bravest among us would tremblingly lift his cap to a pocket pistol he knows is loaded. So it is with speakers.
