A big man who stamps and struts and pounds and bellows so as to leave the impression that he is all in, is not half so big as the little man who quietly but earnestly makes his speech so as to make us think he has ten times as much to say when it is needed. Any student who masters the psychology of reserved force will find himself well on the way toward being an effective speaker.

BODY MOVEMENTS 

In addition to the movements of individual organs, like hands, feet, eyes, shoulders, there are others that involve the entire body. The speaker should spare no pains to perfect the movements of the individual parts of the body and also those of the body as a whole, which for lack of a better term we are here designating as body movements.

THE INTRODUCTION 

Until the speaker has been announced he should be as little in evidence as possible. Up to this time he is without authority. The audience is not his, nor the occasion. Up to this time the presiding officer is in full charge. It is his business to introduce the speaker. 

This he will do, at least he will try to do, in a manner

Physical Response 

149 that will be for the good of the occasion. If it is well done, and the presiding officer always tries to do it well, it will be also for the good of the speaker. It means much to a speaker to be well introduced, to be properly presented to the audience. As soon as the speaker's name has been announced, even if the presiding officer has a word or two yet to say, he should rise. This moment he can use to advantage in getting a deep breath and in putting himself in the right posture. He rises with his heels close together and stands with his eyes fixed on the presiding officer until he has said his final word. Then with a very slight inclination of the body and with his head bending forward he acknowledges the courtesy of the introduction by saying, Mr. 

President, Madam President, Mr. Chairman, or whatever may on this occasion be the proper designation of the master of ceremonies. Some speakers ignore the presiding officer altogether, but this is not in good taste, it is a discourtesy not in place even if the introduction had been poorly done. This lack of courtesy to the presiding officer is not only an injustice to him but will be noted by the audience in a way that will not help the speaker. 

GETTING TO THE FRONT 

After this salutation to the one in charge, when the speaker turns his eyes upon the audience, he takes complete charge of everything and of everybody. Now he is master of the situation. He steps toward the front of the platform in a manner neither defiant nor apologetic, but with complete confidence both in himself and in the message he is about to deliver. If he does not have this confidence and assurance, it would be better for him to go back to his study and do some more work on himself and his message; if he does have it, it will not be long until the audience have it, and then everything will be easy. If a speaker can choose his sitting position on

the platform, it is better to be near one side rather than right in the center facing the audience. It is better for two reasons: On the side he is least in view, the audience have not quite so good an opportunity to discover all his limitations and short-comings before he begins his speech. Also, many speakers prefer to walk out on a diagonal rather than on a direct line. 

Even the manner of advancing to the front is worth a word or two. The speaker should walk out as if he had some strength and vitality left in him. He must be wide-awake. Audiences do not like a sleepy speaker. 

If any sleeping is to be done, they claim the privilege of doing it themselves. On the other hand he is not expected to make too great a show of energy before he begins his work. I have a lecturer friend who is not satisfied to walk out, he runs out. That is not necessary. Presumably the audience, since they are all in their seats, will remain at least till he gets started. He is not going to extinguish a fire or to rescue a drowning man, but to tell some intelligent people what he has been thinking. So in going out to the front he must not compel the audience to wonder whether he is entirely awake, or to guess whether he is running after something or from something. The medium is usually better than either extreme.

BEGINNING THE SPEECH 

The same rule holds for beginning his speech. When the speaker reaches the part of the platform he has chosen and stops, the audience know that it is about time for the speech to begin. He will not be so deliberate as to suggest indifference. On the other hand, he will not bound into his opening sentence as if he were speaking against time. There are men so anxious to get their speech started that they begin it while they are walking out to the front. It is never that important.

Physical Response 151 Or, it might be more nearly correct to say, it is never that unimportant. First, one must get out to the front and stop. And stopping is no unimportant matter. 

The best way to stop is just to quit walking when one has walked far enough. The worst way to stop is for the speaker to give it special attention, look down at his feet as if to see whether he has brought all of them, or as if he is showing the audience that he knows how they should be adjusted. It is an important matter to stop with the feet properly placed. Otherwise the speaker can have no balance, poise, or ease; but he must place them unconsciously, with his eyes still on the audience. 

Then after he stops, it is good both for the audience and for him that he should stand for two or four or five seconds without saying a word. The audience will be getting themselves ready for the message they have come out to hear, and he will be getting himself and them a little better in hand. This will give him time to raise his chest, to lift his head, to take another deep breath, to draw in the waist line, and altogether to perfect his posture. Many speakers count these few seconds of watchful, silent waiting the most valuable time of their entire address. They may or may not be. 

If during this time the speaker's mind is a blank, it would be better to be going ahead with his speech. 

What must be in his mind as he waits? In my judgment it should be a mental attitude that he really has and sincerely desires to communicate to the audience. 

What is this mental attitude? First, it is an appreciation of the compliment the audience have paid him in coming out to hear him. And it certainly is a compliment to any speaker to have an audience come out to listen to him. The speaker who does not appreciate it is lacking in one of the essentials. In these few seconds also there may very appropriately be in his mind a recognition that the audience before him is extending to

him a challenge. Although their lips are closed and no audible sound is made, he hears or should hear this challenge: Here we are. We have come out to hear something worth while. Give it to us if you have it; or if you do not, let us go home right now. Then as the finish of his mental attitude the speaker should, mentally at least, accept this challenge. It will do him good to entertain this mental attitude. And if he feels like expressing all of it to his audience sincerely, quietly, modestly, it will lack a lot of being the worst possible way to begin his speech. 

INTRODUCTORY REMARKS 

Now he is ready, or almost ready, to begin. Only one other preliminary is possible. Except on extremely serious occasions audiences welcome a little pleasantry while the speaker is finding his way to the minds and hearts of his hearers. So, if a speaker deems it in order, can do it well, and knows when to stop, he may tell a story or indulge in a bit of wit or humor to let the listeners smile once before they begin to think. Some of this may seem a little remote from body movements. 

My apology is, I do not know how to speak of any movement except in connection with the thought or feeling it expresses. Any direction for coming out upon the platform or for standing on the platform would be meaningless apart from the mental state such movement or posture helps to express. It must be constantly kept in mind that all posture, gesture, movement, is either an aid to expression or it is nothing, worse than nothing. 

That is what is meant in saying that all these things together make up the physical response. Any position, attitude or movement that is the natural, appropriate response to a thought or feeling is an aid in expression. 

If it is not such a response, it is a hindrance.

Physical Response 

153 

THE RESPONSE THEORY 

Let us take a minute to refresh our minds on the response theory of the art of speaking, which this study is endeavoring to set forth: If in the presence of facts one's mind responds with their meaning; if to this meaning one's feeling responds with appropriate emotions; if to both the thoughts and emotions one is able to respond with the most suitable words; if to thoughts, emotions, words, one can give the proper vocal response; then if to thought, emotion, words, and tones one can make all the right physical responses and none of the wrong ones, that one may be said to have mastered the art of speaking. All these responses must be toward the same end. Or we may say, only one speech must be made at a time. If the words are saying one thing, the voice another, and the physical movements another, the result will be no speech, but babble. 

Every physical movement that says the same thing the words and tones say is a help. If the physical movement says something different or nothing, it might better be omitted. Posture and movement are not things good in themselves. They are always for expression.

GRACE 

A speaker must strive to be graceful all the time he is before the audience. He is never allowed to get his feet so tangled up that he will stumble over them. Nor is he permitted to make ungainly strides across the platform. He is never allowed to step with his right foot to the left in front of his left foot; nor must he step with his left foot to the right in front of his right foot. If he wishes to move to the right, he will first shift the weight to his left foot, leaving the right foot free. It will then step not directly to the right, but forward diagonally to

the right. By reversing these movements he may easily make his way toward the left. If he is already too far toward the front and wishes to move toward the right or left, he will first shift his weight to the left foot, allowing the right to step backward and to the right; or to the right foot, allowing the left to step backward and to the left.

AT HOME ON THE PLATFORM 

An audience likes to have a speaker seem to be at home on the platform. This he can do only by feeling at home there. For any of us to have this feeling we must have experience on the platform. This at-home feeling is helped along greatly by correct posture, poise, balance, confidence in oneself and his message, and being satisfied with the preparation he has made. Just how much experience a speaker should have is a question upon which speakers themselves do not agree, nor do the authorities. Clay tells us that he lost no opportunity to make a speech. Charles James Fox, a distinguished member of the English Parliament, tells us, -"During five whole sessions in Parliament I spoke every night but one, and I regret that I did not speak that night too." This is high authority and it is commended by great teachers. Personally, while I certainly would not condemn it, if a speaker were obliged to choose between many speeches with small preparation or few speeches with large preparation, for his good I would recommend him to choose the latter.

HOW TO IMPROVE 

With reference to all his body movements a speaker wishing to improve must be his own severest critic. He cannot hope to get it all from books, teachers, or classmates. He must criticize himself. He can easily see that leaning or lounging on a table or desk, clutching

Physical Response 155 the lapels of his coat, fumbling with a ring or watch chain, folding his arms behind him, twirling his thumbs in front of him, running his fingers through his hair too often or ramming his hands down into his pockets, all make speeches different from the one his words are making. Every position or movement that detracts from his real message would be better omitted. Every useless movement should be omitted, but under useful movements are included those that add to the speaker's comfort as well as those that aid him in expression. 

Another very undesirable platform movement is swaying from one side to the other or rhythmically rising upon the balls of the feet and settling the weight back upon the heels only to repeat and continue the repetition.

CONTROL OVER AN AUDIENCE 

How a speaker stands and what he does, or does not do, with his body, has even more effect than his words in controlling his audience. Audiences, as a rule, have only the best of intentions and are as anxious as the speaker himself to have him succeed. Occasionally, however, some members of it are thoughtless. Occasionally also the presiding officer is thoughtless. 

Sometimes when there is much confusion in an audience, he will turn it over to the speaker. That is not fair. 

He is shirking his responsibility. The presiding officer must have the audience perfectly quiet before he releases the speaker. The speaker is always entitled to speak his first words to a listening audience. The responsibility of keeping them listening is his own. If he fails to do it, no one else is to blame. Even when the presiding officer has done his full duty, it sometimes happens that confusion will arise before the speaker has had time to begin. Late arrivals may be coming in, some one may have found it necessary to leave just at

that time, or a table or a chair may have fallen. In such cases the speaker must just stand and wait. He will certainly not betray any great disappointment over having the audience wait a minute or two for his message. Nor will he scold anybody. It may be that no one was to blame. And even if he was, a scolding will not make him a better listener. Neither will he begin his speech. That would only add to the confusion. He will simply stand and wait. It will not be long. Audiences can stand any amount of scolding and railing and enjoy it. At least they laugh at it. But they cannot keep up the confusion very long when they see a speaker standing quietly, looking serious but goodnatured, indicating that he is ready to begin, that he does not wish to waste their time, that he wishes them to hear what he has to say, and that they are responsible for any delay that may occur. 

As a closing word on body movements, let the speaker make himself comfortable on the platform, be sure of his message, have plenty of confidence in himself, be sincerely interested in his audience, have a strong desire to help them, be really in earnest in every sentence and every word of his speech, and then allow his body, his entire body, to be wholly responsive to his thoughts, his feelings, his words, and his tones. 

THE FACE 

Any one who has listened to a radio speech while staring at a cabinet and then heard the same speech while looking into the face of the speaker has a good idea of the services that may be rendered by facial expression. 

The muscles that control the lips, the chin, the nose, the forehead, and the brows are able to make a large contribution to the expression of thought and even a larger one to the expression of feeling.

Physical Response 

157 

THE EYES 

The eyes of orators and actors have received more praise than all other parts of the body together. Novelists have told stories about them, poets have sung about them, literature loves to tell of their conquests. Eyes have won battles that seemingly were lost. They have determined how debates should be decided. Most men know not their destiny until they see it in eyes. Most men never find their fortune till they read it in eyes. 

Of all the instruments of orators and actors the most powerful is said to be the eye. I have no desire to question this view. It will remain as true as it has ever been. The speaker will do well to know the power of the eye and to put himself in possession of as much of it as possible. I do ask permission to say one thing about the eye that is not universally understood. It is that in itself the eye is not very much. One removed from its socket is a very commonplace thing. The glory of the eye is all reflected. It all comes not from the eye but from the mind. If the mind is wonderful, so may be the eye. 

If the mind is a blank, so is the eye. If the mind is keen, the eye is keen. If the mind is deep, the eye is deep. If the mind is shallow, so is the eye. 

If the mind fights, the eye fights. If the mind loves, the eye loves. If the mind hates, the eye hates. If the mind worships, so may the eye. If the mind is sleepy, so will be the eye. The eye of the speaker has no influence over his audience unless his eye is influenced by his mind. We have all heard of the art of the eyes. 

Well, it is all meaningless and impotent, unless the eyes steal their magic from the mind. If eyes can do this, stay away from them unless you wish to be under their power. All artificial tricks of the eyes are ineffective. 

Eyes that are responsive to every intellectual distinction and to every emotion of the mind deserve all

the poems that have ever been written about them. 

The eyes are perhaps the largest windows through which the mind looks. Let us not pull the shades down. On the contrary, anything and everything that makes the eyes more responsive to the mind is invaluable to a speaker.

GESTURE 

We do not have complete unanimity of opinion as to the value of gesture. Some teachers and some excellent speakers contend that any use of bodily movement in the expression of thought and feeling is an admission of the inadequacy of our verbal language. This we may all readily grant. Verbal language is inadequate. It needs all the help it can get. Some contend that more work on one's words would make many of our gestures unnecessary. Let us grant this also. Let us spare no pains and labor to perfect our words and sentences. 

The more improvement in them the better; but after we have done our best in this respect we shall still be in need of all the additional help available. In every effort of the speaker to improve his language he should strive to make it so perfect that bodily movements will be unnecessary. It is always good to strive, but this goal can never be attained. The most perfect speakers find themselves constantly in need of supplementing words with physical movements. So, the sooner a speaker gives a little serious attention to gesture the better it will be for him.

There is no foundation in fact for the opinion some hold that gesture is superfluous and unnatural. The fact that words at their best are inadequate is proof enough that gesture is not superfluous. Nor is it unnatural. Indeed it is the most nearly natural thing that we know. Words are altogether artificial. Long before they were invented, our progenitors were express-

Physical Response 

159 ing their thoughts and feelings by means of movements. 

In this early life of the race the contents of consciousness were mostly feeling with but a minimum of thinking. 

When the race came into possession of more thought and more accurate and definite thought, it became necessary to invent a word language. So, if one wishes, instead of regarding gesture as a means of supplementing words, he may take the directly opposite view, that words are a means of supplementing gesture. 

WORDS AND GESTURES 

We must keep in mind, of course, that words are at their best in the expression of thoughts, and need help most in the expression of feeling; and that gesture is at its best in expressing feeling, and renders much less aid in the expression of thought. We must remember that such feelings as are called emotions do not exist separately and independently but always in connection with thoughts. Whether there can ever be any thought that is not attended by at least a shade of feeling is yet a debatable question in psychology, we do know, however, that art, every form of art, consists, not in the expression of pure thought, if there is such a thing, but in the expression of thought that is attended by feeling. 

We know too that some thoughts are attended by the merest tinge of feeling, and that others are completely saturated, through and through, with feeling. It is the latter, rather than the former, that need the aid of gesture.

HOW MUCH MOVEMENT 

No specific rule can be laid down as to how much movement, how much gesture, a speaker should use. 

There is no rule that would be good for all subjects and all speakers. We do know that meaningless prancing back and forth on the platform is not good. It draws

to itself attention that belongs to the speech. It is easy also for the speaker to go to the opposite extreme and make no movements. Naturally, it will depend largely on the subject. If the subject is one of pure thought, or almost pure thought, but few gestures will be needed, while if the subject is highly emotional, more movement will be in place. Just as naturally also it will depend largely on the speaker. If he is coldly intellectual, you need not expect many gestures, while if he is strongly emotional, he will be inclined to gesture more. Which kind of subject is better? And which kind of speaker?

First, from the standpoint of the audience, I would strongly urge them to like or to learn to like both kinds of subjects and both kinds of speakers. The most admirable, the most lovable, of all audiences is the group of people that show clearly and unmistakably that of all possible subjects their preference is for the one the speaker before them is about to discuss, and that of all speakers their preference is for the one about to talk to them. From the standpoint of the speaker 

I would say about the same thing. Every speaker should have both thought and feeling. He must show clearly and unmistakably that he is capable of making not only the intellectual response to the facts but also the emotional response to his own thoughts, and then the physical response to both. I am inclined to believe that this also is wholesome advice: if a speaker is predominantly intellectual, he should strive to be more emotional, and if he is predominantly emotional, he should spend more time and effort in thinking. We cannot please everybody all the time. If we please a few people some of the time, we have reason to be encouraged. It is always good to avoid extremes. One should try to find that happy medium between the dryas-dust stuff of the brutally intellectual and the mushy stuff of the insanely emotional. But here again hear

Physical Response 

161 

Portia say: "If to do were as easy" etc. But if I were pressed to say which is better, provided a speaker can have only one and not both, I should unhesitatingly say thought. A speaker destitute of emotion, or as nearly destitute as is possible, will be listened to by most audiences if he really has thoughts; but a speaker who is all feeling and is without thought is always and everywhere intolerable.

As to the amount of movement, each speaker must make the final decision for himself. Each must keep in mind that gesture may be a powerful factor in expression and that the more adequately he expresses himself the more interesting and effective he will be as a speaker. 

So, in accord with this principle every speaker should toil tirelessly to make his body as a whole and every part of it assist in his speech. We must keep in mind also that every movement or gesture that is out of time or out of place is merely another speaker, and a loud speaker, unsaying what the man before the audience is trying to get said in words. Some divide gestures into three classes, good, bad, and indifferent; but there are in reality only two kinds, good ones and bad ones. All indifferent gestures are bad ones.

With reference to the amount of gesturing there are two extreme types of speakers. In one type the body and every part of it, hands, arms, head, face, eyes, shoulders, trunk, hips, legs, and feet, are alive and alert, ready instantly to respond and do respond to every shade of thought and feeling in the speaker's mind. In my judgment this type of speaker should be the ideal of every beginner. If he is to err on either side, and of necessity he must, it should be on the side of too much gesture rather than too little. In later years it will be easier for him to restrain an overresponsive live body than to stimulate an irresponsive dead one. The other type is made up of speakers who

depend but little upon bodily movements. They make few gestures. They depend upon the fitness of their words and the skill with which they utter them. This class includes some of the world's greatest orators. It is said that Demosthenes made but few movements. 

Dr. Lyman Abbott and Wendell Phillips made next to none.

PLANES OF GESTURE 

Three fairly well-defined planes of gesture have been recognized by teachers of speech, - central, higher, and lower. This diagram shows roughly at least these three Higher

Plane Central Plane Lower Plane planes. The angle is supposed to center in the breast a little below the shoulder of the speaker. Each of the three planes is included within an arc of approximately thirty degrees. In each plane is supposed to belong the movements aiding in the expression of a corresponding class of mental acts and states. 

Most of the gestures of all speakers are in the central plane, such movements as aid in common description, narration, and in the expression of unemotional thought, calm reasoning and such mental states as one wishes to communicate in conversation and ordinary,

Physical Response 163 unimpassioned public address. Many speakers never have occasion to get out of this plane. 

The higher plane begins at the upper boundary of the central and extends upward for another space of thirty degrees. It is the field in which occur all movements aiding in the expression of our higher sentiments, such as aspiration, hope, faith, benediction, patriotism, achievement, liberty, and all such feelings as pertain to home, heaven, duty, etc.

The lower plane begins at the lower boundary of the central and extends downward for another thirty degrees. It is the field of movements expressive of such lower sentiments as hate, revenge, aversion and all other malevolent feelings; also gloom, discouragement, despair, despondency, and horror. 

These planes are not arbitrary and artificial. They are natural. For illustration, a teacher before her classes or a doctor giving instructions to a nurse would very seldom if ever have occasion to move his hands except in the central plane. The same would be true of a preacher explaining to us the setting of his text and of a lawyer presenting his facts to the jury. When the ire of the preacher is aroused by our indifference to the higher things of life, he naturally expresses his disapproval and condemnation by hand movements in the lower plane. So does the prosecutor of a criminal.

Naturally also, when the preacher holds up to us the rewards of a higher life, his movements are instinctively in the higher plane. So also are those of a lawyer defending his client, when he pleads with the jury for sympathy and mercy, and appeals to all higher motives. 

HANDS AND ARMS 

The arms are for the most part merely instruments for taking the hands from their position of repose to the place where they are to do their work and back again to

their position of repose. It is the hand that renders the largest service as an aid to expression. It is said to be the most ingeniously constructed organ of the body. 

Mechanics has reached the goal of perfection when it has fashioned an instrument that can even poorly mimic the movements of the human hand. Old Anaxagoras exclaimed, "Animals would have been men, if they had had hands." Not altogether out of harmony with this ancient declaration, modern biologists are agreed that for man's brain development he is chiefly indebted to his hands. As an aid in expression the hand deserves all the study that has been devoted to it. It is most specifically the hand that is meant when we talk of organs of gesture. 66 

DELSARTE 

The disciples of Delsarte have given us a study of the hand as it moves in correlation with postures of the entire body and with other parts or organs of the body. 

The object of this study was to show the effectiveness of these bodily movements in expressing grace, beauty, and other æsthetic emotions. Recently in books on speech but little has been heard of Delsarte except in explosions of ridicule. Some teachers have coupled with Delsarte movements such expressions as "silly," "foolish," "crazy," etc. crazy," etc. So far as such criticism has been warranted it has been on unintelligent and mechanical efforts to teach these postures and movements to children and youths incapable of having or of understanding the emotions they were to express. For the most part the criticisms have been altogether unwarranted. Delsarte did render a real service. 

Emotions are evasive at best. We need all the help available in expressing them. The only criticisms in order are on movements that hinder rather than help in expression. Personally, although I have not availed

Physical Response 165 myself of its benefits, I believe that every actor and every speaker would be richly rewarded by a thorough and extended course in physical movements in relation to the emotions they express. Of course, any such study apart from the emotions would be only a waste of time. I lay no claim to beauty of posture or grace of movement, but I make this admission as an apology and not as a boast.

While all possible positions and movements of the hands are needed by actors and occasionally by some speakers, a book setting forth only the elements in the art of speaking will have room for only those positions and movements needed most frequently in ordinary public and less formal address. 

HAND POSITIONS 

The beginner and the experienced speaker alike must be familiar with at least six hand positions and their corresponding feelings. These are: (1) The Supine Hand, palm up. (2) The Prone Hand, palm down. (3) The Vertical Hand, palm in direction of other hand. (4) The Averse Hand, palm from you. (5) The Index Hand, the first finger pointing. (6) The Clenched Hand, made into a fist.

Any one who masters these six positions of the hand together with what each is supposed to express, makes the hand correctly responsive to the mental content, and becomes able to put every other part of the body instinctively in correlation with the hand position, will have a mastery of gesture superior to that of most speakers. 

1. The Supine Hand. In this position the palm is up and the hand is extended obliquely toward the front. 

The palm is not in a straight line with the forearm, but

is dropped, by bending the wrist, at considerable of an angle. This might very well be called the teaching hand. It is used when facts and principles are presented. It presents with approval or commends. It invites inspection, and recommends. It presents a proposition as being acceptable or even admirable. It reveals, makes clear, welcomes. It also gives and receives, asks and answers. It makes and grants concessions. It expresses submission and humility. It presents a thing as good, true, beautiful, or in any other way attractive. It exalts what it presents. 

2. The Prone Hand, extended the same as the Supine 

Hand but with the palm down, has various meanings. 

Some of them are directly opposite to that of the Supine 

Hand. It is used to express opposition, dislike, disapproval. Instead of lifting up in commendation, it presses down in condemnation. It conceals and suppresses what is presented as objectionable. It denies, rejects, reproves, and urges moral restraint or hesitation.

It is used also in commands or the expression of authority. Then it is used also in caresses and benedictions, and in the laying on of hands, as in healing. 

In its more intellectual significance it molds, traces out, shapes, and outlines. 

3. The Vertical Hand extends obliquely in front and has its palm facing the opposite side. It is less emotional and more intellectual than the two previously mentioned. It is used in making distinctions, in outlining boundaries, and in marking divisions or making separations. 

4. The Averse Hand. This is the open hand extended from you toward the front or in an oblique-front direction. It is highly emotional, expressing disapproval so strong as to amount to complete rejection. 

With it the speaker pushes away from him what is strongly objectionable.

Physical Response 167 5. The Index Hand. In this the whole arm is extended, with all the fingers closed except the forefinger, which is pointing toward some person or other object to which the speaker wishes to call special attention. It is distinctly a place or locative gesture. 

It is as little emotional as the vertical hand, and even more intellectual. It is a logical gesture, caring chiefly about meaning. It prevails in expository speaking and is prominent in debate. This hand position becomes a little more emotional when it warns, expostulates, cautions, rebukes, or commands. 

6. The Clenched Hand is so strongly emotional that it should not be used to excess. It expresses deep, personal approval or disapproval. With it a speaker can pledge equally his loyalty or his enmity. 

EXERCISES 

I. Go over again the sentiments, feelings, and ideas designated under Planes of Gesture and Hand Positions to see that you have a clear and correct notion of each. Then assume the attitude and make the movement or gesture that in your judgment is suitable in each case. In this exercise, as in all that follow, seek the criticism of teacher and classmates. Keep in mind that all the time the entire body must act as a whole. Every part must be properly correlated with every other part and with the whole body, or no gesture can be good. Also, the most important of all, see that every posture, movement, or gesture is the natural, spontaneous expression of its corresponding mental state. No gesture is an end in itself; it is always a means to an end, the mental state to be expressed. 

2. Take the posture, assume the attitude, and make the movement that you think would most naturally, spontaneously, gracefully, and forcefully express, or help to express, each of the following, keeping in mind head, shoulders, hips, trunk, legs, feet, and face: (1) That is true. (2) That is true and you know it. (3) Who said that was not true? (4) I did that.

(5) I did that and I would do it again. (6) Yes, I did that. What are you going to do about it? (7) I tell you, boys, we must stand by our team. 

As a preliminary to each of the preceding speeches and to each of the following, let the student who makes it first explain in a conversational way to teacher and classmates just what ideas and feelings he will try to express in making it. Criticisms and corrections will then be in order. After this another student intending to make the same speech will first tell the group what ideas, thoughts, and feelings he will try to express. 

Wherever possible to do so, different students should give variations in the content. Let each student be held responsible, not for what some one else thinks the speech means, but for expressing what he says it means. 

7. Imagine yourself in each of the following situations, assume the proper posture for it, have clearly in mind the thoughts and feelings belonging to it, make in from one to three short sentences the speech that best expresses these thoughts and feelings, allowing the body, every part of it, head, face, eyes, neck, shoulders, arms, hands, chest, lower trunk, hips, legs, and feet, to make its proper contribution to the expression. 

Teacher and class will make their criticisms, favorable and unfavorable. The wise critic will be able to find in every effort at least twice as much to commend as to censure; and every student caring at all for his own improvement will welcome all criticism, particularly if it points out his faults, and strive to benefit by it.

EXERCISES 1. What is the narrow meaning of gesture? The wider meaning? 

2. What is the real purpose of gesture? What is meant by saying that gesture, or physical movement, is a response? 

3. Why is gesture training necessary? What is your own opinion of the value of criticism? Of the value of a mirror?

4. Discuss poise or balance with reference to head, chest, back, abdomen, hips, knees, and feet. Illustrate in your own posture. 

5. Discuss particularly the positions of the feet of a speaker. 

Name the four positions of the feet. State and illustrate how changes may best be made from one position to another. Discuss the use and abuse of rules. 

6. Make clear what you mean by reserved force, and its value. 

7. Discuss the movements of a speaker previous to his introduction to the audience. Getting to the front. 

8. Show how posture and movement may aid expression. 

How they may hinder it. 

9. State again, clearly and fully, the response theory of speech. 

10. What would you say of grace? Of being at home on the platform? Of how often one should speak? Of how one may improve his movements? Of the influence of posture and movement in controlling an audience. 

II. Discuss the contribution that is made to a speech by the face. By the eyes. 

12. What is your own opinion of the value of gesture? Which most needs gesture, thought or feeling? Which is older, words or movements? 

13. Discuss the amount of movement necessary. The planes of gesture. The sentiments whose movements belong in each plane. The hands and arms. Delsarte. The six hand positions and the feelings they aid in expressing. Illustrate everything you describe.

CHAPTER XI SELECTIONS FOR ORAL READING 

THIS book is chiefly concerned with speaking; it is not primarily for the purpose of teaching reading. The author is, however, strongly of the opinion that oral reading is a most excellent preparation for speaking. 

It might be going a little too far to say that every good reader is a good speaker. I do feel safe, however, in saying that every good reader may become a good speaker. And then, oral reading is itself an art well worth cultivating. For one to be able to read well the selections contained in this chapter, to an audience large or small, so as to express their varied thought and feeling, with proper emphasis and in pleasing tones of voice, would itself be far from an insignificant accomplishment, even if he never made a speech. 

Nearly all that has been said on the subject of speaking is applicable also to reading. There is very little difference between oral reading and speaking. The only difference is in the intellectual response and the verbal response. In speaking these two responses are creative or productive, while in reading they are only reproductive. The emotional, vocal, and physical responses in reading are identical with those in speaking. 

Again, the superiority of the objective to the subjective attitude in speaking is just as obvious in reading. In attempting to read a selection to an individual or a group one's main concern must not be any content of his own mind or even of the page. He must have gone beyond this. The attitude for reading is identical 171

with that for speaking. That is, nearly the whole concern of the reader or speaker is for a certain state of mind which he wishes to induce the listener to have. 

All the qualities of voice, and its control, together with proper breathing for tone support, can be learned in oral reading as well as in speaking; and their use in the one differs not at all from their use in the other. 

It is inconceivable that there can ever be a time when good oral reading is not strongly in demand. It seems to me that every man, woman, and child should be ambitious to be a good oral reader. A long time ago this accomplishment was valued much more highly than it is today. Then it was the only kind of reading stressed in the schools. The inestimable value of expert silent reading was then entirely overlooked. When we came to recognize its value, which was a great step forward in education, we came to overlook entirely the value of oral reading, which in my judgment was a step backward. It is unfortunate that we have not yet learned how to place a new emphasis without destroying an old one. 

In a way I regret that we are not allowed space for more selections; but since these are easily accessible in separate manuals it is thought best not to print more of them here.

In addition to the few prose and poetry selections for general voice training, most of the others are speeches or orations. Most if not all of them are classics. No student need feel that time put upon them is wasted. 

If one wishes to do so, he may commit any or all of them to memory. One will be likely to come upon few things that one might more profitably store away in his mind. It never pays to spend time and energy in burdening our minds with trash. No one can afford it. It is just as easy, yes it is easier, to commit to memory the gems of literature, — masterful expressions of worthwhile thoughts and feelings. 

---

When it comes to reading these selections orally, the student must do his very best. That means much study to put ourselves in possession of the thought and feeling, and then to express both adequately. Some will prefer to read from memory. My own preference is that the student should read with the book open before him, but with the selection he reads so well in mind that only an occasional glance at the book will be necessary. 

When one comes to read in public, of course, he must have in mind only one thing, - that is, to have his audience think the thoughts and feel the emotions he is attempting to express. This kind of reading can come only after a large amount of study and practice. In all this practice one must of necessity give attention to many other things until they become habits. Here for convenience I am listing some of these other things that the student may profitably pay attention to in all his practice: 

1. Read only the selections you like. It will pay sometimes to try to make yourself like a selection that at first does not appeal to you; but if after sufficient effort you fail, then leave that selection out of your list. To read well you must like what you read; it will be all the better if you love it. 

2. Read only what you are in a mood for or are able to put yourself in the mood for. 

3. Look after your breathing. Master diaphragmic or abdominal breathing. Learn to feel that every syllable and every sound you utter is supported by the diaphragm. Learn to lift every tone up with the diaphragm instead of trying to push it out with the throat. 

Practice deep breathing till it becomes a habit. 

4. Learn to feel a wide open throat. Keep the throat perfectly relaxed. Teach your throat to keep itself entirely out of the way of the tones as they come up

and out. The throat can very soon wear itself out by attempting to do the work of the diaphragm. The diaphragm needs all the work it can get, and the throat all the resting. 

5. Enunciate every sound perfectly. Do not be satisfied with an approximation of a sound; make the exact sound. Articulate, that is, join together, the sounds, syllables, and words properly. See that you pronounce every word correctly. 

6. Practice the exercises that will give your voice resonance. See that all m, n, ng, and I sounds reverberate in the nasal and head cavities. 

7. Cultivate modulation. See that your voice feels at home in all the pitches of your range, and that it is able to pass easily and naturally from any one pitch to any other. Avoid monotony. 

8. Master the inflections, rising and falling. Do not use one when the other is needed. 

9. Look after your phrasing, using a group of words as a single word when they should together express a single idea, or when their individual ideas do not need to be distinguished. 

10. Make your emphasis of words and phrases express the corresponding correlation of their ideas. Note carefully all transitions of thought and feeling, and see that your tones express them. 

Be noble! and the nobleness that lies 

In other men, sleeping but never dead, Will rise in majesty to meet thine own. 

- LOWELL. 

The Lord is my shepherd; I shall not want. He maketh me to lie down in green pastures; he leadeth me beside the still waters. He restoreth my soul; he

leadeth me in the paths of righteousness for his name's sake. Yea, though I walk through the valley of the shadow of death, I will fear no evil; for thou art with me; thy rod and thy staff they comfort me. Thou preparest a table before me in the presence of mine enemies; thou anointest my head with oil; my cup runneth over. Surely goodness and mercy shall follow me all the days of my life; and I will dwell in the house of the Lord for ever.

Thou art the ruins of the noblest man 

That ever lived in the tide of times.

A GRAMMAR LESSON 

Little owlet in the glen, I'm ashamed of you;

You are ungrammatical 

In speaking as you do. 

You should say, "To whom! to whom!" 

Not, "To who! to who!" 

Your small friend, Miss Katy-did, May be green, 'tis true. 

But you never heard her say, "Katy do! she do!" 

THE RECESSIONAL 

God of our fathers, known of old,

Lord of our far-flung battle-line, Beneath whose awful hand we hold 

Dominion over palm and pine 

Lord God of Hosts, be with us yet, Lest we forget lest we forget!

AMERICA - RUDYARD KIPLING. 

I do not know beneath what sky 

Nor on what seas shall be thy fate; 

I only know it shall be high, I only know it shall be great.

RICHARD HOVEY. 

Roll on, thou deep and dark blue ocean, roll! 

Ten thousand fleets sweep over thee in vain ;

Man marks the earth with ruin - his control Stops with the shore; upon the watery plain, The wrecks are all thy deed, nor doth remain

A shadow of man's ravage, save his own,

When for a moment, like a drop of rain, He sinks into thy depths with bubbling groan,

Without a grave, unknell'd, uncoffin'd, and unknown. 

BYRON. 

The heavens declare the glory of God, and the firmament showeth his handiwork. Day unto day uttereth speech, and night unto night showeth knowledge.

There is no speech nor language where their voice is not heard. Their line is gone out through all the earth, and their words to the end of the world. In them hath he set a tabernacle for the sun, which is as a bridegroom coming out of his chamber, and rejoiceth as a strong man to run a race. His going forth is from the end of the heaven, and his circuit unto the ends of it; and there is nothing hid from the heat thereof. 

The law of the Lord is perfect, converting the soul; the testimony of the Lord is sure, making wise the simple. The statutes of the Lord are right, rejoicing the heart; the commandment of the Lord is pure, enlightening the eyes. The fear of the Lord is clean, enduring forever; the judgments of the Lord are true and righteous altogether. More to be desired are they than gold, yea, than much fine gold; sweeter also than honey and the honeycomb. Moreover, by them is thy servant warned; and in keeping of them there is great reward. 

Who can understand his errors? Cleanse thou me from secret faults; keep back thy servant also from presumptuous sins; let them not have dominion over me; then shall I be upright, and I shall be innocent from the great transgression. Let the words of my mouth and the meditation of my heart be acceptable in thy sight, O Lord, my strength and my redeemer.

The Nineteenth Psalm.

SONG 

Stay, stay at home, my heart, and rest;

Home-keeping hearts are happiest, For those that wander they know not where 

Are full of trouble and full of care; 

To stay at home is best. 

Weary and homesick and distressed, They wander east, they wander west, And are baffled and beaten and blown about

By the winds of the wilderness and doubt; 

To stay at home is best. 

Then stay at home, my heart, and rest; 

The bird is safest in its nest; 

O'er all that flutter their wings and fly 

A hawk is hovering in the sky; 

To stay at home is best. 

- LONGFELLOW. 

LIGHT 

The night has a thousand eyes, And the day but one,

Yet the light of the whole world dies 

With the setting sun.

The mind has a thousand eyes, And the heart but one,

Yet the light of the whole life dies 

When love is done.

FRANCIS BOURDILLON.

WARREN'S ADDRESS AT BUNKER HILL 

Stand! the ground's your own, my braves! 

Will ye give it up to slaves? 

Will ye look for greener graves? 

Hope ye mercy still? 

What's the mercy despots feel? 

Hear it in that battle peal! 

Read it on yon bristling steel! 

Ask it -ye who will. 

Fear ye foes who kill for hire? 

Will ye to your homes retire? 

Look behind you! They're afire! 

And before you, see 

Who have done it! From the vale 

On they come and will ye quail? 

Leaden rain and iron hail 

Let their welcome be! 

In the God of battles trust!

Die we may and die we must; 

But, O, where can dust to dust

Be consigned so well, As where heaven its dews shall shed

On the martyred patriot's bed, And the rocks shall raise their head, Of his deeds to tell? 

-JOHN PIERPONT. 

AFTER BLENHEIM 

It was a summer evening; 

Old Kasper's work was done, And he before his cottage-door 

Was sitting in the sun;

And by him sported on the green 

His little grandchild, Wilhelmine.

She saw her brother Peterkin

Roll something large and round, That he beside the rivulet, In playing there had found: 

She ran to ask what he had found,

That was so large and smooth and round. 

Old Kasper took it from the boy, Who stood expectant by;

And then the old man shook his head, And with a natural sigh, "Tis some poor fellow's skull," said he, "Who fell in the great victory. "I find them in my garden,

For there's many here about; 

And often, when I go to plough,

The ploughshare turns them out; 

For many thousand men," said he, "Were slain in that great victory." "Now tell us what 'twas all about," 

Young Peterkin, he cries; 

And little Wilhelmine looks up 

With wonder-waiting eyes; "Now tell us all about the war, And what they fought each other for." "It was the English," Kasper cried, "Who put the French to rout;

But what they fought each other for 

I could not well make out; 

But everybody said," quoth he, "That 'twas a famous victory.

66 

My father lived at Blenheim then,

Yon little stream hard by ; 

They burned his dwelling to the ground, And he was forced to fly;

So with his wife and child he fled, Nor had he where to rest his head. "With fire and sword the country round 

Was wasted far and wide, And many a childing mother then 

And new-born baby, died;

But things like that, you know, must be 

At every famous victory. "They say it was a choking sight

After the field was won; 

For many thousand bodies here 

Lay rotting in the sun :

But things like that, you know, must be 

After a famous victory. "Great praise the Duke of Marlborough won 66 

And our good Prince Eugene.' 

Why, 'twas a very wicked thing!" 

Said little Wilhelmine. "Nay, nay, my little girl," quoth he, "It was a famous victory. "And everybody praised the Duke, Who this great fight did win." "But what good came of it at last?" 

Quoth little Peterkin. "Why, that I cannot tell," said he; "But 'twas a famous victory.' 

- ROBERT SOUTHEY.

MY SHIPS 

If all the ships I have at sea 

Should come a-sailing home to me, Ah well! the harbor could not hold

So many ships as there would be, If all my ships came home to me. 

If half my ships now out at sea 

Should come a-sailing home to me, Ah well! I should have wealth as great

As any king that sits in state, So rich the treasure there would be In half my ships now out at sea. 

If but one ship I have at sea 

Should come a-sailing home to me, Ah well! the storm clouds then might frown, For if the others all went down, Still rich and glad and proud I'd be, If that one ship came home to me. 

If that one ship went down at sea, And all the others came to me,

Weighed down with gems and wealth untold, Of riches, glory, honor, gold, The poorest soul on earth I'd be, If that one ship came not to me. 

Oh skies, be calm! oh, winds, blow free! 

Blow all my ships safe home to me; 

But if thou sendest some awrack, To never more come sailing back, Send any all that skim the sea, But send my love ship back to me. 

-ELLA WHEELER WILCOX.

THE GLOVE AND THE LIONS 

King Francis was a hearty king, and loved a royal sport, And one day as his lions fought, sat looking on the court;

The nobles filled the benches, with the ladies in their pride,

And amongst them sat the Count de Lorge, with one for whom he sighed : 

And truly 'twas a gallant thing to see that crowning show,

Valor and love, and a king above, and the royal beasts below.

Romped and roared the lions, with horrid laughing jaws; 

They bit, they glared, gave blows like beams, a wind went with their paws;

With wallowing might and stifled roar they rolled on one another, Till all the pit with sand and mane was in a thunderous smother,

The bloody foam above the bars came whisking through the air; 

Said Francis then, "Faith, gentlemen, we're better here than there!" 

De Lorge's love o'erheard the king, a beauteous, lively dame, With smiling lips and sharp bright eyes, which always seemed the same; 

She thought, "The Count, my lover, is brave as brave can be, He surely would do wondrous things to show his love for me;

King, ladies, lovers, all look on; the occasion is divine ; 

I'll drop my glove, to prove his love; great glory will be mine!" 

She dropped her glove to prove his love, then looked on him and smiled;

He bowed, and in a moment leaped among the lions wild;

The leap was quick, return was quick, he has regained his place, Then threw the glove, - but not with love, in the lady's face. 

right "By Heaven!" said Francis, "rightly done!" and he rose from where he sat ; "No love," quoth he, "but vanity, sets love a task like that." 

LEIGH HUNT. 

THOU, TOO, SAIL ON! 

Sail forth into the sea, O ship! 

Through wind and wave, right onward steer!

The moistened eye, the trembling lip, Are not the signs of doubt or fear. 

Sail forth into the sea of life, O gentle, loving, trusting wife, And safe from all adversity 

Upon the bosom of that sea 

Thy comings and thy goings be! 

For gentleness and love and trust 

Prevail o'er angry wave and gust; 

And in the wreck of noble lives 

Something immortal still survives! 

Thou, too, sail on, O Ship of State! 

Sail on, O Union, strong and great!

Humanity with all its fears, With all the hopes of future years, Is hanging breathless on thy fate! 

We know what Master laid thy keel,

What Workmen wrought thy ribs of steel, 

Who made each mast, and sail, and rope, What anvils rang, what hammers beat, In what a forge and what a heat 

Were shaped the anchors of thy hope! 

Fear not each sudden sound and shock, 'Tis of the wave and not the rock; 'Tis but the flapping of the sail, And not a rent made by the gale! 

In spite of rock and tempest's roar, In spite of false lights on the shore, Sail on, nor fear to breast the sea!

Our hearts, our hopes, are all with thee, Our hearts, our hopes, our prayers, our tears, Our faith triumphant o'er our fears, Are all with thee, - are all with thee!

HENRY W. LONGFELLOW. 

BREAK, BREAK, BREAK 

Break, break, break, On thy cold gray stones, O Sea! 

And I would that my tongue could utter 

The thoughts that arise in me. 

O, well for the fisherman's boy, That he shouts with his sister at play! 

O, well for the sailor lad, That he sings in his boat on the bay! 

And the stately ships go on 

To their haven under the hill; 

But O for the touch of a vanished hand, And the sound of a voice that is still!

Break, break, break, At the foot of thy crags, O Sea! 

But the tender grace of a day that is dead 

Will never come back to me. 

- ALFRED TENNYSON. 

SENCE YOU WENT AWAY 

Seems lak to me de stars don't shine so bright, Seems lak to me de sun done loss his light, Seems lak to me der's nothin' goin' right, Sence you went away. 

Seems lak to me de sky ain't half so blue, Seems lak to me dat ev'thing wants you, Seems lak to me that I don't know what to do, Sence you went away.

Seems lak to me dat ev'thing is wrong, Seems lak to me de day's jes twice as long, Seems lak to me de bird's forgot his song, Sence you went away.

Seems lak to me I jes can't he'p but sigh, Seems lak to me ma th'oat keeps gittin' dry, Seems lak to me a tear stays in ma eye, Sence you went away. 

-JAMES WELDON JOHNSON. 

THE KITCHEN CLOCK 

Knitting is the maid o' the kitchen, Milly,

Doing nothing sits the choir boy, Billy: "Seconds reckoned, seconds reckoned, Sixty in it, Milly, Billy, Billy, Milly,

Tick-tock, tock-tick, Nick-knock, knock-nick,

Knockety-nick, nickety-knock," 

Goes the kitchen clock.

Something's happened, very red is Milly, Billy boy is looking very silly; "Pretty misses, plenty kisses;

Make it twenty, take a plenty. 

Billy, Milly, Milly, Billy, Right-left, left-right, That's right, all right,

Knockety-nick, nickety-knock," 

Goes the kitchen clock. 

Week's gone, still they're sitting, Milly, Billy; 

O the winter winds are wondrous chilly! "Winter weather, close together; 

Wouldn't tarry, better marry. 

Milly, Billy, Two-one, one-two, Don't wait, 'twon't do,

Knockety-nick, nickety-knock," 

Goes the kitchen clock. 

Winters two have gone, and where is Milly? 

Spring has come again, and where is Billy! "Give me credit, for I did it: 

Treat me kindly, Mind you wind me. 

Mister Billy, Mistress Milly, My-O, O-my,

By-by, by-by, Nickety-knock, cradle rock," 

Goes the kitchen clock. 

- JOHN VANCE CHENEY.

THE LAST LEAF 

I saw him once before,

As he passed by the door; 

And again

The pavement stones resound 

As he totters o'er the ground 

With his cane. 

They say that in his prime,

Ere the pruning-knife of time 

Cut him down, Not a better man was found 

By the crier on his round 

Through the town. 

But now he walks the streets, And he looks at all he meets 

So forlorn; 

And he shakes his feeble head, That it seems as if he said, "They are gone. 

The mossy marbles rest

On the lips that he has press'd 

In their bloom; 

And the names he loved to hear 

Have been carved for many a year 

On the tomb. 

My grandmamma has said 

Poor old lady! she is dead 

Long ago - 

That he had a Roman nose 

And his cheek was like a rose 

In the snow.

But now his nose is thin, And it rests upon his chin 

Like a staff; 

And a crook is in his back, And a melancholy crack 

In his laugh. 

I know it is a sin 

For me to sit and grin 

At him here; 

But the old three-corner'd hat 

And his breeches, and all that, Are so queer! 

And if I should live to be

The last leaf upon the tree 

In the spring, Let them smile, as I do now,

At the old forsaken bough 

Where I cling. 

- OLIVER WENDELL HOLMES. 

BALLAD NONSECTARIAN 

A sparrow who lived in the eaves of a church, And whose ideas were somewhat erratic,

Met a pigeon one day from a church o'er the way 

Whose opinions were very dogmatic.

They stopped in their search for a midday repast, And in hunting they both were astute; 

And, I hate to relate, they engaged in debate 

That led to a frightful dispute.

Quoth the pigeon, "I hold theological views Considered both learned and wise, So without more delay, I will show you today 

The only straight road to the skies."

Said the sparrow, "Your effort would be unrepaid, For I follow a different road, And it causes me grief to hear your belief 

In such a ridiculous code." 

Then without more ado, I am sorry to say,

They engaged in a battle most gory, Till each was bereft of his senses and breath,

And the curtain here falls on their story. Envoy 

Now a strange little bird from a blossoming tree

Had witnessed the scene with surprise, Then with unfettered wing and a heart that could sing

He took the straight road to the skies. 

-JULIA CLOPTON CRESAK in the Saturday Evening Post. 

THE ISLE OF LONG AGO 

O, a wonderful stream is the river of Time As it runs through the realm of tears, With a faultless rhythm and a musical rhyme, And a boundless sweep and a surge sublime,

As it blends with the Ocean of Years. 

How the Winters are drifting, like flakes of snow, And the Summer like buds between,

And the year in the sheaf; so they come and they go, On the river's breast, with its ebb and flow, As it glides in the shadow and sheen. 

There's a magical isle up the river Time, Where the softest of airs are playing; 

There's a cloudless sky and a tropical clime, And a song as sweet as a vesper chime, And the Junes with the roses are straying.

And the name of that isle is the Long Ago, And we bury our treasures there; 

There are brows of beauty and bosoms of snow;

There are heaps of dust, - but we loved them so! 

There are trinkets and tresses of hair; 

There are fragments of song that nobody sings, And a part of an infant's prayer;

There's a lute-unswept, and a harp without strings ; 

There are broken vows and pieces of rings, And the garments she used to wear.

There are hands that are waved when the fairy shore 

By the mirage is lifted in air; 

And we sometimes hear through the turbulent roar 

Sweet voices we heard in the days gone before,

When the wind down the river is fair. 

O, remember'd for aye be the blessed isle, All the day of our life until night;

When the evening comes with its beautiful smile, And our eyes are closing to slumber awhile, May that Greenwood of Soul be in sight!

BAYARD TAYLOR. 

THE THROSTLE 

"Summer is coming, summer is coming,

I know it, I know it, I know it, Light again, leaf again, life again, love again!” 

Yes, my wild little Poet.

Sing the new year in under the blue, Last year you sang it as gladly, "New, new, new, new!" Is it then so new 

That you should carol so madly?

"Love again, song again, nest again, young again," 

Never a prophet so crazy! 

And hardly a daisy as yet, little friend, See, there is hardly a daisy. "Here again, here, here, here, happy year!" 

O warble unchidden, unbidden! 

Summer is coming, is coming, my dear, And all the winters are hidden. 

- ALFRED TENNYSON. 

LIFE, LOVE, AND DEATH 

Living and loving and dying,

Life is complete in the three, Smiling or sobbing or sighing, Which is for you or for me? 

Hoping and struggling and striving,

Dreaming success by and by; 

But whether we're driven or driving, We live and we love and we die. 

Aiming and hitting and missing,

Life is complete in the three, The fickle world praising or hissing, Which is for you or for me? 

Striding or limping or creeping, Time drives us heartlessly by; 

Meeting and parting and weeping,

We live and we love and we die. 

Yearning, rejoicing, and mourning,

Life is complete in the three, Sackcloth or garland adorning, Which is for you and for me?

The web of our little day stretched 

Meshes a sob or a sigh; 

Joyful or joyless or wretched,

We live and we love and we die. 

Wishing and fearing and fretting, Life is complete in the three.

The world's remembrance or forgetting, Which is for you or for me? 

Gnarled and knotted and tangled

The skeins of our little lives lie; 

Mud-spattered or jewel-bespangled,

We live and we love and we die. 

-JAMES W. FOLEY. 

CROSSING THE BAR 

Sunset and evening star, And one clear call for me! 

And may there be no moaning of the bar, When I put out to sea,

But such a tide as moving seems asleep, Too full for sound and foam,

When that which drew from out the boundless deep 

Turns again home. 

Twilight and evening bell, And after that the dark! 

And may there be no sadness of farewell, When I embark;

For tho' from out our bourne of Time and Place, The flood may bare me far, I hope to see my Pilot face to face 

When I have crossed the bar. 

· ALFRED TENNYSON.

LITTLE BOY BLUE 

The little toy dog is covered with dust, But sturdy and stanch he stands; 

And the little toy soldier is red with rust, And his musket moulds in his hands. 

Time was when the little toy dog was new,

And the soldier was passing fair; 

And that was the time when our Little Boy Blue 

Kissed them and put them there. "Now, don't you go till I come,” he said "And don't you make any noise!" 

So, toddling off to his trundle-bed, He dreamt of the pretty toys; 

And, as he was dreaming, an angel song

Awakened our Little Boy Blue - 

Oh! the years are many, and the years are long, But the little toy friends are true! 

Ay, faithful to Little Boy Blue they stand, Each in the same old place 

Awaiting the touch of a little hand, The smile of a little face;

And they wonder, as waiting the long years through In the dust of that little chair, What has become of our Little Boy Blue, 

Since he kissed them and put them there. 

EUGENE FIELD. 

THE DAY IS DONE 

The day is done, and the darkness 

Falls from the wings of Night, As a feather is wafted downward 

From an eagle in his flight.

I see the lights of the village, Gleam through the rain and the mist, And a feeling of sadness comes o'er me 

That my soul cannot resist.

A feeling of sadness and longing, That is not akin to pain, And resembles sorrow only

As the mist resembles the rain. 

Come, read to me some poem, Some simple and heart-felt lay, That shall sooth this restless feeling 

And banish the thoughts of day. 

Not from the grand old masters, Not from the bards sublime, Those distant footsteps echo 

Through the corridors of Time.

For, like strains of martial music, Their mighty thoughts suggest 

Life's endless toil and endeavor; 

And tonight I long for rest. 

Read from some humbler poet, Whose songs gushed from his heart, As showers from the clouds of summer, Or tears from the eyelids start; 

Who, through long days of labor, And nights devoid of ease, Still heard in his soul the music 

Of wonderful melodies.

Such songs have power to quiet 

The restless pulse of care, And come like the benediction 

That follows after prayer.

Then read from the treasured volume 

The poem of thy choice, 

And lend to the rhyme of the poet 

The beauty of thy voice. 

And the night shall be filled with music, And the cares that infest the day, Shall fold their tents like the Arabs, And silently steal away.

LONGFELLOW. 

THE BIRTH OF THE CONSPIRACY 

May be read by one person, but preferably by two. Note carefully the psychology of Cassius in winning over Brutus to his plan. Make an effort to get into the spirit of the dialog. Make naturally and spontaneously but fully and forcefully the intellectual and emotional responses. Also, see that your tones are a suitable response to the meaning and the feeling. Then, permit the hands, feet, face, the entire body, - to respond naturally to the meaning, feeling, words, and tones of voice. Make no effort to do it as you have seen or heard it done; do it rather as you yourself think it and feel it after getting yourself thoroughly into the spirit of it. It matters not at all what movements or what tones some one else might make; it is of all importance that you make the movements and tones you feel like making after you have entered into the meaning and the meaning has entered into you. 

Cass. Will you go see the order of the course?

Bru. Not I. 

Cass. I pray you, do.

Bru. I am not gamesome: I do lack some part 

Of that quick spirit that is in Antony.

Let me not hinder, Cassius, your desires; 

I'll leave you. Cass. 

Brutus, I do observe you now of late: 

I have not from your eyes that gentleness 

And show of love as I was wont to have: 

You bear too stubborn and too strange a hand 

Over your friend that loves you. Bru. Cassius, Be not deceived: if I have veil'd my look, I turn the trouble of my countenance 

Merely upon myself. Vexèd I am, Of late, with passions of some difference, Conceptions only proper to myself, Which give some soil, perhaps, to my behaviors; 

But let not therefore my good friends be grieved,

Among which number, Cassius, be you one, — Nor construe any further my neglect, Than that poor Brutus, with himself at war,

Forgets the shows of love to other men. 

Cass. Then, Brutus, I have much mistook your passion;

By means whereof this breast of mine hath buried 

Thoughts of great value, worthy cogitations. 

Tell me, good Brutus, can you see your face? 

Bru. No, Cassius; for the eye sees not itself

But by reflection from some other thing. 

Cass. 'Tis just: 

I have heard, And it is very much lamented, Brutus, That you have no such mirror as will turn 

Your hidden worthiness into your eye, That you might see your shadow.

Where many of the best respect in Rome, Except immortal Caesar, - speaking of Brutus, And groaning underneath this age's yoke, Have wish'd that noble Brutus had his eyes. 

Bru. Into what dangers would you lead me, Cassius,

That you would have me seek into myself 

For that which is not in me? 

Cass. Therefore, good Brutus, be prepared to hear: 

And, since you know you cannot see yourself

So well as by reflection, I, your glass, Will modestly discover to yourself That of yourself which you yet know not of. 

And be not jealous of me, gentle Brutus: 

Were I a common laugher, or did use 

To stab with ordinary oaths my love 

To every new protester; if you know

That I do fawn on men, and hug them hard, And after scandal them; or if you know 

That I profess myself in banqueting 

To all the rout, then hold me dangerous. 

Bru. What means this shouting? I do fear the people 

Choose Caesar for their king. Cass. 

Ay, do you fear it?

Then must I think you would not have it so, Bru. I would not, Cassius; yet I love him well. 

But wherefore do you hold me here so long? 

What is it that you would impart to me?

If it be aught toward the general good, Set honor in one eye, and death i' the other, And I will look on death indifferently; 

For, let the gods so speed me as I love 

The name of honor more than I fear death. 

Cass. I know that virtue to be in you, Brutus,

As well as I do know your outward favor. 

Well, honor is the subject of my story. 

I cannot tell what you and other men

Think of this life; but, for my single self, I had as lief not be as live to be 

In awe of such a thing as I myself. 

I was born free as Caesar; so were you:

We both have fed as well; and we can both

Endure the winter's cold as well as he: 

For once, upon a raw and gusty day,

The troubled Tiber chafing with her shores, Caesar said to me, Darest thou, Cassius, now 

Leap in with me into this angry flood, And swim to yonder point? Upon the word, Accoutred as I was, I plunged in, And bade him follow; so indeed he did.

The torrent roar'd; and we did buffet it 

With lusty sinews, throwing it aside 

And stemming it with hearts of controversy;

But, ere we could arrive at the point proposed, Caesar cried, Help me, Cassius, or I sink! 

I, as Æneas, our great ancestor,

Did from the flames of Troy upon his shoulder The old Anchises bear, so from the waves of Tiber 

Did I the tired Caesar: and this man 

Is now become a god; and Cassius is

A wretched creature, and must bend his body, If Caesar carelessly but nod on him.

He had a fever when he was in Spain; 

And, when the fit was on him, I did mark 

How he did shake: 'tis true, this god did shake: 

His coward lips did from their color fly; 

And that same eye, whose bend doth awe the world, Did lose his lustre. I did hear him groan :

Ay, and that tongue of his, that bade the Romans Mark him, and write his speeches in their books, Alas, it cried, Give me some drink, Titinius, As a sick girl. - Ye gods, it doth amaze me,

A man of such a feeble temper should 

So get the start of the majestic world, And bear the palm alone. 

Bru. Another general shout! 

I do believe that these applauses are

For some new honors that are heap'd on Caesar.

Cass. Why, man, he doth bestride the narrow world

Like a Colossus; and we petty men 

Walk under his huge legs, and peep about To find ourselves dishonorable graves. 

Men at some time are masters of their fates: 

The fault, dear Brutus, is not in our stars,

But in ourselves, that we are underlings. 

Brutus, and Caesar: what should be in that Caesar? 

Why should that name be sounded more than yours? 

Write them together, yours is as fair a name;

Sound them, it doth become the mouth as well; 

Weigh them, it is as heavy; conjure with 'em, Brutus will start a spirit as soon as Caesar. 

Now, in the name of all the gods at once, Upon what meat doth this our Caesar feed, That he is grown so great? Age, thou art shamed! 

Rome, thou hast lost the breed of noble bloods!

When went there by an age, since the great flood, But it was famed with more than one man?

When could they say, till now, that talk'd of Rome, That her wide walls encompass'd but one man?

Now is it Rome indeed, and room enough, When there is in it but one only man. 

O, you and I have heard our fathers say,

There was a Brutus once that would have brook'd 

Th' eternal Devil to keep his state in Rome 

As easily as a king. 

Bru. That you do love me, I am nothing jealous; 

What you would work me to, I have some aim:

How I have thought of this, and of these times, I shall recount hereafter; for the present, I would not, so with love I might entreat you, Be any further moved. What you have said, I will consider; what you have to say, I will with patience hear; and find time

Both meet to hear and answer such high things.

Till then, my noble friend, chew upon this:

Brutus had rather be a villager Than to repute himself a son of Rome Under these hard conditions as this time 

Is like to lay upon us.

Cass. I am glad That my weak words have struck but thus much show 

Of fire from Brutus. 

SHAKESPEARE'S Julius Caesar. 

THE QUARREL AND RECONCILIATION 

This dialog may be read either by one person, or by two. 

In either case, the reader or readers will first come into possession of all the information the play affords on these two characters. 

Let each speech be made as the natural and spontaneous response to the speech it is answering. 

Cass. That you have wrong'd me doth appear in this: 

You have condemn'd and noted Lucius Pella 

For taking bribes here of the Sardians,

Whereas my letters, praying on his side Because I knew the man, were slighted off. 

Bru. You wrong'd yourself to write in such a case. 

Cass. In such a time as this it is not meet 

That every nice offence should bear his comment. 

Bru. And let me tell you, Cassius, you yourself 

Are much condemn'd to have an itching palm; 

To sell and mart your offices for gold 

To undeservers. Cass. 

I an itching palm!

You know that you are Brutus that speaks this, Or, by the gods, this speech were else your last. 

Bru. The name of Cassius honors this corruption, And chastisement doth therefore hide his head. 

Cass. Chastisement!

Bru. Remember March, the Ides of March remem ber: 

Did not great Julius bleed for justice' sake? 

What villain touched his body, that did stab, And not for justice? What! shall one of us, That struck the foremost man of all the world

But for supporting robbers, shall we now Contaminate our fingers with base bribes, And sell the mighty space of our large honors 

For so much trash as may be grasped thus? 

I had rather be a dog, and bay the moon, Than such a Roman. Cass. 

Brutus, bay not me, I'll not endure it: you forget yourself, To hedge me in; I am a soldier, ay,

Older in practice, abler than yourself 

To make conditions. Bru. 

Cass. I am. Bru. 

Go to; you are not, Cassius. 

I say you are not. 

Cass. Urge me no more, I shall forget myself; 

Have mind upon your health, tempt me no further. 

Bru. Away, slight man! 

Cass. Is't possible? Bru. 

Hear me, for I will speak. 

Must I give way and room to your rash choler? 

Shall I be frightened when a madman stares? 

Cass. O ye gods, ye gods! must I endure all this? 

Bru. All this! ay, more: fret till your proud heart break; 

Go show your slaves how choleric you are, And make your bondmen tremble. Must I budge? 

Must I observe you? must I stand and crouch 

Under your testy humor? By the gods, You shall digest the venom of your spleen,

Though it do split you; for from this day forth

I'll use you for my mirth, yea, for my laughter, When you are waspish. Cass. 

Is it come to this? 

Bru. You say you are a better soldier: 

Let it appear so; make your vaunting true, And it shall please me well: for mine own part, I shall be glad to learn of abler men. 

Cass. You wrong me every way; you wrong me, Brutus ;

I said, an elder soldier, not a better: 

Did I say better? Bru. Cass. 

If you did, I care not.

When Caesar lived he durst not thus have moved me. 

Bru. Peace, peace! you durst not so have tempted him. 

Cass. I durst not! Bru.

No. 

Cass. What, durst not tempt him? Bru. 

For your life you durst not. 

Cass. Do not presume too much upon my love; 

I may do that I shall be sorry for. 

Bru. You have done that you should be sorry for. 

There is no terror, Cassius, in your threats; 

For I am arm'd so strong in honesty, That they pass by me as the idle wind, Which I respect not. I did send to you 

For certain sums of gold, which you denied me; —

For I can raise no money by vile means; 

By Heaven, I had rather coin my heart, And drop my blood for drachmas, than to wring 

From the hard hands of peasants their vile trash 

By any indirection: - I did send 

To you for gold to pay my legions, Which you denied me. Was that done like Cassius? 

Should I have answered Caius Cassius so?

When Marcus Brutus grows so covetous, To lock such rascal counters from his friends, Be ready, gods, with all your thunderbolts, Dash him to pieces! Cass. Bru. 

You did. 

I denied you not.

Cass. I did not; he was but a fool that brought 

My answer back. Brutus hath rived my heart:

A friend should bear his friend's infirmities, But Brutus makes mine greater than they are. 

I do not till you practice them on me. 

You love me not. Bru. Cass. Bru. 

I do not like your faults. 

Cass. A friendly eye could never see such faults.

Bru. A flatterer's would not, though they did appear 

As huge as high Olympus. 

Cass. Come, Antony, and young Octavius, come, Revenge yourselves alone on Cassius,

For Cassius is aweary of the world; 

Hated by one he loves; braved by his brother; 

Check'd like a bondman; all his faults observed,

Set in a note-book, learn'd, and conn'd by rote, To cast into my teeth. O, I could weep 

My spirit from mine eyes! - There is my dagger,

And here my naked breast; within, a heart 

Dearer than Plutus' mine, richer than gold : 

If that thou be'st a Roman, take it forth; 

I, that denied the gold, will give my heart: 

Strike, as thou didst at Caesar; for I know, When thou didst hate him worst, thou lovedst him better 

Than ever thou lovedst Cassius. 

Sheathe your dagger: Bru. 

Be angry when you will, it shall have scope; 

Do what you will, dishonor shall be humor. 

O Cassius, you are yoked with a lamb,

That carries anger as the flint bears fire; 

Who, much enforced, shows a hasty spark, And straight is cold again. Cass. 

Hath Cassius lived 

To be but mirth and laughter to his Brutus, When grief, and blood ill-temper'd, vexeth him? ·

Bru. When I spoke that, I was ill-temper'd too.
