3. Take an hour's walk, preferably in woods or grove near a stream, and run over in your mind the thought and language of your entire speech. 

4. Play one or two games - but do not allow yourself to become fatigued — of tennis, volley ball, or horse shoes; or if the speaker in question is a very old and decrepit man, no longer feeling equal to the vigorous exercise of these sports, he may substitute a round of golf or help the caddy hunt the ball for an hour.

5. Take a good bath. 

6. Have a good evening meal; enjoy it, but do not overeat. Think and talk of things as far removed from your speech as possible. 

7. Stretch yourself out on your back on a hard floor for half an hour. Run your outline through your mind, making sure that you have all the topics in proper order. 

8. Run over the particular vocal exercises that are most helpful to you, giving special attention to resonance. 

9. Practice deep-breathing exercises. 

10. Standing before your mirror, practice the postures and movements you need most to improve. In doing this, think of each not as an end in itself but as an aid in the expression of its corresponding mental state. 

II. Impress yourself strongly with the first and the last topic of your outline. 

12. Make sure that you have your introductory remarks well in mind and are able to make them in a pleasing manner and so that if possible at least some slight connection may be apparent between them and the speech you are about to make. Also, make sure that you have your closing remarks well in mind and are able to make them in a forceful, pleasing manner and so that it is impossible not to see the connection between them and the address. 

13. Dress so that you will be comfortable, and as nearly as possible satisfied with your appearance, — at least so that you need not give this matter a moment's attention until your address has been ended. 

14. Go to the platform full of respect and consideration for your audience; but full also of confidence in the value of your subject, in your mastery of it, and in your ability to discuss it.

Postscripts 

301 

EXERCISES 

I. How does this differ from the preparation of a speech? 

2. Do you think that any of this immediate preparation is necessary? 

3. Which of the fourteen points would you omit, and what would you put in place of them? Why?

VI. THE PSYCHOLOGY OF A RELIGIOUS SERVICE 

The title of this paragraph is entirely too presumptuous. The psychology of a religious service has never yet been set down. For obvious reasons I am not attempting to set it down, but it very much needs to be done. I am hoping that all that my paragraph contains will have at least an indirect bearing on the subject announced.

Whatever may be our religious belief or unbelief, we are obliged to admit that religion has always been and is yet the most fertile field of oratory. It is not surprising that so many young men ambitious to become good speakers are attracted to the ministry. It affords rare opportunities in the way of speech. 

There can be but one purpose of any kind of a religious service and that is to put a group of people in a religious state of mind. Psychology, all psychology, is directly or indirectly concerned with mental states, and I think it can be safely said that the purpose of all speaking is to cause mental states to arise in the minds of one or more persons.

A religious service must evoke not just any state of mind but a particular one. I cannot define it. If we were to select one word that would come nearer than any other to expressing it, that one word would probably be reverence. With the understanding that we may enlarge our conception to include anything that reverence omits, let us take reverence as the state of

mind which it is the purpose of every religious service to arouse. Then from the beginning to the end of the service this must be kept in mind as the one objective or purpose of the meeting. Everything that is said or done must be with reference to this one objective. We cannot go quite so far as to say that the service shall arouse no other state of mind, but we are perfectly safe in saying that it shall not arouse any that are antagonistic to this one. The times that this is forgotten are legion. It must not be forgotten. It needs to be kept constantly in mind by all who are in any way responsible for such a service or any part of it. It could be easily objected that this will make a religious service too serious; but it is intended to be serious, and is. No one should be and no one can safely be in this state of mind all the time or even most of his time. There must be time for work, time for play, entertainment, shows, levity, vaudeville, if you will, but not in a religious service.

THE ROOM 

The room in which a religious service is held should be a church, planned and built for the purpose. In every feature of its construction it should be suited to the mental state to be aroused in its services. The walls should be plain but pleasing. They may even be beautiful if their beauty is not allowed to attract to itself attention that belongs to the service. If they have on them pictures, they must of course be religious pictures, and such as will contribute toward the one end in view, instead of taking attention to themselves. The same principle would hold for church pageants, for statuary and all other forms of decoration. The light must always be soft, never bright or glaring. Religion's message is seldom a truth that is as clear as the noonday It is more likely to be a faith and a hope, neither sun.

Postscripts 

303 certain in the sense that mathematics and science are certain, but both shrouded in mystery. The truths of religion are not like the clearness of noon; they are more like the evening twilight. That is why we have shaded candles and stained-glass windows in places of worship. 

I am aware that it may be objected: "If the preacher is great enough, the service may be held anywhere. In any old, hastily improvised shack with unplaned board walls and a sawdust floor, a Billy Sunday can preach powerful sermons." Yes. On more than one occasion 

I have seen and heard him do it. Two things need to be remembered, the first of which is certain and the second highly probable, - we do not have many Billy Sundays, and the one we do have would probably preach even more powerful sermons in a church, if it were possible to find one that would seat all the people who come. 

THE CONGREGATION 

The congregation is another factor to be taken into account. It must be made up of men and women as nearly as possible a unit in their wants and their wishes. 

They must be assembled not to quarrel and not to criticize, but to receive a spiritual blessing. They must think and believe that man's nature and his life are partly spiritual, and that the spiritual element in man's life needs nourishment as much as does the physical or the intellectual. They must have come together liking one another and in sympathy with their preacher. Of course, we have been informed that congregations assembled to scoff will remain to pray, if only the preacher is great enough. Here again we must remember, not all preachers are that great. Just now we are thinking of the contribution a congregation can make to a religious service, rather than of what the preacher may

be able to do in spite of the congregation. The congregation does have a part, and it is a large one. 

THE MUSIC 

Another factor is the music. It has already been pointed out that religion is akin to all art; but particularly is it closely related to music. A lawyer never thinks of having music in connection with his speech to judge or jury, nor does the public speaker when he addresses an audience. It is not common in political meetings, and would be very unusual in the annual convention of the world's pessimists. I think I have not heard that music was ever sung or played in a congregation of atheists; but it would be hard for any of us to conceive of a religious service without music. We do have music in other than church assemblies. We sing when the boys go to war and we sing when they come back; but these songs are different from the ones we sing in memory of the boys that do not come back. 

That is to say, that church music is different from other music, and it is. Now, what is the character of church music? What music is permissible in a religious service? The answer is, any music that contributes to the state of mind the service is seeking to bring about. 

All other music is out of place. Or we may say, the only music that helps to bring about this mental state and therefore the only music allowable in a religious service, is sacred music. I am aware that exception might be taken. There are those who contend that there should be a place in church for other music, that sacred music alone would make the services too somber; but religion is always serious. Men cannot live long and at their best without entertainment. Life must provide somewhere for amusement; but a religious service is not the place for it. Religion need not be gloomy, but it is never funny. So, to my thinking, the only music

Postscripts 

305 permissible in a religious service is music that is conducive to a religious state of mind, and that is sacred music. This must not be taken to restrict church music to a few meritless songs expressing and inviting distress; on the contrary it includes a generous share of classical music. Much of the world's best music has a religious setting. Any combination of tones consonant with a religious state of mind is in place in a religious gathering; and that combination will always be sacred music. There is much other good music, of course, but it is not good for church. Then there is other music critics do not call good which many of us like to hear, but we do not want it in church. Personally I love Gershwin's Rhapsody in Blue, but I should not care to hear it as a part of a religious service. 

Not only every song but every singer is for the time being an essential part of the service. Every person singing in the choir has resting upon him or her the same responsibility that rests upon the pastor. The smiles exchanged by the singers and certain members of the congregation are understood by too few of the whole assembly to contribute anything worthwhile toward the state of mind sought. Few things in life are more charming than a maiden laughing, but she must not laugh in the choir. One happy but thoughtless girl can giggle more spirituality out of a congregation in a minute than the pastor can preach into them in an hour. Then if there is ever a time when the choir leader or director may attract attention to himself, that time is not when he is leading the music in a religious service. Church music, all of it, is not an introduction to the religious service, it is not something added after the service is ended, it is an essential part of that service. The preacher himself often fails to understand this. It is not at all uncommon for him, after the organ prelude, and it may be after a song or two, to rise and say: "We

shall begin our worship by reading so and so"; but the worship had already begun. If the organist were not playing the worship, he should not have allowed her to play; and if the choir had not been singing the worship, what were they doing? The worship, if it has not begun already, certainly begins when the fingers of the organist first touch the keys, and it continues without a break to the end of the benediction, and sometimes much longer.

THE SERMON 

The chief part of any religious service is the sermon. 

It is supposed to accomplish the end in view, while everything else—the building, the congregation, and the music - are at most only contributing factors. 

There are only two requirements that every sermon must meet. The first of these is that it must be a sermon and not something else. Since I have written this first requirement, it sounds a little impertinent, but it is not so intended. It is intended, it would probably be presumptuous to say to correct an error, - to call attention to a very common defect. Most sermons are not sermons, but are addresses, arguments, lectures. Whether a speech in a religious service is a sermon or not depends largely on its theme. A speech on politics, the tariff, the League of Nations, economics, Hamlet, education, science, philosophy, evolution, literature, music, however excellent, is not a sermon. I am not meaning to imply that preachers shall not be interested in these subjects and should not speak on them. On the contrary, I am convinced that few classes of people have a stronger interest in such subjects than preachers have or are better able to contribute to the rest of us something worthwhile on them. I mean only that while they are doing this they must not fool themselves, — they never fool their congregations, — into thinking they 

---

Postscripts 

307 are preaching sermons. A sermon is the development of a religious theme toward the end of putting a group of people in a religious state of mind. The subjects named above are all very important. They need to be discussed. Only a few people are better prepared than preachers to discuss them. But they are not religious subjects. Sermons cannot be preached on them. When a preacher hears an address on some such subject, naturally enough he is tempted to make one himself. If it is a good address, he is tempted to make another good one, and if it is not so good, he is inclined just as strongly to try to correct it. This is all very well, highly commendable, if he will make his address in almost any club or other organization that he may belong to and call it an address; but he must not take it to his pulpit and call it a sermon. On some such subject as God, Faith, Hope, Sin, Mercy, Redemption, Death, Life, Immortality, Worship, Prayer, and many others, sermons may be preached. These are the subjects that rightfully claim the preacher's attention. 

He makes a mistake when he leaves them for others he supposes the rest of us prefer to have discussed. Not being a minister, I am of course speaking from the outside; but after consulting many students and many older persons I have reached the conclusion that a large majority of the people who attend religious services go in the hope of hearing a sermon, and are disappointed when the preacher substitutes something else. 

The second requirement of a sermon is that it shall not be long. A long time ago, when sermons were scarce, when there were few books and periodicals, we may have been able to sit through a two-hour sermon and like it. If so, that time has gone, and a preacher who thinks he can bring it back is laboring under a delusion. Certainly there are exceptions. There are yet many occasions when a preacher would displease

his congregation by speaking less than an hour, and a few when they will listen to him two hours; but on all ordinary occasions, twenty, twenty-five, or at most thirty minutes will please more people and will be more likely to send them back for another sermon. 

THE PREACHER 

The preacher I have left until the last, not at all because I regard him as the least factor in a religious service. It would be nearer the truth to class him as the greatest of the five. Little, however, has been left to be said about him. In a way, all that is said of the sermon is said of him, for he preaches the sermon. Also in a way, all that can be said of the whole religious service is said of him, for he directs the entire service and is responsible for all of it. It does not need to be said that he must be a serious man. He would not only die for his convictions, but he will live by them. He must be a sincere man. He must himself be profoundly religious, and his one concern in life is to make others religious. He must have a keen and far-reaching insight into life. He must see clearly and be able to make the rest of us see that any life that leaves spiritual values out is only a part of a life. He must be a strong man able to do his best and to keep on doing his best even when we for whom he works are unappreciative and ungrateful. He must be a broad-minded man tolerant of views different from his own, but he must be also a courageous man, unwilling to concede as unimportant what he knows to be vital. He must have a logical mind that never fails to distinguish what is incidental and unimportant from what is essential and vital in religion. It will enable him to decide what particular mental state he wishes to work toward, and then to develop his theme so that all that he says will work toward that end. He must have an unfailing sense of what is fitting and appropriate. After he has

Postscripts 

309 preached a sermon that leaves us feeling as he wants us to feel he must not make an announcement or tell a story that makes us feel some other way. He must be such a good reader that we are all sorry when he comes to the end of his Scripture lesson, and such a good speaker that we get every word that he says and are pleased by the way he says it. He must love humanity, he must live and labor to help humanity. 

With due apology for the manifest disparity between the generous promise of the title of this paragraph and the meager performance of the paragraph itself, I bring it to a close, well aware that I have not written the psychology of a religious service, but hoping that the outline submitted may be suggestive to the one who is to write it.

EXERCISES 

1. Pick out the points in this postscript that you are in agreement with. Give your reasons. 

2. Enumerate the points mentioned that you would take exception to. Discuss them, giving reasons for your attitude. 

VII. SOME PRINCIPLES OF A SALES SPEECH 

A large proportion of all speeches, both public and private, are for the purpose of making sales. Some have so extended the meaning of the word sell as to make it include even the work of the teacher as she stands before her classes. They say she is selling her instruction. Personally I have not been altogether pleased with this use of the word. It has always seemed to me like degrading the noble work of teaching and learning. However, we have all had opportunities to learn that it is infinitely easier to be displeased with some particular usage than to change it. After all, this application of the word sell is not so very bad. There is never a selling without a buying. The words buy and

sell always have reference to receiving something in exchange for something parted with. This always applies in cases of instruction, and it is by no means a degrading conception of instruction. On the contrary it meets the test of up-to-the-minute views of education. 

Instruction is not to be had for nothing. It cannot be thrust upon the learner. He must part with something in order to get it. He must buy it. So, maybe I might as well make up my mind to get along with this use of the word sell.

And it is just as applicable to many other fields. The doctor must sell his service or have no patients. The statesman must sell to us his conception of government or have no following. The Constitution of the United 

States had to be sold to us. So did negro slavery. So did the emancipation of the slaves. From time to time the two great political parties go out into the open market and try to sell to us their respective theories of government. Sometimes one is successful and sometimes the other, depending partly upon the changing views of the buyer, and largely upon their respective skills in applying the principles of salesmanship set forth a little later. 

Prohibition had to be sold to the American Republic.

Just now a very considerable number of our people, thinking the price was too high, are anxious to "trade back." Another considerable number, equally honest, insist that it was the best trade our people ever made. 

The Great War was sold to the world at a cost so tremendous that it staggers our intelligence; and the world will have another war, greater and more terrible, if world peace, a warless world, is not sold to most of the men and women in all the professions and all the industries, and particularly to most of the children of the world. In morals, all ideals are sold and bought or they are not had. Not only do schools and teachers sell us education, but the whole problem of preachers is how to

Postscripts 

311 sell us righteousness. The real purpose of every book that comes from the press is to sell broadcast a certain idea or set of ideas. The purpose of this one is to sell to the reader a stronger emphasis on the importance of speech and its tremendous value as an element in human culture. There are three grades or classes of preparation that every salesman must make. (1) He must make himself a good speaker. Since all sales are made by speech, every person ambitious to become a salesman must first of all do everything, neglect nothing, that would help him to speak better. 

Everything he can do to himself or for himself in the way of training or education he must do. So, in this connection I strongly recommend to any prospective salesman, or to any salesman ambitious to be a better one, the mastery of the contents of this book, or of some other book on the same subject. There is hardly a page or a paragraph in the entire book the mastery of which would not abundantly repay the salesman for his time and effort. (2) He must have a good understanding of buying and selling in general. That is, he must master the principles of salesmanship. This he will best do by starting with an intelligent examination of the principles laid down in this chapter, either as they are or as they become when properly modified. (3) He must study the selling of his particular product. 

Since the products now on the market are so varied and since new ones are appearing almost every minute, it would be an endless task to treat all of them even if one were qualified to do it. This third and last grade of preparation the prospective salesman will make under the direction of the factory or sales organization for which he works. This makes us ready for our first principle. 

I. One must know the product he sells. It is hardly worthwhile to say that this short chapter I am writing

from the outside, not from the experience of a salesman; the reader will soon discover it. Yet, one on the outside has abundant opportunity to come upon information whose value cannot be estimated. Any one interested in the motivation of mental states and caring enough about salesmanship to realize that it is in reality a great art, will observe that while an occasional salesman will drop in so masterful that we cannot withhold our admiration, the great majority are but poorly prepared for their work. They are not prepared because they have not thought about their work; that is, they have not thought about it systematically. It may be that they do not know how to think about it systematically. The purpose at this time is to make at least some suggestions in the direction of systematic thinking on making sales. Every effort of a salesman is to generate mental states in the mind of the prospective purchaser; for as a man thinks, so will he spend his money, or his labor, or his conclusions, or his convictions, or whatever we are asking him to part with. 

In trying to arrive at some of the principles of a sales speech, the first point that becomes clear to us is that this speech, which is the salesman's presentation, cannot be made without reference to the product. That is what the speech is about. The presentation is a presentation of the product. Naturally enough, our first principle is about the product. It is surprising what a meager knowledge most salesmen have of the products they are undertaking to sell us. Yet it would be just as unreasonable for one to expect a large audience to listen to him as he speaks on the League of Nations before he studies it, as to think that a buyer should become enthusiastic over his presentation of a product he knows little more than the name of. Except in cases where the buyer has already made up his mind that he wants the particular product I happen to be offering,

Postscripts 

313 making a sale is no easy matter; or as we learn in the street, it is a man's job. Here as everywhere, its relation to making a speech is apparent. It is an easy matter for a speaker to sell republican doctrine to a republican audience or democratic doctrine to a democratic audience; but to induce either a democrat or a republican to do his work and cast his vote for the opposite party is far from easy. The same is true in selling a product to a man who does not know what he wants or that he wants anything, or who thinks he knows he wants something very different from the product I am offering him. If I sell to such a man at all, I must know my product. I must know it intimately and in its details. I must know it in three very important views : (1) I must know it with reference to its origin, the manner of its construction, and the raw material that has entered into it; (2) I must know it with reference to similar products that may be in competition with it; and (3) I must know it in its relation to the ends it will serve, and be able to show that it will serve these ends better than any other product, or at least as well as any other. 

II. One must have faith in the product he sells. Why? 

There are two reasons. First, one can make more sales, and can make them more easily. It is to the credit of humanity at large that any of us can make out a much stronger case for a thing we believe in than for one we do not believe in. The second reason is, that if one does not have faith in the product he sells, he is not a salesman at all but something very different. The salesman, the real salesman, is one of the benefactors of the human race. He shows us our needs, which without him might never have occurred to us. Then he brings to us a product that will supply those needs. What else is life but discovering our needs and the means of satisfying them? So the salesman brings us life. 

Well, suppose the product I sell is not the best, what

must I do? One or the other of two things. I must make up for its deficiency by means of a lower price, or 

I must drop it and sell the best. Of course, I am justified in continuing to sell my product so long as I honestly believe it is as good as any other. Suppose

I know my product is good. I can induce my prospect to pay me for it, and I need the money, but I know he does not need the product. Then the transaction is not selling, but something else. The story is told of a vacuumcleaner salesman who boasted that he sold two cleaners to a woman who washed for her support and lived in a one-room house with no rug or carpet on the floor. He was not a salesman. The most charitable view is that he manufactured his story. In that case he was only a liar; otherwise he was a thief. To be a good salesman, one must have faith in the product he sells. That faith will remove mountains of obstacles. 

III. One must know the buyer to whom he sells. The intimate personal acquaintance is not necessary. Many think it more of a hindrance than a help. It is certainly not necessary or desirable that the salesman should have a close friendship for the buyer. Selling to strangers is less likely to result in misunderstanding than selling to friends and relatives. But another kind of knowledge of the buyer is indispensable. The salesman should know his work and his needs. The entire presentation, while it is of the product, is with reference to the needs or uses of the buyer. If he does not have those needs, our product is not for him. Then we will not waste his time or ours. In any case, we must know the buyer's needs before attempting to present our product, and the whole presentation must be with reference to his needs.

IV. One must keep faith with the buyer to whom he sells. 

A complete grasp of this principle will enable us to keep buying and selling on a high plane of decency and

Postscripts 

315 respectability. Selling in its reality is not swindling the purchaser; it is inducing him by every possible legitimate means to part with money in exchange for an article the salesman honestly believes will be worth more to him than the money. This is keeping faith with the buyer. It is being square with him. It will lose some individual sales, but it will make more sales than it loses. In the first place, it is the only kind of selling there is. If it is of a different kind, it is not selling at all, but something else. Then, even from the standpoint of mere policy it pays; it increases one's sales. Every day, this kind of selling is increasing in all commodities and in all parts of the country. It is not uncommon to have a salesman tell you, after hearing from you a word of explanation, "Well, in that case you do not need it." This is only plain honesty, but it is keeping faith with the buyer. It makes a buyer want to hear the salesman instead of trying to avoid him.

This is as it should be. The buyer should be even more anxious to find a salesman than the salesman a prospect. 

If I am in need of an article, who can talk more interestingly to me than the seller of that article, particularly if I believe that he knows all about it and will be square with me? Making a trade must never be regarded as unloading a worthless article upon a customer in exchange for an article of value. In every trade, each of the two parties should part with an article in exchange for another that is worth more to him. If these conditions do not obtain with both parties, then no trade should be made; then if either party insists upon making it, he is not trading, but doing something else. There are plenty of trades of this kind to keep all of us busy a good part of our time. The man who helps us find them is a public benefactor. This is not moralizing; it is merchandizing. It is not preaching; it is salesmanship.

V. One must know the best presentation of the product he sells. The universal testimony of sales organizations is that a large majority of all their salesmen know less about the product they sell than many of their prospects. Under such a condition, a good presentation is impossible, for the presentation is always of the product. 

No one can present intelligently a product he does not know. So, in working out an intelligent presentation, the first step is the mastery of the product. 

Then the presentation itself must be studied. It must be thought out as systematically as if it were a sermon, or an address on education. The sales presentation is education. It should be organized. An outline should be made of it. Serious thought must be given as to the different topics to be discussed. Then just as much thought as to the order of these topics is necessary. One must think how to begin; and even more about how to close. The presentation should be studied and studied again. Then it should be written out in full. Then after more study it should be rewritten. It would not be bad to commit it to memory. 

When the salesman comes to use it, he will not speak it from memory; he will be so familiar with it that he will give it naturally from his understanding of it. 

From beginning to end it must be a progressive development in the direction of the particular state of mind the salesman wishes the buyer to be in. It must contain nothing that does not point toward this mental state.

The salesman must be so familiar with his presentation that a question from the prospect will not disturb him. He should welcome questions; but like the good teacher or the experienced speaker, he will so manage the questions that he himself, not the questioner, will remain in charge of the presentation. From start to finish the salesman is working for an affirmative state of

Postscripts 

317 mind, and will therefore have in his presentation as few negative things as possible.

VI. One must have faith in the presentation that is to sell his product. He must believe everything he says, if he expects his listener to believe it. He must have confidence in his presentation, if he wishes the buyer to have confidence in it. He must know the facts and be confident that they are facts, but he must not allow his confidence to induce him to overstate these facts. 

Overstatement of facts leaves the impression that the speaker does not have faith in his presentation. Then the buyer will not have faith in it. It is better to understate the facts. Not so as to imply that you are in doubt about them; but rather so that in case of an emergency you will have something in reserve. 

In glancing backward over these six principles I note that they are half knowledge and half faith. That is not bad. Life itself is about like that. Other principles might be stated, but I shall rest with these. If Just a closing word. When one is displeased, he walks out without buying. The more he is pleased the more likely he is to buy. Both the product and the presentation should be pleasing to the buyer. Particularly should the salesman himself or herself be pleasing. a salesman is good-looking, that helps. If he is not, there are other things that go a long way in making up for the defect. One of these is a pleasing voice. All the work a salesman does in improving his voice is excellent preparation for larger orders. Another thing that helps is good manners, courtesy, sincere consideration for the person or persons to whom one is speaking. I have never met a man or woman with a good voice and good manners, who was not a pleasing personality. These are the two big things. They are indispensable. There may be some little things that help, but they are not indispensable. I think I have

read somewhere that to be a good salesman one must have a certain kind of chin and a certain kind of mouth. 

That may be true. If one happens to have just that kind of chin and mouth, let him be grateful; if he does not, let him keep the chin and the mouth that he has and lose no opportunity to improve his voice and his manners.

Here with permission from his publishers, The Macmillan Company, I am setting down four points from pages 463 and 464 of Ewer's excellent book on Applied 

Psychology: 

"Four points of special importance are the following: (1) In approaching a customer, assume, but do not try to force, his interest. If calling on him, do not acknowledge even as a polite conventionality that he may have more weighty business in hand, for he may accept the suggestion; and in any case it is likely to strengthen an unfavorable attitude. The salesman must steer his course between this error and the opposite one of excessive display of zeal, which invites distrust. (2) Concentrate the customer's attention. Do not distract it by continual talk, or display of other articles. 

Stick to one thing until there is reason for change. If the choice lies between several, try to keep that one in the foreground which seems to be nearest his needs or his desire. It is a psychological truth that so long as his attention is flickering from one point to another he simply cannot reach a decision. (3) Spend more time in presenting positive merits than in answering objections. The negative effect of an objection often remains after it has been rationally met; hence it is better to keep attention upon desirable features as much as possible. This tends automatically to suppress the objections. (4) Watch for the "psychological moment," and then move the point. When the resistance breaks down, as may be indicated by a word of

Postscripts 

319 

acquiescence, momentary silence, or a mere change of facial expression, clinch the sale by speaking or acting as though it were actually being made, pre- senting the contract for signature, proposing to send the goods at once, or using any similarly suggestive device." 

I sincerely hope that this chapter may be at least suggestive to the salesman and of some practical help to young people ambitious to become salesmen. I hardly see how it could fail to do this. Yet, this is not the primary purpose of the chapter. This is a book on speaking. The fundamental aim of this chapter has been to give speakers and students of speech, particularly teachers, preachers, lawyers, and lecturers, a slightly different view of the art they have chosen to practice. I have hoped that they might see, through this discussion, that their product is the thought, the theory, the idea, or the ideal they are seeking to have their classes, their congregations, their juries, their audiences, come into the possession of. These classes, congregations, juries, and audiences are the buyers. 

They never get something for nothing. They must always part with something in exchange for the product offered. Whether they do it or not will depend, of course, upon the relative value, or what they think is the relative value, of the product offered, and the skill with which it is presented. The presentation is the lesson, the sermon, the plea, or the lecture. The six principles set forth above, and so far as I know all other principles of salesmanship, together with all that has been said and all that can be said about the qualities of salesmen, are directly and clearly applicable to speaking. As a rule, the more views one has of his subject, the better he understands it. If salesmanship gives us even a slightly different view of speaking, let us master at least some of its principles.

EXERCISES 

I. What is the reason for including this chapter in the book? 

2. What is the wider application of the word sell? What is your own attitude toward this usage? 

3. What are the three classes of preparation every salesman should make? Show how they apply to some commodity. 

4. State and discuss each of the six principles of a sales speech. 

Are there other principles? 

5. What personal characteristics of a salesman can you name? 

6. State each of Ewer's four points. 

7. Show definitely how this whole discussion applies to teachers, preachers, lawyers, and other speakers. Make this very clear. 

8. After special preparation, make a five- or ten-minute speech on each of the seven postscripts. Do not confine yourself exclusively to the text. Draw on all other available resources. 

Keep your mind open. Be independent in your judgment.

EXERCISES 

I. Present in a three-minute speech your analysis of Grady's oration on p. 238. 

2. Of Lincoln's Speech on p. 243. 

3. Of Roosevelt's Speech on p. 247. 

4. Of Bryan's Speech on p. 251. 

5. Of Wendell Phillips' Speech on p. 254. 

6. Of all these orations which is the greatest? Why? 

7. Of all the poems in this book which is your preference? 

8. Make a list of the six best.
